The honest read is that this is a feature for a specific user archetype — heavy daily-note keepers — and its value should be judged on that population, not on an average vault.

**Needs first.** N1 with real daily-note usage, P1, P2.


### 10. P6. Session co-visit graph — KEEP, with a second mandatory control that I think decides it

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** 'You have opened these two notes in the same sitting eleven times and never linked them.' One click writes the link.

**How.** Strongest independence argument in the list and I agree with it. One correction and one addition. (a) THE FOLDER CONTROL IS NOT SUFFICIENT — add a LINK-ADJACENCY control, which I expect to be the one that bites. You open A then B mostly because A links to B and you clicked it, so the co-visit graph will substantially relearn the existing wikilink graph, which is exactly the shipped channel. Worse, once the panel is open, the user's clicks come FROM the panel, so the panel's own suggestions become the co-visit channel's input and the channel confirms itself. Both must be handled in the data, not the score: tag each open event with its provenance (panel click / wikilink click / quick-switcher / graph view / other) and EXCLUDE panel-originated opens from the co-visit counts entirely. That is a small logging decision that is impossible to retrofit, so it has to be right on day one. (b) The decay constant deserves a number: 0.98 per month is a half-life of ~34 months, which is slow. State it and sweep it.

**Why it is not redundant.** Pure behaviour, zero content, zero link structure — correct, PROVIDED the two exclusions above are enforced. Without the panel-provenance exclusion it is not independent, it is a feedback loop.

**Kill experiment.** Next-note-opened leave-one-out is right, but restrict it to transitions where the next note was NOT linked from the current note and NOT opened from the panel; otherwise the metric measures 'does the user click links', which the ranker already knows. Both controls (folder co-occurrence, link adjacency) must be beaten. Secondary chronological link-recall as stated.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The three original risks stand. Add a fourth that I think is the real one: the measurement takes months of the owner's own usage to accumulate, during which nothing can be shipped or refuted, so this is the highest-latency item in the list even though its cost is only moderate. Mitigate by shipping the LOGGER (opt-in, with the provenance tags and a visible clear-history button) as soon as the privacy surface is agreed, and treating the channel as a decision to be made a quarter later on real data.

**Needs first.** None strictly — it builds its own timestamps. Ship the logger early precisely because it is the only item whose cold start cannot be shortened.


### 11. P7. Kleinberg bursts over the vault timeline — KEEP, one real bug in the base rate

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** A timeline of your own obsessions. 'Feb 2026 — Bayesian inference, 11 notes in 9 days.' Cards can carry an episode receipt.

**How.** Right algorithm, right cost, and the shuffled-timestamp negative control is the best-designed control in the original list — keep it verbatim. One bug: running each cluster's arrival stream against the VAULT-WIDE creation rate is wrong. A cluster holding 30% of the vault will appear to burst almost everywhere and a cluster holding 1% will never clear the threshold, so the detector would be reporting cluster size, not concentration. The null rate for cluster c must be the vault creation rate scaled by c's share of notes (equivalently, run the automaton on the per-cluster stream with base rate lambda_0 = |c| / |V| * vaultRate). This is a one-line fix that decides whether the feature works at all.

**Why it is not redundant.** Correct — not a pairwise channel, competes with nothing, adds a time axis to the existing map (src/vault-map.ts) and reuses the shipped c-TF-IDF labels.

**Kill experiment.** Judged protocol at >= 7/10 plus the shuffle control, exactly as stated. Add one cheap sanity output: the detected burst count as a function of gamma, so the owner can see whether the feature is threshold-sensitive before trusting any single run.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Timestamp resolution, as stated, and it is worse than the proposal assumes — on the lab vault every note is stamped within one of two days, so the shuffle control and the real run would be indistinguishable and the feature would correctly appear dead. This is not a reason to doubt the feature; it is another instance of N1 being the blocker.

**Needs first.** N1, P1. Reuses src/vault-map.ts clustering and labels.


### 12. P8. Replace the hand-set hub constant — KEEP THE INTENT, KILL THE MECHANISM (temporal motifs are dead)

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing visible. Fewer bad cards caused by index and MOC notes that the current degree cutoff misjudges.

**How.** The intent is right — HUB_DEGREE = 40 (src/graph-signals.ts:53) is an unjustified constant that gates both the RA evidence term and the PPR frontier expansion. The mechanism is wrong on four counts and I would not build it. (1) Delta-temporal 3-edge motif counting is not cheaply incrementally maintainable, and the plugin invalidates the whole graph on every metadataCache 'changed' event (src/main.ts:474-483) — a full motif recount per keystroke-batch is not viable, and the alternative (a persisted, incrementally-patched motif index) is a large amount of new state for a signal the proposal's own author estimates is 60% likely to be a wash. (2) It is the most timestamp-hungry item in the list, sitting on the least reliable timestamps. (3) There is a far cheaper replacement that needs NO timestamps: make the hub threshold a DEGREE PERCENTILE of the vault's own degree distribution rather than a constant. (4) If you still want a temporal role signal, use the cheap one: the ENTROPY or Gini coefficient of a node's edge-time distribution — O(deg) per node, trivially incremental, and it captures exactly the distinction the proposal cares about (60 links written in one burst = real topic; 60 links dispersed over years = index note). SUBSTITUTE MECHANISM: hub = degree above the vault's Nth percentile, optionally softened by edge-time entropy where P1 confidence permits.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a scoring channel either way; the only question is whether the ranker improves at equal or lower hubness skew.

**Kill experiment.** Keep the two-sided win condition verbatim — equal or better recall@10 AT LOWER top-1 concentration skew (the shipped comments track 2.3 vs 3.6). Run the percentile version FIRST because it needs no timestamps and can be measured today on the existing random-mask protocol as a no-regression check. NOTE the lab vault cannot really exercise this: I measured exactly ONE note above degree 40 (max degree 235, median 5), so the hub cut currently fires on a single node. This too needs N1.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The temporal-motif version is dead: intractable to maintain incrementally, worst timestamp dependency in the list, and its own author put it at 60% wash. The percentile substitute is cheap but may also be a wash — with one hub node in the lab corpus there is nothing to measure, so its value is entirely a bet on larger vaults.

**Needs first.** Percentile version: none. Entropy version: N1, P1, P2.


### 13. P9. Temporal Katz replaces rooted PageRank as nominator — DOWNGRADE, one premise is wrong about the code

*moderate · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Better candidates reaching the panel from two and three hops out.

**How.** Half the argument does not survive contact with the code. THE INCREMENTAL-UPDATE WIN IS AGAINST A COST THAT DOES NOT EXIST: there is no persisted walk structure to rebuild. pprFrom() is computed on demand, per query, from an adjacency map built lazily in one O(E) pass over resolvedLinks, and invalidateGraph() simply drops the maps (src/graph-signals.ts:79-105, src/index-store.ts:1009). A temporal-Katz accumulator maintained across edge arrivals would ADD persistent state the plugin currently does not need and does not have a place to put. Second problem: the non-decreasing-timestamp constraint degenerates under timestamp ties, and ties are the normal case (the lab vault has two distinct days across 528 notes) — with ties admitted the constraint prunes almost nothing, and with ties rejected the walk dies at hop one. So on realistic vaults it is either a no-op or a random pruner. What IS worth keeping from this proposal is its metric: candidate-set recall at matched candidate budget is the right way to evaluate anything touching nomination, and nothing else in the list measures nomination properly.

**Why it is not redundant.** A swap, so only the delta counts — correct.

**Kill experiment.** Keep candidate-set recall at matched budget as the decisive metric, and adopt it as the standard nomination metric regardless of whether this proposal proceeds. Drop the incremental-update wall-clock comparison; it is measuring against a rebuild the plugin does not perform. KILL CONDITION unchanged: no improvement in candidate-set recall at equal budget means PPR stays.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Elevated to likely-fails: with tied timestamps the causal constraint carries no information, and the plugin's actual nomination budget is small — max(8, width/2) nominations (src/index-store.ts:2538-2540), not 400 — so there is little room for a better nominator to show a difference. The P1 confidence gate (fall back to untimed PPR when edge times are weak) would, on most vaults, mean falling back always.

**Needs first.** N1, P1, P2.


### 14. P10. Recency-first frontier truncation — DEAD. The premise is factually false.

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. There is no change to make.

**How.** The proposal states the frontier is truncated 'in whatever order the adjacency map yields, which is insertion order, i.e. arbitrary'. That is not what the code does. src/graph-signals.ts:174-177 sorts the frontier by accumulated MASS descending and keeps the top 400: `const top = [...next.entries()].sort((a, b) => b[1] - a[1]).slice(0, PPR_FRONTIER_MAX)`. The cut is already principled — it keeps the walk's own evidence ordering — and replacing it with a recency ordering would DISCARD that evidence in favour of a weaker proxy. The cited most-recent-neighbour finding concerns SAMPLING from a neighbourhood too large to enumerate, which is a different operation from selecting the top of a computed mass distribution. Additionally the cap barely fires: hubs never expand at all (`if (nbrs.size > HUB_DEGREE) continue`, src/graph-signals.ts:165), which is what keeps the frontier small in the first place, and on the lab vault exactly one node exceeds HUB_DEGREE while total vault size is 525, so the 400-node frontier cap is unreachable there.

**Why it is not redundant.** Moot.

**Kill experiment.** Keep ONLY the instrumentation, as a 20-minute task with no code change beyond a counter: log how often the 400-cap fires across a realistic query set, and log the mass ratio between the 400th and 401st frontier entries. If the cap never fires, close the item. If it does fire and the mass at the cut is near-flat, THEN a tie-break rule becomes a legitimate question — and recency is one candidate among several, to be argued on fresh evidence rather than on a misreading.

**Most likely reason it fails.** DEAD as proposed: the change would replace a mass-ordered selection with a recency-ordered one, which is strictly worse motivated, to fix a problem the code does not have. The residual instrumentation is worth doing only because it is nearly free.

**Needs first.** None.


### 15. P11. Unfinished threads — KEEP. Best magic candidate in the list.

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** 'You wrote nine notes on variational inference in February and stopped. What you wrote yesterday is close to that.' It fires rarely and only when it is confident.

**How.** The independence argument — that the unit of the answer is a time-bounded SET, not a note — is the strongest reasoning in the original list and I would not change it. Two corrections. (a) DORMANCY MUST NOT BE DEFINED ON mtime. 'Contains no note edited since' is unusable on any vault where mtime was flattened by a sync or a copy, which is the state the lab vault is in and plausibly the state most users are in. Define dormancy on the LINK timeline instead — no new edges into the episode's note set since the burst ended — which reuses P1's journal, degrades to 'unknown' rather than to 'everything is dormant', and is a property of the graph rather than of the filesystem. Keep mtime as a corroborating signal only, gated on P1 confidence. (b) The saturating silence() function is right and the reasoning behind it (abandonment, not age) is right.

**Why it is not redundant.** Correct. Content similarity finds the closest old note; it cannot tell you the note belongs to a nine-note cluster built over a fortnight and abandoned. The graph cannot either, because the abandoned thread is by definition unlinked to current work.

**Kill experiment.** Judged protocol at >= 0.6 with the shuffled-timestamp negative control, exactly as stated — that control is the right one and it must genuinely collapse the suggestion set, not merely reduce it. One addition the proposal needs: 'at most one suggestion per session' makes collecting 15 fired suggestions take 15 sessions. Build an OFFLINE REPLAY mode that walks the vault's history and emits every suggestion the live gate would have fired, so the judged set can be assembled in an afternoon.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Inherits every timestamp weakness from P1 and P7, and the shuffle control will correctly kill it if bursts are not real. Second risk unchanged: on a young vault there are no dormant bursts and the feature looks broken — which is the honest form of 'compounds as the vault grows', and should be stated in the UI as 'nothing dormant yet' rather than rendered as an empty panel.

**Needs first.** N1, P1, P7. Reuses shipped whole-note vectors and cluster labels.


### 16. P12. Semantic drift / historical query vector — DEAD as a temporal proposal; half of it survives elsewhere

*expensive · speculative confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** Nothing, for a long time.

**How.** Three reasons to cut it, one of which the proposal misses entirely. (1) THE QUERY-TIME COST IS UNPRICED. The panel ranks the ACTIVE note against the corpus. Giving the active note a second historical position means running the whole funnel twice — coarse shortlist, BiMax over chunk vectors, structural boost, fusion — and merging two result sets. On a 100k-note vault that is a straight doubling of the operation the constraints cap at a few seconds, for a benefit that exists only for heavily-rewritten notes. The proposal budgets the 7.7 MB of storage and says nothing about this. (2) The cold start is unbounded and unshortenable — past text is gone, there is no reconstruction path, and the plugin cannot measure the feature at all for months, which is a direct conflict with 'anything shipped must be MEASURED first'. (3) Use (a), the split-this-note suggestion, DOES NOT NEED HISTORY: bimodal cluster structure among a note's own chunk vectors is computable today, from vectors already in the index, with no time axis whatsoever. So the valuable half is atemporal and belongs in a different area, and the temporal half is unmeasurable for months at a real query-time cost.

**Why it is not redundant.** Use (b) would genuinely be independent of the content channel — that reasoning was sound. It does not rescue the item.

**Kill experiment.** Not worth designing until the query-time cost is answered. If the owner wants the split-suggestion half, measure that separately and today: 20 flagged notes, does the owner agree each should be split, target >= 0.5, no history required.

**Most likely reason it fails.** DEAD: unbounded cold start with no reconstruction path (violates measure-first), an unpriced doubling of the query-time funnel on the plugin's tightest constraint, and an unvalidated top-5%-most-edited storage heuristic. The one genuinely useful piece — suggesting a note be split — needs no time axis and should be pulled out and pursued on its own.

**Needs first.** Would need months of plugin runtime before producing anything.


### 17. P13. Temporal negative evidence — DEAD. It inverts the product.

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Fewer repeat offenders in the panel.

**How.** I agree with the author's own argument against it and would go further. The plugin exists because users fail to notice connections. 'These two have coexisted for years and were never linked' is therefore not evidence of a non-relationship — it is the definition of the target case, and the pairs it would down-weight are precisely the ones Surprising Connections was built to surface (P@5 1.00, 36% surprising). Down-weighting them is not a precision improvement, it is the ranker learning to hide its own best output. The transfer from TGB's historical negative sampling is invalid because that regime describes how to EVALUATE a model on a graph whose non-edges are genuine negatives; in a personal vault a non-edge is a missing observation, not a negative label.

**Why it is not redundant.** Structurally orthogonal, yes. Orthogonal and harmful.

**Kill experiment.** Do not run it. The proposal's own protocol — a blind judged panel at matched recall, with lambda on and off — is the single most expensive measurement in the list, for a hypothesis its author expects to fail and whose failure mode is inverting the product's premise. That is the wrong place to spend judging budget. If the argument genuinely needs settling, settle it with N2's conditional-lift analysis, which answers the same question for free.

**Most likely reason it fails.** DEAD: down-weights exactly the pairs the product exists to surface. The one salvageable observation is that the ranker has no negative signal anywhere — if a negative signal is ever wanted, EXPLICIT user dismissal ('not related', a button that does not exist yet) is a real negative label and costs nothing to collect, unlike inferred non-linking.

**Needs first.** None. Do not build.


### 18. P14. Time-bucketed template de-crowding — DOWNGRADE to a null-result check; its stated evidence is wrong

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing visible. Notes made from an older version of a template stop clumping together in the panel.

**How.** The stated evidence does not hold and the mechanism partly duplicates shipped behaviour. (a) EVIDENCE: I counted the lab vault. 'Template v' appears 24 times and every one is 'Template v3' — a single generation, all in real/Daily. There are no v1 or v2 notes. The claim that 'at least three template generations share one group' is false; the footer records the template's version, not the note's vintage. (b) MECHANISM: template groups are not template ids — they are formed by heading-signature union-find requiring at least two shared headings between notes (src/index-store.ts:2346-2400). A template whose HEADINGS changed across generations therefore already splits into separate groups and already gets separate directions, automatically, today. The only case P14 addresses is a template whose headings stayed stable while its prose drifted — and prose drift under a stable skeleton is largely what the per-group direction estimate already averages out. So the addressable band is narrow. Keep the item only as a cheap diagnostic: bucket by quarter, recompute the existing crowding metric per bucket, and see whether it is flat.

**Why it is not redundant.** A correction to a correction, operating before ranking, so it cannot interact with fusion weights — correct.

**Kill experiment.** Keep the per-bucket crowding recomputation and the two-sided win condition (lower residual crowding in the newest bucket WITHOUT raising it in older ones). Run the diagnostic before writing the shrinkage estimator: if per-bucket crowding is flat, close the item, and expect that outcome. Chronological recall stays as a no-regression gate only.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Most likely a null result, and the vault that motivated it cannot demonstrate the problem — one template generation, and heading-based grouping already handles the case where a template's structure changed. The item's genuine merit is that it needs only quarter-level timestamp accuracy, making it the most robust thing here to bad edge times; that is an argument for running the diagnostic cheaply, not for building the estimator.

**Needs first.** P1 at quarter-level accuracy only.


## Time in the embedding space: drift and evolution


<details><summary>12 techniques surveyed</summary>

- **Diachronic local-neighborhood drift measure** (established, 2016) — Measures semantic change of one item by comparing its similarity profile over the union of its old and new top-K neighbours (second-order vectors), rather than comparing raw embeddings across frames. Removes the need for cross-frame alignment entirely — which is why proposal 7's 'store rankings, not vectors' works.  
  <sub>Hamilton, Leskovec, Jurafsky, 'Diachronic Word Embeddings Reveal Statistical Laws of Semantic Change', ACL 2016 (arXiv:1605.09096)</sub>
- **Kernel change-point detection with linear kernel + exact DP** (established, 2019) — Exact dynamic-programming segmentation of a sequence of vectors under a kernel-mean cost. For unit-norm vectors the linear-kernel segment cost collapses to (b-a) - ||sum v_i||^2/(b-a), computable from prefix sums in O(D) — no Gram matrix. Proposal 8's math checks out; complexity is O(M*D) precompute + O(K*M^2) DP.  
  <sub>Arlot, Celisse, Harchaoui, 'A kernel multiple change-point algorithm via model selection', JMLR 2019; implementation reference: Truong, Oudre, Vayatis, 'Selective review of offline change point detection methods' / ruptures, Signal Processing 2020</sub>
- **Foote novelty (checkerboard kernel on a self-similarity matrix)** (established, 2000) — Detects boundaries by convolving a checkerboard kernel down the diagonal of a self-similarity matrix. Kernel width is the time-scale knob. Useful here as an independent cross-check on the DP boundaries (agreement filter = precision over recall).  
  <sub>Foote, 'Automatic Audio Segmentation Using a Measure of Audio Novelty', IEEE ICME 2000</sub>
- **TextTiling and the Pk / WindowDiff segmentation metrics** (established, 1997/2002) — The baseline any linear text segmenter must beat, and the two standard error metrics that tolerate near-misses. Needed to make proposal 11's ship bar meaningful.  
  <sub>Hearst, 'TextTiling: Segmenting Text into Multi-paragraph Subtopic Passages', Computational Linguistics 1997; Pevzner & Hearst, 'A Critique and Improvement of an Evaluation Metric for Text Segmentation', Computational Linguistics 2002</sub>
- **Event segmentation by prediction error / Bayesian surprise** (established, 2007/2020) — Human perceivers place event boundaries where a running predictive model must update, not where adjacent content is merely dissimilar. Motivates proposal 11's refinement — but the Gaussian-KL formulation needs diagonal covariance or shrinkage, since w~5 samples in 384-D is rank-deficient.  
  <sub>Zacks et al., 'Event perception: a mind-brain perspective', Psychological Bulletin 2007; Franklin, Norman, Ranganath, Cohen, Gershman, 'Structured Event Memory: a neuro-symbolic model of event cognition', Psychological Review 2020</sub>
- **Temporal Context Model and the lag-CRP** (established, 1996/2002) — Human free recall shows an approximately scale-free contiguity effect — items studied near in time cue each other, with a power-law-ish rather than exponential falloff. This is the only principled reason to prefer 1/(1+|dt|/tau) over exp(-|dt|/tau) in proposals 4/5/14.  
  <sub>Howard & Kahana, 'A distributed representation of temporal context', Journal of Mathematical Psychology 2002; Kahana, 'Associative retrieval processes in free recall', Memory & Cognition 1996</sub>
- **Continuous-Time Dynamic Network Embeddings (CTDNE) — time-respecting walks** (established, 2018) — Argues that time-agnostic random walks encode temporally impossible sequences, and restricts walks to non-decreasing edge times. Correct as a critique of retrospective link-prediction evaluation; see my correction to proposal 4 for why it is the wrong thing to put in a production ranker.  
  <sub>Nguyen, Lee, Rossi, Ahmed, Koh, Kim, 'Continuous-Time Dynamic Network Embeddings', WWW 2018 Companion</sub>
- **Resource Allocation index and time-decayed link-prediction weights** (established, 2007/2009) — RA(s,t) = sum over shared neighbours x of 1/deg(x) — already shipped in src/graph-signals.ts. The temporal-link-prediction literature's standard extension is edge-weight decay by recency; it is a per-vault-fitted tunable, which this project has already rejected twice (CSLS, personal metric).  
  <sub>Zhou, Lu, Zhang, 'Predicting missing links via local information', European Physical Journal B 2009; Liben-Nowell & Kleinberg, 'The link prediction problem for social networks', JASIST 2007</sub>
- **Network archaeology — recovering node arrival order from final topology** (recent-but-replicated, 2011/2021) — Under growth models with preferential attachment, the arrival order of nodes is partially recoverable from the static final graph, and the work quantifies the phase boundary where it becomes unrecoverable. This is the literature basis for my new proposal N2: an ordinal pseudo-time for vaults whose filesystem timestamps are destroyed by sync.  
  <sub>Young, Cantwell, Newman, 'Network archaeology: phase transition in the recoverability of network history', Journal of Complex Networks 2021; also Navlakha & Kingsford, 'Network archaeology: uncovering ancient networks from present-day interactions', PLoS Computational Biology 2011. arXiv ids not verified in this session — confirm before citing.</sub>
- **SpringRank / HodgeRank — scalar hierarchy from a directed graph** (established, 2011/2018) — Both fit a real-valued score per node such that observed edge directions are explained as flowing down (or up) the score. On a wikilink graph, where you can only link to what already exists, the fitted score is a usable seniority axis. SpringRank is a sparse linear solve; HodgeRank is least squares on the graph Laplacian — both fit comfortably in the WASM heap at 100k nodes.  
  <sub>De Bacco, Larremore, Moore, 'A physical model for efficient ranking in networks', Science Advances 2018 (arXiv:1709.09002); Jiang, Lim, Yao, Ye, 'Statistical ranking and combinatorial Hodge theory', Mathematical Programming 2011</sub>
- **Kleinberg burst detection over arrival times** (established, 2002) — Infinite-state automaton over inter-arrival gaps, yielding nested bursts. Cited by proposal 10; I recommend cutting it from v1 — raw note-count-per-slice carries the same information at the size of a 200-pixel strip.  
  <sub>Kleinberg, 'Bursty and Hierarchical Structure in Streams', KDD 2002</sub>
- **Smoothed / shrunk PMI for sparse co-occurrence** (established, 1990/2015) — Raw PMI blows up on low counts; add-alpha smoothing or context-distribution smoothing (raising context counts to the 0.75 power) is the standard fix. Directly relevant to proposal 6's session co-occurrence scoring, where nearly all counts are 1 or 2.  
  <sub>Church & Hanks, 'Word association norms, mutual information, and lexicography', Computational Linguistics 1990; Levy, Goldberg, Dagan, 'Improving Distributional Similarity with Lessons Learned from Word Embeddings', TACL 2015</sub>

</details>


### 1. [CORRECTED] Authoring-time resolver t(note) — a memoized derivation, NOT an index field, with per-feature gates

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing visible on its own, except one honest line in settings: "Time features: 78% of your notes have a reliable date (2019-2026)." Below the threshold, every time-based feature stays switched off instead of lying to you.

**How.** The idea is right and it is the correct first build. Four corrections. (1) DO NOT persist into the index entry. The researcher points at StoredEntryMeta.mtime (src/vector-math.ts:265/297/314) — that field is the incremental-indexing staleness key, compared by exact equality at src/index-store.ts:1866 (`existing.mtime === file.stat.mtime`). Overloading or crowding it risks the rebuild path, and INDEX_VERSION is at 8 with a documented no-silent-half-migration policy. Nothing in resolve() needs the index: frontmatter comes from metadataCache, the path from TFile, ctime/mtime from file.stat — all synchronous at rank time. Ship src/note-time.ts as a pure function plus a Map<path, {created, modified, confidence}> memo invalidated on the same events that already invalidate wordCache/snippetCache. Zero index-format risk, zero reindex, deletable in one commit. (2) Demote tier (3) — 'first date-shaped string in the first 200 chars' — from medium to LOW. In a German/mixed personal vault a body date is as often a referenced date (a meeting, a historical event, a due date) as an authoring date. Counting it as medium inflates coverage, which is precisely what the gate exists to prevent. (3) Reading Daily/Periodic Notes formats needs app.internalPlugins.getPluginById('daily-notes')?.instance?.options?.format or a vault.adapter.read of .obsidian/daily-notes.json — both undocumented. Wrap in try/catch, fall back to regexes, never let the gate's value depend on it. (4) Replace the single global gate with two: features that partition the whole vault (chapters, era de-crowding, drift) need global coverage; pairwise features (dormant rediscovery, entity-time) need only high confidence on THE TWO NOTES IN THE PAIR and should gate per-pair. A global 0.6 gate would needlessly silence a per-pair feature on a vault where 40% of notes are confidently dated.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel — the precondition for all of them, and a precision-first safety device. It is what makes a temporal feature stay silent on a freshly-synced vault, a restored backup, or a Dropbox vault that rewrote every mtime. I confirmed the researcher's lab-vault diagnosis independently: 528 markdown files, every single mtime in 2026-07, zero notes with a date/created/Created/ctime frontmatter key, 24 date-shaped filenames of which 6 are the Daily folder. The time axis in the lab corpus is flat.

**Kill experiment.** (a) Correctness on a git-versioned vault (proposal 2): compare resolve() against `git log --diff-filter=A --format=%aI -- <file> | tail -1`; require median absolute error < 1 day on confidence>=medium, and require that low-confidence notes are exactly the ones git disagrees with. (b) Gate behaviour: pointed at /Users/justus/obsidian_atomized_intermediary/lab/vault it must report coverage < 0.1 and disable every temporal feature. If it reports high coverage there, the resolver is broken. (c) Added: run it on the owner's real vault via a plugin command and report only the aggregate — that number decides whether this whole area is live or dead.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Frontmatter date conventions are endlessly idiosyncratic, so real-vault coverage may be low and the gate may keep everything switched off. That is the correct failure, and it is the cheapest possible way to learn it. Secondary risk the researcher missed: on a synced vault, file.stat.mtime is rewritten by the sync client, so tier (5) is not merely low-confidence, it is actively misleading for any 'dormancy' use (see proposal 12).

**Needs first.** none — build this first, this week


### 2. [CORRECTED] A corpus with a real time axis, and a prospective held-out protocol

*moderate · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. This decides whether any of the other proposals is allowed to ship.

**How.** Right instinct, second-highest value item. Corrections. (1) COST, which the researcher omitted: a prospective split at cutoff T needs the note TEXT as of T, so each cutoff is a fresh embedding pass. Four cutoffs x two model families = eight passes. This is affordable only because bench/jina-cache.mjs already keys its cache by note text — unchanged notes hit the cache, so real cost is proportional to revisions, not notes. Reuse that cache; do not write a new one. (2) The proposed leak control ('were both timestamps inside the masked window') is weak and artificial. Use the temporal contiguity score itself as the control — i.e. run proposal 5's TC(s,t) = 1/(1+|dt|/tau) on both protocols. If TC scores high recall under random masking and near-chance under the prospective split, the leak is demonstrated AND proposal 5 is adjudicated in the same run. One experiment, two answers. (3) Per the house rule that reimplemented harnesses measure the wrong code: a .mjs harness is fine for the corpus study, but any ship decision must be re-run through the real IndexStore. Budget that as a second, separate integration harness. (4) Provenance and licence: this pulls other people's published vaults onto disk. Extend lab/corpus-manifest.json with source URL, licence, and commit SHA per note; keep the corpus out of the plugin repo; never redistribute. The manifest convention already exists — extend it, don't invent one. (5) Record link-CREATION times (the commit where each [[link]] first appears) — the researcher is right that this is the real ground truth, and it is also what makes proposals 6 and N3 measurable retrospectively.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel. It is the admission rule's measuring instrument, and the current instrument reads zero on the axis in question.

**Kill experiment.** Self-validating. The protocol is correct if (i) the content-only baseline on the prospective split is materially LOWER than 0.664 — predicting the future is harder than filling a hole — and (ii) the TC control separates the two protocols as described in (2). If TC does not separate them, the leak argument is wrong and random masking can be kept, which would save a great deal of work.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Public git-versioned vaults are mostly small, English, and digital-garden-shaped (heavy MOCs, few journals), so they may not represent the owner's German/mixed personal vault. Second risk the researcher underweighted: a digital garden's git history reflects PUBLISHING events, not authoring — many gardens are exported in bulk from a private vault, which flattens creation times exactly the way iCloud does. Screen candidate corpora for commit-time dispersion before adopting them, or the new corpus inherits the old corpus's disease.

**Needs first.** proposal 1


### 3. [CORRECTED] Era de-crowding — copy the template mechanism exactly, and only after N1 proves the pathology exists

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Related notes stop being dominated by "everything else I wrote that month". You get the note from three years ago that actually belongs, instead of four siblings from the same week.

**How.** The analogy is right; three mechanism details are wrong and one cost is missing. (1) The shipped template correction does NOT use PCA or power iteration. src/index-store.ts:subtractTemplateDirections calls computeCentroid(vectors, dims) — an L2-normalised group MEAN — then centerVector, i.e. full projection removal at beta=1, no sweep. vault-map.ts:pca2 is a 2-D layout projection, not a de-crowding tool. Copy the shipped pattern; a power-iteration path is strictly more machinery for a worse-understood estimate. (2) It is NOT applied at index time on the int8 vectors. It is applied post-dequantisation at RANK time, in DequantCache.get (src/index-store.ts ~842-850), which is why beta is tunable without a reindex. Baking it into the stored bytes would destroy that. Keep it where the template correction is. (3) The structural difference the researcher missed: templates cover a SUBSET of notes; eras PARTITION the whole vault. DequantCache holds exactly one templateDirs entry per note, and subtractTemplateDirections does dirs.set(path, shared) — one direction, last group wins. Subtracting K era-means at beta=1 across a partition is equivalent to deleting a K-dimensional subspace globally, and with monthly slices K can exceed 60. That is 'remove the top 60 principal components', a known discriminability-destroyer. Concrete fix: derive eras from proposal 8's CHAPTERS (K ~ 5-9), not from months, and make beta mandatory and shrunk by slice mass. (4) MISSING COST, and it is the real one: this correction has to live inside recomputeCentroid(), which is called on EVERY single-file add/edit/remove (src/index-store.ts:2193, 2211) and already does an O(N*D) centroid, an O(N*D) centred-mean rebuild, a whole-vault heading-bucketed union-find in templateGroups(), and a full dequant-cache clear. At 100k notes that path is already the most expensive thing in the plugin per save; adding era grouping to it is what will freeze the UI, not the ranking. This needs incremental maintenance or a debounced recompute before any era work starts.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is a correction to the content channel, not a new channel, so the admission rule reads differently: it must move a number the graph channel cannot, namely CROSS-ERA link recall.

**Kill experiment.** Gate on N1 first: if the shipped panel does not over-return same-era notes relative to the null |dt| distribution, there is no pathology and this must not be built. If it does: prospective split, partition held-out links into SAME-ERA (|dt| < 90d) and CROSS-ERA (|dt| > 1y), ship only if cross-era recall@10 rises >= 0.02 while same-era drops <= 0.005 at some beta, on BOTH model families (the CSLS precedent). Secondary: top-1 concentration skew must not rise (the metric that killed PPR-as-score, currently 2.3). Pre-check, five minutes: cosine between each era direction and the existing template directions — if near-collinear, the fix is already applied and beta's optimum is 0.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Three live failure modes. (1) The era direction may occupy the template subspace already (people adopt templates in phases). (2) On a vault where topic genuinely changes with time, the era direction IS signal and subtracting it hurts — a student vault is exactly that case. (3) The recomputeCentroid hot path: this is the change most likely to be measured as a recall win and shipped as a perceptible stutter on every save.

**Needs first.** proposals 1, 2, and new proposal N1


### 4. [CORRECTED] Time-respecting RA belongs in the EVALUATION, not in the ranker

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing user-facing. The corrected version is an evaluation-hygiene fix, not a feature.

**How.** The researcher's own premise defeats the production version. 'A note that did not exist cannot have been the bridge' is true of causation and false of EXPLANATION. At query time every note in the vault exists now; the user is asking 'what is related to this note today', not 'what could have mediated this in 2019'. A bridge written last week that genuinely connects two old notes is a legitimate and often more interesting receipt — filtering it out actively hides present-day structure. So: implement the time-respecting edge filter as part of the prospective evaluation harness (where using post-cutoff bridges to predict post-cutoff links IS leakage and must be stopped), and do NOT put it in src/graph-signals.ts. Second: HUB_DEGREE = 40 already excludes MOCs and index notes from RA evidence, and the concept notes that remain as bridges in a personal vault are typically OLDER than both endpoints — so the time-respecting filter is close to a no-op on exactly the population it targets. Third, on decayed RA: it introduces a per-vault-fitted tau. This project has rejected two fitted corrections already (CSLS, the personal similarity metric); the prior against a third should be stated up front rather than discovered after a day of work.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not independent — a replacement, so it must beat RA head to head. My correction is that it should not be evaluated as a ranker change at all; the honest claim is that the retrospective protocol has been crediting impossible bridges and the prospective protocol must stop doing so.

**Kill experiment.** In the harness, not the product: rerun proposal 2's prospective split twice, once with time-respecting edge filtering and once without, and report how much of the graph channel's 0.081 recall contribution survives. If the difference is under 0.005, the finding is 'the graph channel is genuinely atemporal' — publish that and close the line. Only if the difference is large is there a case for revisiting the ranker, and then the bar is head-to-head recall@10 >= 0.015 at every cutoff on both models, plus blind judging of 30 changed 'via <note>' receipts.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The production variant is dead on the argument above: it would suppress legitimate present-day bridges to satisfy a constraint that only exists inside a retrospective evaluation. The decayed variant is a fitted tunable with two prior rejections against it. Realistic outcome: one day spent, no ranker change, and the genuinely useful finding that the graph channel carries no temporal information — which also means proposals 3, 5 and 14 cannot borrow strength from it.

**Needs first.** proposals 1, 2


### 5. [DEAD as a channel, ALIVE as an instrument] Temporal contiguity

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. Do not build this as a channel.

**How.** Fold it into proposal 2 as the leak control and delete it as a product idea. The score TC(s,t) = 1/(1+|dt|/tau) is ~10 lines and is exactly the right diagnostic for whether random link-masking leaks time; building it as a fusion channel is a separate decision the evidence already argues against. Two accurate notes for the record. First, the researcher's self-assessment is correct and should be trusted: expected rank correlation with the graph channel of 0.4-0.8, against 0.916 that killed shared-tags and 0.075 that admitted the graph. Second — I checked, because ARCHITECTURE.md line 91 claims otherwise — there is currently NO recency term in the ranker. The structural boost weights are W_DIRECT_LINK 1.0, W_SHARED_TAGS 0.6, W_BIBLIO 0.5, W_FRONTMATTER 0.3 (src/index-store.ts:183-186); recency appears only as a display string in view.ts and as a tiebreak sort. The ARCHITECTURE doc is stale and should be fixed, but the absence of an existing recency term is not a reason to add one.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not. That is the finding.

**Kill experiment.** Already specified inside proposal 2: run TC on both the random-mask and prospective protocols. High recall on random-mask + near-chance on prospective = the leak is real and TC is confirmed as an artefact detector, not a signal. If someone insists on the channel test anyway, the kill criterion stands: correlation with RA > 0.6 AND delta recall < 0.01.

**Most likely reason it fails.** DEAD as a channel. Even in the branch where it wins on recall, it wins by dragging results into a recency ghetto, which breaks the compounding-vault promise the owner is explicitly optimising for — so it would have to be cut on inspection after being shipped, the worst possible order in which to learn it. The one branch that could revive it (recall@10 restricted to pairs with zero shared neighbours) is worth reporting from the proposal-2 run at no extra cost.

**Needs first.** proposal 2


### 6. [KEEP, measurement corrected] Session co-edit channel — and start the logger NOW

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** "You were working on these together." Notes you have repeatedly had open in the same sitting show up as related even when they share no words and no links — the notebook and the invoice, the recipe and the trip.

**How.** Best proposal in the set; keep the mechanism (workspace file-open/editor-change ring buffer, 30-minute session gaps, shrunk PMI with alpha ~ 1, per-session contribution cap, z-normalised rank fusion, local-only, opt-in, one-click wipe). Three corrections. (1) DECOUPLE COLLECTION FROM SCORING AND SHIP THE LOGGER IMMEDIATELY. Its only cost is calendar time, and every week of delay is a week of history the feature will not have. Ship an opt-in, off-by-default, write-only logger in the next release with no scoring attached; decide about the channel six months later against real data. The researcher framed this as a 10-week blocking experiment; it is not, if you start the clock now. (2) THE RETROSPECTIVE PROXY THE RESEARCHER MISSED: a git commit IS a session. Files touched in one commit were held in mind together. So the entire PMI + cap + fusion pipeline is measurable TODAY on proposal 2's corpus, using commit co-occurrence as the session proxy and links created in later commits as the target. That converts the go/no-go from ten weeks to about a day. Caveat to state in the writeup: commits are coarser and skew toward bulk-tidying sweeps — which is exactly what the per-session cap exists to handle, so the cap gets tested for free. (3) Intern paths to integer ids in the ring buffer; 50k raw path strings is several MB, not the claimed ~1 MB. Also: the wipe must clear the derived PMI counts, not just the log, and the log must be excluded from any diagnostic export.

**Why it is not redundant.** The one signal here that cannot be redundant by construction: it is behaviour, not text and not links. Neither the encoder nor resolvedLinks has access to which notes were open at 23:40 last Tuesday. It is also the only proposal that improves purely by continued use of Obsidian — the compounding property the owner asked for.

**Kill experiment.** Two stages. (a) NOW, retrospective on the git corpus: build PMI from commit co-occurrence up to cutoff T, measure recall@10 of links created in (T, T+90d] for content-only vs content+RA vs content+RA+session, plus rank correlation against RA. Kill immediately if correlation with RA > 0.85 — that is the shared-tags outcome. (b) LATER, prospective on the owner's real vault once the logger has six months of history: same protocol with true sessions. Kill at delta recall < 0.02 or coverage < 25% of new-link pairs having both endpoints in the log.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Cold start is brutal, and worthless forever on a read-heavy vault — which the shipped-logger-now correction mitigates but does not remove. It may simply re-derive the link graph, because you link the notes you had open together; the correlation test says so in a day. And a local activity log makes some users uncomfortable regardless of it never leaving the machine, so opt-in is non-negotiable and the setting copy has to be blunt about what is recorded.

**Needs first.** none — ship the logger this release


### 7. [KEEP, two blocking gaps] "The company this note keeps has changed" — neighbourhood drift receipts

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** On a note you have had for years, a quiet line: "In 2022 this sat next to Annealing, Revision, Sleep. Now it sits next to Retrieval, Ranking, Evaluation." One sentence, five note names, verifiable in two seconds by clicking them.

**How.** Store rankings, not embeddings — correct and elegant, and the Hamilton local-neighbourhood second-order measure is the right choice. The vault-growth control (score against a t0-restricted index and report only that) is the strongest single idea in the whole submission and must be mandatory. Two gaps to close. (1) IDENTITY. The ring buffer is keyed by note, and this store is keyed by PATH: renameFile (src/index-store.ts:2223) deletes the old entry and re-embeds under the new path, so a folder reorganisation makes every affected note look 100% drifted. You need a stable id with rename-following — either a path-history map maintained in renameFile, or a content-derived id — and that is the real engineering cost of this proposal, not the 12.8 MB. (2) WHAT IS A 'GENERATION'? Indexing here is incremental and idle-triggered; there is no natural full-index event, so the buffer would either never advance or fill with four near-identical snapshots taken hours apart. Define it explicitly: snapshot on a fixed cadence (quarterly) gated on the vault having changed by more than X% of notes since the last snapshot. Without this the feature silently does nothing for a year and then does something meaningless.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a similarity score — a DIFF of a ranking across time. Built from the content channel, but its output is a statement no channel can express: not 'these are related' but 'the relation changed'. Nothing in the current architecture has any memory of its own past output.

**Kill experiment.** Not link prediction. On the git corpus, build indices at two real dates 18+ months apart, then blind-judge: 40 notes, 20 from the high-drift head and 20 matched controls from the middle of the distribution; judge sees only the note and the two neighbour lists and answers 'did the meaning or role of this note change between these snapshots?'. Ship at judged precision >= 0.7 on the head with the control materially lower. MANDATORY second readout: the vault-growth control must remove >= 50% of naive high-drift candidates — near-zero removal means the machinery is unjustified, near-total removal means there is no feature.

**Most likely reason it fails.** On a personal vault notes are rarely rewritten, so drift is almost entirely vault growth and the control kills nearly every candidate — a true but empty feature. Cold start is a year on a normal iCloud vault, which is why new proposal N4 (Obsidian's own File Recovery snapshots) matters here; git backfill only exists for the lab corpus.

**Needs first.** proposals 1, 2


### 8. [KEEP, slicing corrected] Chapters: changepoint segmentation of the vault's own timeline

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** A short list at the top of the vault map: "Your vault has seven chapters." Each one a date range and three keyphrases. Click one to filter everything to that era.

**How.** I checked the math and it holds: for unit-norm vectors the linear-kernel segment cost is (b-a) - ||sum v_i||^2/(b-a), giving O(M*D) prefix-sum precompute plus O(K*M^2) DP; at M~100 months and D=384 that is a few million operations, genuinely trivial. Keep the DP, keep the Foote cross-check with the agree-within-one-month filter, keep the c-TF-IDF labeller with the rest of the vault as background. ONE SUBSTANTIVE CORRECTION the researcher missed: monthly slices are the wrong unit for a personal vault. Note volume per month is wildly uneven — one month with 300 notes and twenty months with three is normal — so a monthly-centroid segmentation mostly finds the big months and their dominant project. Use adaptive slices of roughly EQUAL NOTE COUNT (e.g. 25 notes per slice), run the DP over those, and map boundaries back to dates afterwards. This also makes the c-TF-IDF labels stable, which proposal 10 depends on. Second, smaller: state the model-selection penalty as beta*K*log(M) with beta calibrated once on the elbow AND validated by the stability test below, not by eye.

**Why it is not redundant.** It produces an object the plugin does not have: a partition of the vault by time and meaning jointly. The vault map partitions by meaning only and is recomputed from scratch each run; the link graph has no notion of when. Chapters are also the correct K for proposal 3's era directions (K~5-9, not K~60 months) and the substrate for proposal 9.

**Kill experiment.** (a) Face validity, blind: K detected boundaries interleaved with K random boundaries from the same date range, judge sees only surrounding note titles, ship at >= 0.7 discrimination. (b) Stability, which matters more: re-run with 10% of notes randomly removed, 20 times; a boundary must survive within +/- 1 month in >= 80% of runs. (c) DP and Foote must agree on >= 60% of boundaries, else the segmentation is not identifiable on this vault and the feature must not render.

**Most likely reason it fails.** A vault with steady interests has no chapters and the penalty picks K=1 — the honest answer, but it reads as broken; say so plainly in the UI. Bigger risk: boundaries may track LIFE events the vault records only indirectly (a job change, a move), so the c-TF-IDF label will be right about the words and wrong about the meaning. Added risk: chapters inherit proposal 1's coverage gate completely — a vault at 55% date coverage produces chapters built from the dated 55%, which is a biased sample (dailies and project notes are dated; evergreen concept notes are not).

**Needs first.** proposals 1, 2


### 9. [DOWNGRADED to speculative] "You were here before" — return detection across eras

*moderate · speculative confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** While you are writing, occasionally: "You were here before — Spring 2022." Three notes from that era and nothing since. Not "here is a similar note", but "you spent a season on this, then stopped, and you are back."

**How.** Mechanism is fine as written (top-5-within-chapter affinity rather than centroid; margin m; the cold-gap condition; the 30-day novelty condition; the not-already-linked condition; firing rate under 1 per 20 notes). The problem is the precedent, which the researcher did not cite: this project ALREADY measured 'Resurfacing' — 0.70 judged useful but 1/23 = 4% non-obvious — and cut it as the magic feature, re-scoping it to write-time link autocomplete (lab/results/REPORT-20260726.md lines 105/168, V3-SCOPE section 2.4). Proposal 9 is resurfacing with a temporal gap condition bolted on. The gap condition is a plausible reason it could clear the bar this time, but the bar must be the house's non-obviousness metric, not judged usefulness — Surprising Connections v2 set the reference at P@5 1.00 and 36% surprising. Second problem, structural: it sits four deep in a dependency chain (1 -> 2 -> 8 -> 9), on a feature with a 4% precedent. It should be the LAST thing attempted in this area, not the ninth.

**Why it is not redundant.** The firing condition is a SHAPE in a similarity-over-time profile — high, then cold, then high — which no scalar similarity and no graph score can express. That claim survives; it is the empirical prior that does not.

**Kill experiment.** Blind judged precision as specified, PLUS two additions. (a) Report % NON-OBVIOUS against both anchors — 4% (resurfacing, cut) and 36% (SC v2, shipped) — and require >= 25%, not merely 'is this a genuine return?'. (b) The mandatory SC-overlap check the researcher already specified: > 50% pair overlap with Surprising Connections means it is a re-skin and must be cut rather than shipped as a second card. Ship bar: P@5 >= 0.8, P@20 >= 0.6, firing rate under cap, non-obvious >= 25%.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The most speculative item in the set, and the resurfacing precedent is the reason: a feature that scored 0.70 useful and 4% non-obvious was already cut here, and this is that feature with a gap condition added. Three concrete deaths beyond that: the gap condition may almost never hold on a vault with continuous interests; chapter affinity may be dominated by writing style rather than topic (proposal 3 is the counterweight, and if that fails this probably fails too); and 'return' may be indistinguishable from 'I keep coming back to this constantly', which is not interesting. It also cannot work at all in a vault's first year, and it is the deepest item in the dependency chain.

**Needs first.** proposals 1, 2, 8 — build last


### 10. [KEEP, scope trimmed] Thread history strip: how one topic developed

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** On a cluster in the vault map, a compact horizontal strip: the topic's notes ordered by date, with the keyphrase label changing along it — "2021: note-taking, zettelkasten -> 2023: retrieval, ranking -> 2025: evaluation, held-out". Bars sized by how much you wrote; a spark where something new entered.

**How.** Keep almost verbatim. The three reuses are correct: spherical k-means cluster membership from vault-map.ts, the existing c-TF-IDF labeller with the cluster's OTHER slices as background (this is what makes the label change instead of repeating the cluster label), and the incremental running-centroid novelty for the spark. Two trims. (1) CUT Kleinberg burst detection from v1. Raw note-count-per-slice carries the same information at the size of a 200-pixel strip; nested sub-bursts are invisible there. (2) Use the same equal-note-count adaptive slicing as corrected proposal 8, and refuse to render a slice below the minimum. c-TF-IDF on six notes produces labels that churn for statistical reasons, which is exactly what the shuffled control is designed to catch — better to not render than to render noise and discover it in the A/B.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel, so the admission rule does not gate it — it is a presentation of structure the plugin already computes, plus a time axis. Its value is answering a question the sidebar cannot: not 'what is related' but 'how did this get here'.

**Kill experiment.** Keep the researcher's design verbatim — it is the best measurement idea in the submission. Control = the same strip with time SHUFFLED within the cluster. Blind A/B on 15 clusters: which strip tells a coherent story? Ship only if true-order wins >= 70%. Second readout: label churn between adjacent slices must be neither ~0 (constant labels, no story) nor ~1 (random).

**Most likely reason it fails.** Slice-level c-TF-IDF instability, mitigated by the equal-count slicing above. The source finding that running-centroid novelty correlated r = -0.002 with quality ratings stands: 'new direction' here means 'different from what came before' and nothing more, and the copy must not imply otherwise. Also inherits proposal 1's gate — an undated half of a cluster simply cannot be placed on the strip, and hiding those notes silently would misrepresent the history.

**Needs first.** proposals 1, 2


### 11. [CORRECTED] Episode segmentation — re-derive ideaOf, do not add a fourth granularity

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** A long journal, or a run of daily notes, gets a table of contents you never wrote: "Feb 3-11: the move", "Feb 12-28: back to the thesis". Collapsible, and each episode becomes a retrievable unit in its own right.

**How.** The detector is right; the integration story is wrong and would triple the cost. The plugin already has three granularities, and the middle one is exactly this object: StoredEntryMeta.ideaOf is one idea id per chunk row, currently derived from heading/paragraph structure, and BiMax already aggregates chunks into ideas with a live ideaInfluence knob. So do NOT add an 'episode' granularity — RE-DERIVE ideaOf for journal-shaped notes using the segmentation instead of the current structural grouping. ideaOf lives in the JSON manifest, not the binary blob, so this changes no byte layout and needs no re-embedding, only a relabel pass. The whole ranking path, the receipts, and the idea blend come along for free. Second correction: the Bayesian-surprise refinement as written (KL between Gaussians fit to the last w embeddings on each side) is rank-deficient — w~5 samples in 384 dimensions gives a singular covariance. Use diagonal covariance with shrinkage, or drop the refinement; this is where two days disappear if nobody says it first. Third: within-note paragraph segmentation and cross-note daily-note-sequence segmentation are two different features with different gates; the sequence one is meaningless below a few hundred dailies (the lab vault has 6) and should be gated on daily-note density, not on proposal 1's global coverage.

**Why it is not redundant.** It changes what the retrieval UNIT is, which is orthogonal to how units are scored. Today a long journal is one blob or arbitrary windows; a coherent temporal unit should raise precision on journal-heavy vaults and make receipts far more legible.

**Kill experiment.** Keep all three parts. (a) Pk and WindowDiff on Choi-style synthetic documents built by concatenating unrelated notes at known boundaries, against uniform segmentation, embedding-TextTiling, and Foote novelty — must beat all three. (b) The real check: hand-label boundaries in 10 real long journals and report Pk THERE, and set the ship bar on that number, not on (a). (c) End-to-end through the real IndexStore: does the re-derived ideaOf raise held-out link recall@10 for links whose endpoint is a long note, and does it leave short notes unchanged?

**Most likely reason it fails.** Real journals do not have clean boundaries — one day's entry mixes three threads — so linear segmentation is the wrong model and Pk on real text will be poor. If (b) lands near the uniform-segmentation baseline, cut: a table of contents with wrong chapter breaks is worse than none. It also only pays off on journal-heavy vaults, which may not be the median user, and the re-derivation must be conditional on note shape or it will degrade ideaOf on the atomic notes where the current heading-based grouping is already correct.

**Needs first.** proposals 1, 2


### 12. [DOWNGRADED to speculative] Dormant rediscovery — its load-bearing input is the one the vault cannot supply

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** While writing something new: "You wrote this before — March 2019." One old note, strongly on point, not linked, and not near anything you have touched since.

**How.** The gate stack is well designed and the per-gate ablation requirement is exactly right. But the sharpest objection is one the researcher wrote the evidence for in proposal 1 and then did not apply here. Gate (iii) — 'c has not been modified or OPENED in >= 12 months' — has no trustworthy source. Obsidian records no per-note last-opened time a plugin can read retrospectively, so 'opened' is unavailable until proposal 6's logger has a year of history. That leaves mtime, and on an iCloud/Dropbox/Syncthing vault mtime is rewritten wholesale by the sync client — which is precisely the pathology documented in the lab vault (528 files, every mtime in 2026-07). So the gate doing the most work in the whole proposal rests on the least reliable input available. Corrections: (a) dormancy must come from the proposal-6 session log or from proposal 1 at confidence >= medium, and (b) on a vault whose mtime distribution is degenerate the feature must not render at all — add that as an explicit render gate, not a caveat. Gate (vi), the 90-day recency centroid, is cheap and is the right defence against 'obviously related old note', but it depends on the same timestamps. Everything else — the top-few-percent similarity gate, the 2-hop graph exclusion, the outside-graph-top-N exclusion, and never fusing this into the ranker — keep verbatim.

**Why it is not redundant.** Every ingredient except the similarity floor is a rejection test content similarity cannot apply. And the researcher is right that it must never be measured on recall@10, because as a filter over the content channel it can only lose there.

**Kill experiment.** Blind judged precision as specified, with the same correction as proposal 9: report % NON-OBVIOUS against the 4% resurfacing precedent and the 36% Surprising Connections reference, and require >= 25%, because 'genuine rediscovery' versus 'obviously related old note' is exactly the distinction the earlier resurfacing evaluation failed to make. Keep the per-gate ablations (drop iii, drop iv+v, drop vi) — precision must fall in each case or the gate is decoration. Keep the mandatory overlap check against Surprising Connections v2 and the dupe alarm; > 50% overlap with either means cut.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The dormancy gate is the one doing the real work and it has no reliable input on a synced vault — mtime is the sync client's timestamp, not the user's. That makes the most likely shipped behaviour 'fires on whatever the sync happened not to touch', which is noise wearing a receipt. Beyond that: it will fire on duplicates the dupe alarm already handles better, and on evergreen reference notes that are dormant because they are finished rather than forgotten. Same 4%-non-obvious resurfacing precedent applies.

**Needs first.** proposal 1 (proposal 2 for the ablations); genuinely blocked on trustworthy dormancy, i.e. proposal 6's logger


### 13. [DEAD] Index-age bias audit — the bug does not exist; verified in the code

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. Do not build this.

**How.** The premise is false and I checked it directly. The proposal assumes centring happens BEFORE int8 quantisation, so that a chunk quantised in 2023 is frozen in an old corpus frame. It does not. quantizeChunksRaw (src/vector-math.ts:170) quantises RAW model-space chunk vectors with a per-row symmetric scale of maxAbs/127 — no centroid is involved at any point, so the stored bytes carry no corpus frame at all. Centring is applied LIVE at rank time, after dequantisation, inside DequantCache.get (src/index-store.ts ~842): centerChunksInPlace(f, entry.chunkCount, entry.dims, this.centroid) against the CURRENT centroid, followed by the template direction. And recomputeCentroid() (src/index-store.ts:2261) runs after every entry add/edit/remove and calls dequant.setCentroid(), which clears the entire dequant cache — so no vector can survive against a stale centroid even in RAM. There is no old-frame divergence to instrument, no monotone tilt to find, and no re-quantisation to schedule. WHERE TO LOOK INSTEAD, since the instinct to audit the substrate was sound: that same recomputeCentroid() is the real defect. It runs on EVERY single-file change and performs an O(N*D) centroid pass, a full centred-mean rebuild, a whole-vault heading-bucketed pairwise union-find in templateGroups(), and a complete dequant-cache flush. On a 100k-note vault that is a whole-vault main-thread pass per save, with a cold chunk cache afterwards. That is a measurable, shippable fix and it blocks proposal 3.

**Why it is not redundant.** n/a — withdrawn.

**Kill experiment.** The falsifying observation has already been made by reading src/vector-math.ts:170-200 and src/index-store.ts:786-850, 2261-2292. If anyone wants the confirmatory number anyway it is one afternoon: build the index in chronological halves, then compare stored int8 vectors dequantised-and-centred under the final centroid against freshly computed ones — they will be identical up to int8 rounding, because centring is applied at read time in both cases. Spend the afternoon on the recomputeCentroid hot path instead: time a single-file save on a synthetic 100k-note vault, before and after debouncing/incrementalising that path.

**Most likely reason it fails.** DEAD. Stated plainly: the bug described does not exist in this codebase. Quantisation is applied to raw vectors and centring is applied at rank time against the live centroid, which is recomputed on every entry change and invalidates the dequant cache. Building the instrument would produce a flat line by construction and would be mistaken for a clean bill of health on a question that was never open — which is worse than not building it.

**Needs first.** none


### 14. [KEEP as a single decisive measurement, not a feature] Entity-time binding

*moderate · speculative confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** "Both about Anna, both from 2019." Notes mentioning the same person or place around the same time surface together; the same name a decade apart is not treated as evidence.

**How.** The researcher's honesty about the shared-tags precedent (0.916 correlation, cut) is correct, and the specific bet — that the TIME KERNEL makes timed entity co-occurrence a different function from untimed shared tags — is the only part worth testing. So do not build the channel. Build the correlation measurement, which needs about 60 lines: extract entities (wikilink targets, tags, and capitalised multiword spans surviving a df filter of >= 3 notes and <= 5% of notes, screened against the de/en 50k frequency lists already in bench/data), build the note-entity incidence with timestamps, compute E(s,t) = sum over shared entities of [1/deg(e)] * 1/(1+|dt|/tau_e) with tau_e from that entity's own temporal spread, and correlate against RA. That is the whole decision. Two corrections. (1) Reuse the continuity harness's CP_LANG gate rather than writing a second German-capitalisation defence — the existing machinery already fights this battle and a parallel implementation will diverge. (2) tau_e estimated from an entity's own occurrence spread is a per-entity fitted parameter over a population where most entities appear in 3-5 notes; that estimate is noise for the long tail. Floor it: entities below ~8 occurrences get the global tau, not their own.

**Why it is not redundant.** Genuinely at risk and the proposal says so. The concrete argument for testing anyway is sound: two notes sharing an entity a decade apart score near zero under E and scored high in the untimed shared-tags test, so the two are demonstrably not the same function even on identical inputs. That is a testable claim, and it is cheap.

**Kill experiment.** Correlation FIRST, before any downstream work: rank correlation between E(s,t) and RA(s,t) over all candidate pairs on the prospective split. Above 0.85, stop — same fate as shared-tags, and report it as a negative result. Below 0.85, run the full admission test (delta recall@10 on top of content+RA), and report two extra slices: recall restricted to pairs with no shared link-neighbours (where it could actually earn a place), and the whole thing re-run with wikilink-derived entities EXCLUDED, so the test is not re-measuring the link graph in disguise.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Most likely it lands between shared-tags and the graph channel and fails the admission rule. Entity extraction without an LLM on a German/English mixed vault is the concrete failure point: German capitalises all nouns, so the capitalised-span heuristic degrades to noun extraction on half the corpus, and the df filter then removes the specific entities (a person appearing in 3 notes) along with the noise. Budget for the extractor being the hard part and the scoring being trivial — the opposite of how the proposal reads.

**Needs first.** proposals 1, 2


### 15. [NEW — N1] Measure the pathology before building the cure: a delta-t audit of the shipped ranker

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. It decides whether proposals 3, 5 and 14 have a problem to solve at all.

**How.** Every temporal fix in this set assumes the current ranker has a time bias, and nobody has measured whether it does. Do that first, in half a day. For every note in a dated corpus, take the shipped panel's actual top-10 (through the real IndexStore, not a reimplementation) and build the distribution of |t_query - t_result|. Compare it against the NULL: the |dt| distribution over all candidate pairs in the same vault, which already skews by however the vault was written. Three readouts. (a) Excess same-month mass in the top-10 over null — this is the era-crowding pathology, and if it is near zero, proposal 3 is dead before it starts. (b) The same split with template de-crowding disabled, which tells you how much of any era effect the shipped template correction already removes (my prediction: most of it, because dailies and project notes are the notes that are both same-era and same-template). (c) Top-1 |dt| median versus top-10 |dt| median — if the very top result is systematically more recent than the tail, the bias lives in BiMax's max operation rather than in the space. Then make it permanent: add a |dt|-stratified breakdown to the standard recall@10 readout in the bench harnesses, so every future channel reports whether its gains are concentrated in one era bucket. That is a five-line reporting change and it makes the admission rule time-aware forever.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel — an instrument, and the one that decides the priority order of everything else in this area. It is also the only item here that produces a number for the CURRENT product rather than a hypothetical one.

**Kill experiment.** Self-measuring. Ship criterion for the instrument itself: it must reproduce a known-positive control — run it on the pre-template-de-crowding index, where the documented pathology was 9.9/10 daily-note siblings in a daily note's top-10, and confirm the excess-same-month statistic fires loudly there. If it cannot detect a pathology that severe, the statistic is wrong.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The likely finding is that there is no meaningful era bias left after template de-crowding, which kills proposal 3 and weakens 5 and 14. That is a good outcome delivered in half a day rather than a month, but it does mean this proposal's most probable contribution is deleting other proposals rather than adding a feature. Secondary risk: it needs a dated corpus to run on, so it inherits proposal 2's dependency for the real number — though the known-positive control runs on the lab vault today.

**Needs first.** none for the control; proposal 2 for the real number — build alongside proposal 1


### 16. [NEW — N2] Ordinal pseudo-time from the link graph, for the vaults where timestamps are gone

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** On a vault whose file dates were destroyed by sync or a restore, time features still work — approximately. Settings says: "No reliable dates found. Using link structure to estimate note order (rough)." Chapters and history strips render as an ordering without calendar labels.

**How.** Proposal 1's most likely outcome is that the gate switches everything off, and the honest response is not to give up but to find a second time axis. The wikilink graph has an arrow of time baked in: you can only link to a note that exists when you write. Two cheap estimators, both within this codebase's existing competence. (a) SENIORITY BY SPECTRAL RANKING: fit a scalar score per note explaining observed link directions (SpringRank is a sparse linear solve; HodgeRank is least squares on the graph Laplacian) — old concept notes accumulate inbound links from many later notes and link out to few, so they sink to one end. Sparse, iterative, comfortably inside the 4 GiB heap at 100k notes, and the plugin already runs power iteration for the vault map. (b) NETWORK ARCHAEOLOGY: the growth-model literature shows node arrival order is partially recoverable from final topology under preferential attachment, and — crucially for a precision-first product — it also characterises the regime where it becomes unrecoverable, which gives a principled 'do not render' condition rather than an invented threshold. Output is ORDINAL only: a rank, never a date, and the UI must never print a year from it. Feeds proposals 8 and 10, which need an ordering rather than a calendar; it cannot feed proposal 12, which needs real elapsed time.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is derived from the link graph, so it is by construction correlated with the graph channel and must never be fused as a similarity channel — that is not what it is for. It is an AXIS, not a score: its job is to keep the temporal features alive on the vaults where filesystem timestamps are worthless, which is the failure mode proposal 1 predicts and does not answer.

**Kill experiment.** Direct and cheap on the proposal-2 git corpus, where true creation order is known. Spearman correlation between the pseudo-time ranking and git-derived creation order, reported per corpus. Usable at rho >= 0.5; below 0.35 mark it unrecoverable and have the plugin say so. Second, the falsifying control: the same correlation computed on a corpus whose creation order was deliberately shuffled must land at chance, otherwise the estimator is reading something other than time. Third, the end-to-end test that actually matters: run corrected proposal 8's chapter detection on pseudo-time and on true time over the same corpus and report boundary agreement; if pseudo-time chapters do not match real-date chapters within one slice on >= 60% of boundaries, this axis is not good enough to carry a user-facing feature.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The most likely failure is that a personal vault is not a citation network: many isolated notes, heavy bidirectional MOC linking, and a hub structure that swamps the directional signal — HUB_DEGREE=40 exists precisely because hubs distort everything here. If the corpus check comes back at rho ~ 0.2, the finding is 'a personal wikilink graph has no usable arrow of time' and the line closes in two days. Second risk: it invites a UI implying more precision than an ordinal ranking has, which is why the output must never be rendered as a date.

**Needs first.** proposal 2 (for ground-truth order); independent of proposal 1 by design


### 17. [NEW — N3] Self-diff at re-index: rank against the passage you just wrote

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** You add a paragraph to a long note and the panel quietly changes to match what you just wrote, not the note's overall average. No new card type, no setting, no waiting — the results are simply about the part that is live.

**How.** The submission goes straight to macro-time (eras, chapters, decades) and skips micro-time, which is where the immediate product value sits and where the data already exists. When updateFile re-embeds a note (src/index-store.ts ~2181), the OLD entry is still in this.entries at that moment — its int8 chunk buffer, scales and ideaOf are all in hand — and it is overwritten one line later. So a self-diff is free: dequantise both, match new chunk rows to old ones by nearest cosine, and flag rows with no close predecessor as NEW. Persist a small per-entry field (changedIdeas: number[] plus a timestamp) in the JSON manifest — no blob layout change, no re-embedding, no INDEX_VERSION bump, and old entries simply lack the field. Then, when the active note has recent changed ideas, weight those idea groups up inside the existing BiMax/idea-blend path. This is a knob on machinery that already exists (ideaInfluence is already a live, no-reindex knob), and the receipt writes itself: "matches the passage you added". It is temporal in the only sense that needs no resolver at all — it uses the plugin's own indexing events as the clock, so it is completely immune to proposal 1's coverage gate and works on day one on any vault, synced or not.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not a similarity channel and not a graph signal — it is a reweighting of the query side driven by a diff of the index against itself. Neither content similarity nor the link graph has any notion of which part of the active note is new. It is also the only proposal in this area with no cold start, no corpus requirement, and no dependency on trustworthy timestamps.

**Kill experiment.** Directly on the prospective protocol, with ground truth that already exists in the git corpus: for each commit that both modifies a note and adds wikilinks from it, the diff gives the changed chunks and the added links give the target.
