Measure recall@10 of those same-commit links for the shipped ranker versus the changed-idea-weighted ranker, sweeping the weight. Ship at delta recall@10 >= 0.02 on same-commit links with NO regression (<= 0.005) on the standard held-out protocol, on both model families — the second half is the important one, because this must not degrade the normal case. Second readout: what fraction of re-embeds produce any changed idea at all; if a typical edit rewrites most chunks (likely for short atomic notes), the feature only ever fires on long notes and should be gated to them.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Two real ones. (1) Chunk boundaries shift when text is inserted, so a one-sentence addition mid-note can re-window every subsequent chunk and make the whole note look new. Nearest-cosine matching absorbs some of this, but on a note where every chunk moved you get a false 'all new' — needs a guard that suppresses the feature when the changed fraction exceeds ~50%. (2) On an atomic-note vault, notes are short and edits are whole-note rewrites, so the feature may almost never fire; that is what the second readout measures, and it is a clean kill criterion.

**Needs first.** proposal 2 for the corpus; none for the implementation


### 18. [NEW — N4] Backfill local history from Obsidian's own File Recovery snapshots

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** Nothing directly. It is what makes proposal 7's drift receipts and proposal 11's episode detection work in month two instead of year two, on a normal vault with no git.

**How.** Proposal 7's fatal practical problem is that its ring buffer needs four index generations, which on a real vault means a year of waiting, and it cannot be backfilled from git because a normal iCloud vault has no git. But Obsidian ships a core plugin, File Recovery, that already keeps periodic local snapshots of every edited note's TEXT in a local IndexedDB with a user-configurable retention window. That is historical note text sitting on the user's own disk, offline, never having left the machine — exactly the input drift and episode detection need. The build: read the recovery store, reconstruct each note's text at N past timestamps, embed those in the existing Web Worker during idle, and synthesise the past index generations proposal 7 would otherwise wait for. Everything stays local; no new model, no new heap pressure beyond a transient embedding pass the idle scheduler already handles. BE HONEST ABOUT THE COST: the recovery store's schema is undocumented and reading another core plugin's IndexedDB is exactly what an Obsidian community-plugin review flags. So this must be built as a strictly optional, explicitly opt-in, read-only, fully feature-detected path that degrades to 'no history available' the moment the schema does not match, and it must never be on the critical path for anything. The one-day experiment that decides it: open the store on the owner's real vault, see what is actually in there, and measure how far back it reaches.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel and not a scoring change — a data source. It is the only route to historical note text on a vault with no version control, and without it two of the three 'magic' proposals in this area have a one-year cold start that no amount of clever scoring can fix.

**Kill experiment.** Staged, cheapest first. (a) One day: enumerate the recovery store on a real vault and report coverage — how many notes have snapshots, how far back the oldest reaches, at what interval. If the answer is 'a week of the twelve notes you edited', the proposal dies immediately and cheaply, and proposal 7 keeps its one-year cold start as a stated fact. (b) If coverage is months: reconstruct a synthetic past index and validate against the git corpus by running the same reconstruction on a git-versioned vault, where `git show <sha>:<path>` gives the true past text — require reconstructed drift scores to correlate above 0.8 with git-derived ones. (c) Only then wire it to proposals 7 and 11.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Two, both serious. (1) Retention may be far too short — if the default window is days rather than months there is no history here and the proposal is worthless; step (a) settles that for one day of work. (2) Reading an undocumented internal store is a plugin-review and forward-compatibility liability; an Obsidian update can silently change the schema, and the failure must be a shrug rather than a crash. Neither risk justifies skipping step (a), because the alternative on offer is telling every non-git user that the drift feature starts working in a year.

**Needs first.** proposal 7 is the consumer; step (a) can run today, independent of everything


## Events, timelines and narrative structure


<details><summary>16 techniques surveyed</summary>

- **HeidelTime rule-based temporal tagger** (established, 2013) — Language-separated regex resource files + a normalisation layer that turns a matched expression into a TIMEX3 value; a domain switch (news / narrative / colloquial / scientific) decides whether relative expressions resolve against the document creation time or chain off the previously resolved TIMEX. The architecture the researcher wants to port is real and is the right one.  
  <sub>Strötgen & Gertz, 'Multilingual and cross-domain temporal tagging', Language Resources and Evaluation 47(2), 2013 (system introduced at SemEval-2010 Task 13). Identifier not re-verified this session — web search budget was exhausted; confirm before citing in release notes.</sub>
- **TimeML / TIMEX3 and TempEval-3 scoring** (established, 2003/2013) — The annotation standard for temporal expressions and the standard split of extraction score from value-normalisation accuracy. Worth adopting purely for the measurement discipline: reporting extraction P/R and value accuracy separately is what stops a tagger from looking good while normalising wrongly.  
  <sub>Pustejovsky et al., 'TimeML: Robust Specification of Event and Temporal Expressions in Text', 2003; UzZaman et al., 'SemEval-2013 Task 1: TempEval-3', SemEval 2013.</sub>
- **chrono-node** (established, ongoing) — MIT-licensed JS natural-language date parser. English is strong; German is partial. Returns Date objects, not half-open intervals with granularity, so its output must be wrapped either way. Roughly 60-100 KB gzipped against a 637 KB bundle.  
  <sub>github.com/wanasit/chrono (npm: chrono-node). Not fetched this session.</sub>
- **Kleinberg burst detection (two-state gap automaton)** (established, 2002) — Infers burst intervals from the gaps in an event stream by fitting a hidden two-state (or hierarchical) automaton with a state-transition cost. Used here for two different jobs: flagging machine-written timestamp bursts, and detecting when a topic had momentum.  
  <sub>Kleinberg, 'Bursty and Hierarchical Structure in Streams', KDD 2002 / Data Mining and Knowledge Discovery 7(4), 2003.</sub>
- **Recurrency Baseline (RecB) for temporal KG forecasting** (recent-but-replicated, 2023) — A parameter-light baseline that scores a candidate by how recently and how often the same fact recurred, with an exponential decay 2^(lambda*(t - t_now)) and a per-relation lambda fitted from a small grid, plus a relaxed back-off term when no exact recurrence exists. Competitive with, and on several datasets better than, neural TKG models.  
  <sub>Gastinger et al., 'History repeats itself: A Baseline for Temporal Knowledge Graph Forecasting', IJCAI 2023. Title/venue from memory; re-verify the identifier.</sub>
- **Resource Allocation index** (established, 2009) — Local link-prediction score, sum over shared neighbours x of 1/deg(x). Already shipped in src/graph-signals.ts and measured at 0.771-0.775 R@10 when fused. Named here because the decay proposal is a reweighting of it.  
  <sub>Zhou, Lü & Zhang, 'Predicting missing links via local information', European Physical Journal B 71, 2009.</sub>
- **CTDNE — continuous-time dynamic network embeddings** (established, 2018) — Random walks restricted to non-decreasing edge timestamps, so a walk traces a path that could actually have propagated influence. Requires per-edge timestamps, which Obsidian does not store.  
  <sub>Nguyen et al., 'Continuous-Time Dynamic Network Embeddings', WWW '18 Companion.</sub>
- **Narrative event chains and the narrative cloze** (established, 2008) — Learns PMI between event types that share a protagonist and orders them; evaluated by holding out one event from a chain and measuring the rank at which it is predicted. The cloze evaluation is the genuinely useful export here — it needs no UI and no labels.  
  <sub>Chambers & Jurafsky, 'Unsupervised Learning of Narrative Event Chains', ACL 2008; 'Unsupervised Learning of Narrative Schemas and their Participants', ACL 2009.</sub>
- **TextTiling** (established, 1997) — Places topic boundaries at valleys in lexical cohesion between adjacent blocks, with a threshold set from the mean and standard deviation of the cohesion series. This is exactly what assignIdeas already implements (index-store.ts:689, valleyThr = mean - 0.5*sd over a Jaccard series).  
  <sub>Hearst, 'TextTiling: Segmenting Text into Multi-paragraph Subtopic Passages', Computational Linguistics 23(1), 1997.</sub>
- **Bayesian surprise as a predictor of human event boundaries** (frontier-unproven, 2023) — Reported finding: when people listen to a story, the points they mark as event boundaries are predicted by Bayesian surprise (KL between the belief state before and after the new evidence) rather than by surprisal, and cosine distance between successive embeddings is the weaker signal. This is the strongest motivation for changing the chunker's boundary statistic.  
  <sub>Kumar, Goldstein, Michelmann, Zacks, Hasson & Norman, 'Bayesian Surprise Predicts Human Event Segmentation in Story Listening', Cognitive Science, 2023. Author list and venue from memory and NOT verified this session — treat as a lead to confirm, not a citation to quote.</sub>
- **Event Segmentation Theory** (established, 2007) — The cognitive-science frame that people parse continuous experience into discrete events at prediction-error spikes, and that boundaries act as memory access points. Useful as the design rationale for the episodes surface.  
  <sub>Zacks, Speer, Swallow, Braver & Reynolds, 'Event perception: A mind-brain perspective', Psychological Bulletin 133(2), 2007.</sub>
- **Point algebra / Allen interval algebra with path consistency** (established, 1983/1986) — Constraint network over interval endpoints; path consistency detects contradictions and determines which pairwise orderings are entailed, leaving the rest explicitly indeterminate. Tractable for the point-algebra fragment.  
  <sub>Allen, 'Maintaining Knowledge about Temporal Intervals', CACM 26(11), 1983; Vilain & Kautz, 'Constraint Propagation Algorithms for Temporal Reasoning', AAAI 1986.</sub>
- **TLEX — timeline extraction from TimeML temporal graphs** (recent-but-replicated, ~2022) — Extracts a main chronology plus subordinate branches from a TimeML temporal graph, marks indeterminate sections explicitly, and reports how many annotated documents are internally inconsistent. The 'trunk and branches with explicit unknowns' output shape is the precision-first pattern worth copying.  
  <sub>Ocal & Finlayson et al., TLEX, JAIR ~2022. Venue, year and the '123 of 385 documents inconsistent' figure the first researcher quotes are unverified — I could not check them this session.</sub>
- **c-TF-IDF cluster labelling (BERTopic)** (established, 2022) — Treats each cluster as one concatenated document and ranks terms by class-based TF-IDF to label it. Already shipped in src/vault-map.ts; reusable verbatim for episode labels.  
  <sub>Grootendorst, 'BERTopic: Neural topic modeling with a class-based TF-IDF procedure', 2022.</sub>
- **EpiMine / discriminative term combination for episode mining** (frontier-unproven, unknown) — Claimed by the first researcher as a way to sharpen episode boundaries via terminology shifts. I could not locate or verify this reference.  
  <sub>UNVERIFIED. Do not build against this citation until someone confirms the paper exists and says what the proposal claims.</sub>
- **Obsidian internal APIs that already carry time** (established, current) — Three the proposals ignore: app.internalPlugins.getPluginById('daily-notes').instance.options gives the user's own date format and folder (so daily notes are identified by configuration, not by guessing filename regexes); app.workspace.getLastOpenFiles() gives the persisted reading order (already used at view.ts:365); and the core File Recovery plugin, enabled by default and confirmed on in this lab vault's core-plugins.json, keeps periodic local snapshots per note in the renderer's IndexedDB.  
  <sub>Obsidian plugin API and the lab vault's own .obsidian/core-plugins.json (file-recovery: true, daily-notes: true, sync: true).</sub>

</details>


### 1. [CORRECTED] Note-time resolver — but its first deliverable is a coverage census, not code

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Every note gets one reliable 'when', or an honest admission that it has none. On a vault with dates that means cards can say 'written three days before this one'. On a vault without them the plugin says nothing at all rather than reporting a sync event as new thinking.

**How.** Right idea, three corrections to the ladder. (1) The claimed downstream ranking effect does not exist. I read structuralSignals() at src/index-store.ts:3062: the boost is directLink (W_DIRECT_LINK 1.0) + shared-tag Jaccard (0.6) + bibliographic coupling (0.5) + shared frontmatter (0.3), normalised by their sum. There is no recency term anywhere in rank(). mtime appears in ranking code nowhere; it appears in view.ts:392 and view.ts:463 as a display string, and in link-suggester.ts:265 as a cold-start ordering fallback. So delete measurement part (b) — it measures a nudge that isn't there. (2) Tier 1 should not regex-guess daily-note conventions. app.internalPlugins.getPluginById('daily-notes').instance.options returns the user's configured {format, folder}; parse against that first, and fall back to ISO patterns only for notes outside it. (3) Add a tier 0 the ladder is missing entirely — see the daily-note spine proposal below — because on a real vault propagation from dated linkers has more coverage than tiers 2 and 3 combined. Drop Kleinberg from this proposal: on the lab vault, detecting the sync rewrite needs one ratio, not an automaton (465 notes, mtime in exactly 2 distinct day buckets, 333 on 2026-07-02 and 132 on 2026-07-01). distinct_mtime_days / note_count < 0.02 is the whole diagnostic.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel; it is the substrate. But the correct first output is not a resolver, it is a number, and that number decides whether anything else in this area gets built.

**Kill experiment.** Reordered. FIRST, before writing note-time.ts: a coverage census, one hour of scripting, run across the lab vault and two or three real vaults. Report per tier: fraction of notes with a filename/folder date, with a frontmatter date key, with a day-or-better date in the first 200 words, and distinct_mtime_days / note_count. I ran this by hand on the 465-note real/ corpus and the answer is 18/465 dated filenames (3.9%), 0 notes carrying any of date/created/start/published/when/datum/Datum/erstellt (the only frontmatter keys present in the whole corpus are tags 163, 'Related Notes' 145, aliases 140, words 1), 1 note with an ISO date in its body, and mtime/ctime both fully degenerate. Only if a target vault clears roughly 40% combined tier-1+tier-2 coverage does the resolver get built. THEN correctness: 150 notes stratified by tier, hand-checked, bar 0.95 exact-day on tiers 1-3 and 'reports low confidence' on tiers 4-5 — but this sample can only be drawn from a vault where tiers 2-3 are non-empty, which the lab vault is not.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The census is the risk made explicit. On the benchmark vault the ladder collapses to 3.9% coverage and every temporal feature downstream inherits garbage while looking confident. The coverage gate must be hard, and it must be a gate on shipping, not just a runtime switch.

**Needs first.** None. This is the gate for everything else in this list.


### 2. [CORRECTED, prose layer downgraded to speculative] TIMEX tagger — filename/frontmatter normaliser only

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** The plugin understands the date formats users actually write in filenames and frontmatter, across locales, and refuses to guess when a format is ambiguous.

**How.** The full HeidelTime port is the wrong first move and the researcher's own cost estimate of 'moderate' understates it — a pattern layer plus a normalisation layer plus a narrative-mode domain switch plus separate EN and DE resource files is weeks, and per-rule ablation on top of that. Split it. SHIP: a ~150-line normaliser that takes a string and returns {t_start, t_end, granularity, confidence} for ISO dates, ISO weeks, YYYY-MM, and the user's configured daily-note moment format, with a hard refusal on ambiguous numeric forms (5/6 returns nothing rather than guessing May 6 vs 5 June). That covers tiers 1 and 2 and needs no dependency. DEFER the prose layer behind a mention-density census, and do not add chrono-node for it: 60-100 KB gzipped against a 637 KB bundle is not free, and on the corpus it would serve it fires on one note.

**Why it is not redundant.** Produces structure, not scores, so the admission rule does not bind. But its justification was 'it is the only way to know two notes are about the same moment', and that justification only holds if notes mention moments.

**Kill experiment.** The prose layer's gate is a density census, run before any tagger code, and I already ran the proxy on real/: 20 of 465 notes contain any four-digit year, 1 contains an ISO date in its body, 1 contains any English or German month name. I inspected the year matches and they are citation years and physical quantities — '(1985)' Goldwasser-Micali-Rackoff, '(1977)' RSA, '1971' Cook-Levin, '1936' Turing, plus '2048 bits' and '$1930\,\text{C}$' which is coulombs, not a year. A tagger firing on that corpus would decide the RSA note is about 1977 and pair it with the Cook-Levin note about 1971. So the prose layer's real bar is a density census on a target vault: at least ~15% of notes must carry a day-or-better prose TIMEX that is not a citation year before the layer is worth writing. Then the researcher's original protocol (200 annotated candidates, extraction precision ≥0.90, value accuracy ≥0.85, per-rule deletion below 0.90) applies unchanged — it is well designed, it just cannot be executed on a corpus with n≈1.

**Most likely reason it fails.** For the shipped normaliser: almost none, because refusal is the default. For the deferred prose layer: it is a bottomless engineering pit whose measured yield on the only corpus available is one note, and the false positives it would generate (citation years, bit-lengths, physical constants) are exactly the kind that silently poison a downstream channel.

**Needs first.** None for the normaliser. The prose layer is prereq'd on its own density census.


### 3. [DEAD] Shared-time channel: TIMEX overlap weighted by date IDF

*moderate · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** n/a — does not survive.

**How.** n/a.

**Why it is not redundant.** It isn't, once you strip out the part that has no data. With the prose TIMEX layer deferred, the channel reduces to t_doc overlap, which the researcher himself named as the kill condition: 'a vault whose notes rarely mention dates in prose leaves only t_doc, at which point this collapses into an expensive recency boost and should be cut.' That is the measured state of the corpus. The one variant with real coverage — two notes both linked from the same dated daily note — is not a time signal at all: it is a shared neighbour, which RA already scores at 1/deg(x), and shared outgoing links, which structuralSignals already computes as the biblio term at W_BIBLIO 0.5. It would be the shared-tags channel again under a new name.

**Kill experiment.** The correlation gate the researcher specified (Spearman rho < 0.2 against content and against RA) is the right gate and would fail it. Not worth running: the day-bucket variant is RA restricted to dated hubs, so rho against RA is high by construction.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Dead for three compounding reasons. (1) No data: 3.9% of notes carry a filename date, 0% carry a frontmatter date, 1 note carries a prose date. (2) Double counting: sharedFrontmatter() at index-store.ts:3418 already exact-matches primitive frontmatter values, so on precisely the vaults where a t_doc term would have signal (every note stamped with created:), two notes written the same day ALREADY receive a structural boost. The channel would be re-adding a term the product ships. (3) The surviving high-coverage variant is bibliographic coupling through a dated hub, already in two existing signals. Confidence set to speculative per instruction.

**Needs first.** n/a


### 4. [CORRECTED] Time-decayed Resource Allocation — the proposed experiment returns zero by construction

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing new on screen; shared neighbours you touched recently would count for more.

**How.** The lambda=0-is-a-superset argument is sound and the change inside src/graph-signals.ts really is an afternoon. The measurement is broken in a way that would produce a confident false negative. On the benchmark vault t_x is drawn from two values one day apart, so 2^(lambda*(t_x - t_now)) is effectively a constant c across every shared neighbour, RA_lambda = c * RA, and since fusion z-normalises the channel across the candidate pool (zNormaliser in graph-signals.ts strips scale), the ranking is provably bit-identical for every lambda. The ablation over lambda in {0, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 1.0} would return five identical numbers and someone would write down 'lambda=0 wins, cut it'. Correct the mechanism too: t_x must come from a real t_doc, and per the census that means a daily-note vault or the file-recovery clock, not mtime.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not independent and does not claim to be — it is a reweighting of a shipped channel, justified by RecB's evidence rather than by novelty. That framing is honest and I would keep it.

**Kill experiment.** Add a hard precondition to the protocol: refuse to run unless the corpus has at least 100 notes whose t_doc spans more than 180 days from a tier-1 source. Then the researcher's design is right, including the part most people would skip — the time-split variant, where you hold out links appearing in the most recent 20% of notes rather than a uniform random 20%. Uniform masking systematically under-rewards recency, so reporting only the uniform number would hide a real effect. Keep the five-seed variance reporting and the +0.01 bar, but raise the baseline: the current best is 0.782 R@10 (CSLS + RA + PPR, lab report 2026-07-26), not the 0.745 the brief quotes.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Even with a proper corpus, the most likely outcome is that best lambda is at or near 0, because a personal vault is not a news stream and a 2019 note may be exactly as relevant as last week's. Cut it in an afternoon if so. The worse risk is the one above: running it on a flat-timestamp corpus and recording the resulting zero as evidence.

**Needs first.** A corpus with real t_doc spread. On the current benchmark it is unmeasurable.


### 5. [DEAD] Authoring-session channel from filesystem timestamps

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** n/a — the data was destroyed before the plugin ever sees it.

**How.** n/a. Superseded by the file-recovery revision timeline proposal below, which is the same idea over data that survives.

**Why it is not redundant.** Untestable, because the input is constant.

**Kill experiment.** The researcher set a hard coverage floor of 50% of notes with a non-degenerate timestamp after the machine-burst filter. Measured on the benchmark corpus: 0%. Both ctime and mtime for all 465 notes in real/ fall in two adjacent day buckets (333 on 2026-07-02, 132 on 2026-07-01) — a copy, not a writing history. Even the gate-first experiment (Spearman rho of S_sess against RA, billed as one hour) cannot run: there is one session containing the entire vault, so S_sess is constant and rho is undefined.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Dead because the failure is not noise, it is erasure, and there is no recovery path from erased data. mtime is rewritten in bulk by Obsidian Sync (this vault's core-plugins.json has sync: true), by iCloud (the user's own active vault, per project notes), by git checkout and by any vault migration; ctime does not survive a copy or a restore at all. Kleinberg would correctly flag the burst and then have nothing left to score. Confidence set to speculative per instruction.

**Needs first.** n/a


### 6. [CORRECTED] Episodes — re-founded on the daily-note spine, with a tractable surprise statistic

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** Under the related notes, one card: 'This belongs to a run of nine days between 3 and 21 March 2024 about <label>. Three of the notes you touched in that run you have never linked to what you are reading.' Click through to see the run in order.

**How.** Best idea in the list, wrong substrate and an intractable statistic. Two fixes. (1) SUBSTRATE: 'notes sorted by t_doc' is unbuildable at 3.9% date coverage. Build the episode over the DAILY-NOTE STREAM instead — the sequence of dated daily notes, each represented by the centroid of its outbound link targets' note vectors plus its own body vector. Daily notes carry tier-1 dates by construction, so the timeline is exact, and their link targets are the undated concept notes the episode is actually about. An episode becomes a run of consecutive daily notes with cohesive subject matter, and its membership is the union of their link targets. That is computable today with vectors the index already holds. (2) STATISTIC: KL between Gaussians fitted to a running centroid and variance is not estimable — you are fitting a covariance from three or four vectors in 384 to 1024 dimensions, which is rank-deficient, and the KL between two such fits is numerically meaningless. The researcher flags this in the risk field and then specifies it anyway in the mechanism. Use the isotropic form: running mean plus a single scalar variance, KL between two isotropic Gaussians in closed form, with a shrinkage prior on the variance and a minimum-window guard. Also (3): 'idle-time work stored like the vault map' describes a pattern that does not exist — buildVaultMap() is called from map-view.ts:47 on view open and is never persisted. Episodes need either the same recompute-on-open treatment or a real slot in the index manifest.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a ranking channel; a second surface whose admission gate is temporal contiguity. Its value is exactly the pairs the content ranker suppresses — two notes from one week of work can be far apart in vector space and still obviously belong together to the person who wrote them.

**Kill experiment.** Keep all three parts; the null test is the best-designed experiment in the entire list and should not be dropped. (a) Objective proxy: restrict the held-out link set to pairs whose dates differ by under 30 days and compare an episode-only ranker against content-only on that subset; the signature of a real independent signal is that episodes win decisively there and lose everywhere else. (b) Blind judged precision, 30 episode cards, three-point scale, bar ≥0.7 rated 2 or 3. (c) Null test: shuffle the dates and re-run; if the judged score does not collapse, the feature is measuring content and adds nothing. Add a scale precondition: at least 60 daily notes spanning at least 6 months. The benchmark vault has 18 daily notes reaching 24 distinct link targets, so it cannot host this experiment — a target vault is a prerequisite, not a nice-to-have.

**Most likely reason it fails.** On a vault written in bursts about many things at once, episodes are temporally real but semantically incoherent and the c-TF-IDF labels read as noise; the cohesion floor handles this by emitting fewer episodes, which is the right failure mode. The re-founded version inherits a sharper risk: it is a daily-note feature and says nothing on a vault without a journaling habit. That should be stated in the UI, not hidden.

**Needs first.** The daily-note spine proposal below; reuses vault-map c-TF-IDF labels.


### 7. [CORRECTED] Dropped threads — rank by where the thinking went, not by how hard it stopped

*moderate · speculative confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** 'You had this going for five weeks in 2023, and here is where it went instead.' At most five threads, each showing its span, its last note, and the closest thing you have written since.

**How.** Keep the burst machinery, change the ranking objective and the framing. The researcher ranks candidates by (peak rate × burst duration × −log recency of last note), which optimises for the version of this feature that is unpleasant — the loudest, deadest, most abandoned thing wins. Rank instead by the STRENGTH OF THE SUCCESSOR: the cosine of the best note written after the burst ended against the thread centroid. The feature then only speaks when it has somewhere to point, and the card is 'your thinking moved here' rather than 'you stopped'. That also fixes the receipt problem — the successor note IS the receipt, so a thread with no good successor emits nothing instead of emitting an accusation. Use an exponentially-weighted arrival rate rather than Kleinberg; the researcher already concedes it is adequate and it is a tenth of the code.

**Why it is not redundant.** No existing channel can produce it — content similarity has no notion of 'stopped', the link graph has no notion of 'when'. Redundancy is not the risk; being uninteresting is.

**Kill experiment.** Promote the cheap prior kill test to phase one and stop there if it fails: count how many candidates clear the burst gate at all. On the benchmark vault the answer is zero — 18 daily notes spread over roughly five months cannot produce a burst-level-2 run of 3+ that then goes 180 days silent. Then the blind panel: 20 candidates against 20 controls drawn as random episodes matched on size and age, interleaved and unlabelled, 'would you want to be reminded of this?', candidates preferred at ≥2:1. Add one gate the researcher missed: the lab already measured a neighbouring surface and the verdict was that the useful hits are obvious siblings (resurfacing, judged useful 16/23 = 0.70 but non-obvious only 1/23). So the successor note must also clear a non-obviousness bar — it has to sit outside the thread's content top-10 — or this is resurfacing with a calendar attached.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Two ways to fail and one is worse than useless. It surfaces things you abandoned deliberately, which is actively unpleasant; the cap of five, the successor-strength ranking and opt-in are the mitigations. And on a small or young vault there are no bursts, in which case it must say nothing rather than lower its threshold. Speculative because it has never had a corpus that could test it.

**Needs first.** The daily-note spine and the episodes proposal.


### 8. [KEEP — and ship it first, it is a live bug] Temporal receipts on cards

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Each card gains one small line: 'written 4 days before this', 'same week', 'two years earlier' — and, where there is no trustworthy date, nothing at all. Two filter chips: 'same period' and 'written before this note'.

**How.** Keep the design as written; it is the highest value-per-line item in the list and the only one that ships without a coverage gate, because its behaviour on a dateless note is silence, which is constraint 5 rather than a compromise with it. One correction to how it is framed: this is not a nice-to-have on top of proposal 1, it is a CORRECTNESS FIX that can ship this week, before any t_doc work exists. view.ts:463 renders relativeTime(item.file.stat.mtime) as 'edited 3d ago' whenever settings.showRecency is on (and view.ts:392 does the same on recent cards). On the user's own iCloud vault, and on the lab vault where every one of 465 notes carries one of two sync timestamps, that line is uniformly false — it reports a sync event as an edit. Step one is deleting the mtime rendering, with silence as the honest interim state. Step two, later, is rendering a real delta with granularity-aware wording that never claims day precision from a month-granularity source, and rendering nothing when the source is tier 4 or 5.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel. It is constraint 5 extended from 'via <note>' to 'when', and it is what converts date resolution from invisible infrastructure into something the user can falsify in two seconds — which is the only thing that makes the rest of this area trustworthy enough to build on.

**Kill experiment.** Two things, neither of them a ranking metric. Honesty: on the 150-note hand-checked sample, the rendered phrase must be correct or absent in ≥98% of cases, where a wrong phrase is a failure and an absent one is not; the sample must over-represent the nasty case, a note whose date came from a date mentioned in its first paragraph rather than from its authorship. Cost: panel render time on a 100k-note vault, before and after, must stay inside the existing budget — cheap, since it is one subtraction per card. For step one specifically there is nothing to measure: removing a line that is provably wrong on a synced vault needs no experiment.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The subtle failure is a phrase that is technically true but misleading — 'written 2 years earlier' for a note whose date came from a date it mentions rather than when it was written. Source-aware wording and confidence gating are the fix, and the hand-check sample has to be deliberately stacked with those cases rather than randomly sampled.

**Needs first.** Step one: none. Step two: the note-time resolver.


### 9. [CORRECTED] Entity timelines — ship the strip, drop the boost outright

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** Open a note about a person, place or project and see a compact strip of the months you wrote about them, spikes marked, notes underneath.

**How.** Stronger than the researcher realised, for a reason he did not state: the mention series does not depend on the dates of the mentioning notes in general — it depends on the dates of the DAILY notes that mention the entity, and those are tier-1 reliable by construction. So this is the one temporal surface whose data exists in an ordinary vault. Two corrections. (1) Drop alias-text matching from v1. TitleIndex.compiledFor(path) gives one alternation regex per active note, so a vault-wide alias scan is a full-corpus regex pass — fine as idle work, never at query time on 100k notes — and the researcher's own ambiguity risk ('Mark', 'Bath') has no fix without a model we are not allowed to run. Ship resolved wikilinks only; alias matching is a v2 behind a surface-length and corpus-frequency gate. (2) Drop the ranking boost entirely rather than conditionally. The researcher says to drop it if the shared-time channel ships; that channel is dead, but the boost still fails on its own terms — 'candidates in the same burst bin as the active note' means co-mentioned by the same daily notes, which is a shared neighbour that RA already scores and shared outgoing links that the biblio term already scores. Shipping the strip as a pure view with no scoring path also keeps it out of the query-time budget, which already carries a pprFrom() walk (PPR_STEPS 3, frontier capped at 400) plus a graph.evidence() call per candidate on the main thread.

**Why it is not redundant.** As a view it does not compete with the channels at all. That is the point of dropping the boost: the moment it touches the ranker it has to clear the admission rule, and it cannot.

**Kill experiment.** One protocol, not two, since the boost is gone. Blind usefulness over 20 entity strips: 'does this tell you something you did not already have?', bar ≥0.6 yes. Report alongside it the coverage statistic that decides whether the strip is ever non-empty: fraction of linked notes with at least 3 dated inbound linkers. On the benchmark vault that is near zero — 18 daily notes reach 24 distinct targets out of 465 notes — so this needs a journaling vault to evaluate.

**Most likely reason it fails.** On a vault with no journaling habit every strip is empty or has two bars, which is worse than absent because it looks like a feature that is broken rather than one that has nothing to say. Gate the strip on a minimum number of dated mentions and hide it entirely below that.

**Needs first.** The daily-note spine proposal.


### 10. [DEAD] Superseded-statement detector

*expensive · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** n/a — no corpus, and no offline way to know two sentences make the same claim.

**How.** n/a.

**Why it is not redundant.** It would be genuinely independent if it worked. It does not.

**Kill experiment.** Already effectively run. The lab's 2026-07-02 report records that contradiction detection was seeded and not measured because 'the offline-tractable slice (same entity + numeric mismatch) has no natural instances in the current corpus'. Independently, the mechanism needs day-or-better prose TIMEX co-occurring with an anchor in the same sentence, and the corpus contains 1 note with any ISO body date and 1 with any month name. Expected candidate count is zero; the researcher's 'expect tens, not thousands' is off by at least an order of magnitude.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Dead on two independent grounds. The corpus has no instances — already established by a previous lab pass, not a new guess. And the core inference is unavailable: without an LLM there is no way to check that two sentences assert the same proposition, and a cosine floor cannot separate 'the meeting moved to September' from 'a different meeting is in September'. This is the proposal most likely to be quietly wrong in a way the user only discovers after trusting it once, which is the exact opposite of the precision-first commitment. The researcher's own honest expectation is that it fails the 0.80 bar; I agree and would not spend the expensive build to confirm it.

**Needs first.** n/a

### 12. [CORRECTED] Event-segmentation chunker — lexical surprise, not embedding surprise

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing visible. The boundaries between 'ideas' inside a note land where the thinking actually turns, so BiMax matches on cleaner units.

**How.** The motivation is good and this is the only proposal in the list with no date dependency, which makes it the second thing to build after the receipts fix. But the mechanism as written cannot be implemented. 'One extra pass over vectors that already exist' is false: assignIdeas (index-store.ts:689) runs at chunk-construction time, called at :1655 and :1764, both BEFORE embedBatch and before the chunk cap is applied. In whole-note/jina mode the idea IS the embed unit — chunkNoteWhole joins each idea's windows and embeds the joined text — so the vectors cannot exist before the boundaries are chosen; that is a hard circularity, not an ordering detail. In windowed mode you could do a second pass, but that means restructuring the pipeline to embed, re-segment, re-embed, and it gives up a property the code documents deliberately at :686 — ideas are pure text, 'deterministic and model-independent (ideas don't shift when the embedding model is swapped, which keeps an A/B between models clean)'. This project runs MiniLM-vs-jina comparisons constantly; that property is load-bearing. THE FIX: keep the surprise idea, change the distribution. Replace the Jaccard-valley statistic with a KL divergence between the content-word distribution of the current idea so far and the same distribution after absorbing the next window, with Dirichlet smoothing. That is Bayesian surprise over a lexical belief state — it is the statistic the cited result argues for, it is a genuine upgrade over a raw cohesion valley, it needs no vectors, it preserves model-independence, and it drops into the existing loop beside valleyThr. That version really is an afternoon.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel. It changes the input to the BiMax stage, so its entire effect shows up in the existing recall metric with no fusion and no correlation analysis — which is what makes it cheap to adjudicate.

**Kill experiment.** The named harness cannot measure this. bench/v3-integration.mjs synthesises chunk vectors as hashJitter(text, noteVector) — a deterministic hash perturbation of the cached whole-note vector — precisely so the chunk code paths are exercised without a real model. Any statistic computed over those vectors measures the jitter, and even for the lexical version the downstream BiMax scores would be dominated by it. Measure on a harness with real per-chunk embeddings (the jina cache path used by bench/levers-eval.mjs), old chunker vs new, five seeds with variance, through the shipped rank(). Keep the researcher's second metric, which most people would omit and which is the right instinct: report the idea-count distribution and index time alongside recall, because a rule that buys +0.01 by doubling the chunk count is paying for it in RAM on a 100k-note vault and has to be judged on both.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Likely a small effect either way. The atomic-note guard (n<=3 or under ATOMIC_NOTE_WORDS collapses to one idea) and the size rails already dominate boundary placement on short notes, which is most of a typical vault, so the new statistic can only matter on the long-note tail. Cost raised from cheap to moderate because 'cheap' assumed vectors that do not exist at that point in the pipeline; the corrected lexical version is genuinely cheap, but only because it abandons the embedding formulation the proposal was built around.

**Needs first.** None.


### 13. [DEAD] Point-algebra ordering of explicit before/after relations

*expensive · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**How.** n/a.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not the question — the question is whether the relations exist, and they do not.

**Kill experiment.** The researcher specified the right kill experiment (count the constraint edges, stop below ~200) and estimated it at an afternoon. The proxy is already answered: the corpus contains 1 note with an ISO body date and 1 with any English or German month name, and a constraint edge additionally requires a connective ('after [[X]]', 'nachdem', 'seit', 'bevor') co-occurring with a wikilink in the same sentence. The realistic edge count is single digits against a 200 bar — off by roughly two orders of magnitude, not by a tunable margin.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Dead on sparsity, exactly as its author predicted: 'TimeML-style density is a property of annotated news corpora, not of personal writing.' People write 'after the trip' far less often in notes than journalists do in newswire. ### 14. [DEAD] Time-respecting nomination walks

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** n/a.

**How.** n/a.

**Why it is not redundant.** The researcher already answers this honestly — 'probably not independent... marked as likely redundant' — and the arithmetic finishes the job.

**Kill experiment.** No experiment needed; the outcome is determined by the input. Edge time is approximated as max(t_doc(source), t_doc(target)). With tier-1 coverage at 3.9% and the fallback being a two-valued mtime, essentially every edge carries the same timestamp, so the non-decreasing constraint is satisfied by every step and the walk is bit-identical to today's. On the small dated subset the constraint does bind, and there it systematically prunes continuations — which trips the researcher's own kill condition ('kill immediately if nomination recall drops by any amount'). Both branches are failures.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Dead because the edge-time approximation has no data behind it and, where it does, it strangles the walk. That matters more than usual here: PPR's measured job is coverage of the 31% of held-out pairs that share zero common neighbours (lab report 2026-07-26), which is exactly the two- and three-hop region an ordering constraint prunes first. One salvage: the edge-time approximation itself is sound in principle and reusable — it just needs a clock, which is what the file-recovery proposal below would provide. Confidence set to speculative per instruction.

**Needs first.** n/a


### 15. [NEW] Daily-note spine: dates propagated along links from dated notes

*cheap · high confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** 'You first wrote about this on 3 March 2024' — with the daily note itself as the clickable receipt. Undated concept notes inherit a real, checkable date from the dated notes that mention them.

**How.** The tier the researcher's ladder is missing, and on a real vault it has more coverage than his tiers 2 and 3 combined. His five tiers all parse the note itself; this one is a graph operation. Let D be the notes with a tier-1 date — identified via app.internalPlugins.getPluginById('daily-notes').instance.options.{format, folder}, which gives the user's own convention instead of guessing regexes. Then for every note n, t_first(n) = min over d in D with an edge d->n of t_doc(d), t_last(n) = max, and mentions(n) = the full sorted series. Confidence is the count of distinct dated linkers. One pass over metadataCache.resolvedLinks, no parsing, no model, negligible cost, recomputes when links change exactly like GraphSignals.ensure() does today. The output is qualitatively better than a parsed date because it is self-verifying: the claim 'first mentioned 3 March 2024' comes with the note that mentions it, so the user checks it in one click. That is constraint 5 satisfied natively rather than bolted on.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel; it is the substrate that makes the episodes and entity-timeline surfaces buildable at all, and it is the only date source in an ordinary Obsidian vault that is both trustworthy and non-trivially covered. It also reframes the area correctly: a link FROM a dated note IS a timestamped edge, which is the primitive proposals 4, 5 and 14 all needed and none of them named.

**Kill experiment.** Coverage census first, same hour as the note-time census: fraction of notes with ≥1 dated inbound linker, and the distribution of dated-linker counts. I measured it on the benchmark vault and it is 24 of 465 (5.2%) from 18 daily notes against 2110 wikilinks vault-wide — so the benchmark cannot host this either, and a journaling vault is a prerequisite for the whole area. Then correctness, which is cheap and unusually clean: for the notes that DO carry their own tier-1 date, check that t_first from propagation agrees with or precedes it; disagreement in the wrong direction is a bug in the link resolution, not a modelling error. Bar: ≥0.95 non-contradiction.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Coverage is entirely a function of the user's journaling habit — a vault with no daily notes gets nothing, and there is no graceful degradation, only silence. Second risk: t_first is the first MENTION, not the first thought, and a retroactive daily note or an old note linked during a later cleanup will date something wrongly. The mitigation is that the receipt makes the error visible rather than hidden — the card names the daily note, so a wrong date is one click from being spotted.

**Needs first.** None. Should be built alongside the note-time census, not after it.


### 16. [NEW] Open-question resurfacing — the lab already measured this and nobody built it

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** While you are reading, one card: the open question you wrote eight months ago that this note answers — quoted verbatim, with its date and its note.

**How.** This is not speculation, it is an unexecuted instruction from the lab's own 2026-07-02 report. Resurfacing was measured at judged-useful 16/23 = 0.70 but non-obvious only 1/23, and the report's verdict was explicit: the one non-obvious win was 'a daily note resurfacing an old open question', and the recommendation was to re-scope to 'context-triggered open question / TODO resurfacing, narrow-scope'. It was never built. Mechanism, entirely pattern-scoped, no inference: index every unchecked task line ('- [ ]', already in Obsidian's cached listItems so no parsing) and every interrogative sentence (ends in '?', or opens with a small closed EN/DE interrogative set) as a pending item carrying its note, its date from the spine above, and the passage vector it already sits inside — no new embedding pass, these are existing chunks. At render time, if a pending item from a note older than T is close to the active note's content, emit at most one card. Two hard gates that make it precision-first for free: re-read the source line at render and drop it if the checkbox is now ticked, so a resolved task can never resurface; and quote verbatim, because the quote is the entire product. No 'is this still open' inference is required — a checkbox is ground truth.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not a ranking channel and does not touch rank(). It is orthogonal to both existing channels because its unit is a SENTENCE with an open state, not a note with a topic: the content channel cannot represent 'unresolved' and the link graph cannot represent 'still waiting'. It is also the only item in this area that already carries a positive measured usefulness number.

**Kill experiment.** Reuse the existing harness. bench/resurf-eval.mjs and the blind judging protocol in lab/results/resurf-judging-sample.json, restricted to question and unchecked-task items. Two bars, both taken from what the general version scored: judged usefulness must beat 0.70, and non-obviousness must clear a floor well above the 1/23 (4%) that got the unrestricted version rejected — call it 20%, since the narrowing is the entire hypothesis. Also report the fire rate: how often a card appears at all, since a surface that speaks on 2% of notes is fine and one that speaks on 60% is noise. Kill if the restricted version does not beat the unrestricted one on non-obviousness, because then the re-scope did nothing.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The honest worry is that the measured 0.70 came from a corpus where questions are Claude-generated and therefore unusually well formed; the authentic slice in that eval was n=2 and reported as anecdote. Second risk: an open question a user abandoned on purpose is as unwelcome as a dropped thread, so the same at-most-one-card discipline and opt-in apply. Third: date coverage limits the 'eight months ago' phrasing — without the spine it can still fire, it just cannot say when, and per the receipts proposal it should then say nothing about time rather than guess.

**Needs first.** None strictly. The daily-note spine improves the card copy but is not required to fire.


### 17. [NEW] File-recovery revision timeline: the clock that survives sync

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** The plugin knows roughly when you actually worked on a note, even on a synced vault where every file claims it was modified last Tuesday.

**How.** The reason the session and decay proposals died is that mtime was destroyed. There is a local, sync-immune replacement already sitting in the vault, and neither the researcher nor I reached for it first: Obsidian's core File Recovery plugin, enabled by default and confirmed on in this lab vault's .obsidian/core-plugins.json, writes periodic per-note snapshots into the renderer's IndexedDB. Each snapshot is a genuine local write event — it is created when the user types, not when a sync daemon touches a file — so the snapshot timestamps for a note are an authentic authoring trace. Reading them is plain IndexedDB from the renderer: no shelling out, no dependency, no extra storage (the data already exists), and desktop-only is already true (manifest.json isDesktopOnly: true). What this unlocks: a real per-note last-authored time for the receipts feature; a real t_x for time-decayed RA; and, most importantly, the authoring-session clustering that died with mtime — a session is a run of snapshot events across notes with gaps under g, and it is now computed from data that a sync event cannot forge. Step one is not code, it is verifying the store's actual schema and retention policy in a live vault, because both are undocumented internals and could change.

**Why it is not redundant.** The data is independent by construction — it records when you wrote, which neither the embedding nor the link graph observes. Whether the derived SESSION channel is independent of RA is the open question, and it is the same one the researcher correctly identified: you often link the notes you write together. That gate stays exactly as he specified it.

**Kill experiment.** Three steps, cheapest first. (1) Schema verification and coverage, an afternoon: open a real vault, enumerate the file-recovery store, report the retention window, how many notes have ≥2 snapshots, and the span covered. If retention is only a few weeks, this is a recent-activity clock and not a history, which is still enough for sessions and for the receipts line but not for decayed RA — say so and scope accordingly. (2) The researcher's gate, now runnable for the first time: Spearman rho of S_sess against RA over the candidate pool, computed BEFORE any fusion code; stop and report if rho > 0.5. (3) Only then the standard held-out-links protocol through rank(), channel stubbed vs applied, five seeds, against the current 0.782 baseline rather than the stale 0.745.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Two real ones. The IndexedDB schema is an undocumented internal — it can change between Obsidian releases and would break silently, so every read must be defensive and every failure must degrade to 'no time data' rather than to a wrong time. And retention is bounded: this is very likely a window of weeks or months, not the full life of the vault, which caps what it can support. A third, smaller: users can disable file recovery, so coverage is a per-vault property that has to be detected, not assumed.

**Needs first.** None. Independently useful, and it is the only path that resurrects the decay and session proposals.


### 18. [NEW] Reading trail: the session you are in right now

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** 'You came here from <note>, two notes ago.' The panel knows the path you took through the vault in this sitting and can point back along it.

**How.** The cheapest temporal signal in the product and the only session signal that survives sync, because it never touches the filesystem. app.workspace.getLastOpenFiles() is already called at src/view.ts:365 for the empty-panel fallback — it returns the persisted recently-opened path list, maintained by Obsidian itself. Two uses. (a) A breadcrumb receipt: when a ranked card is a note the user visited earlier in this trail, label it 'you were just here' instead of 'Similar text'. Zero cost, zero indexing, pure presentation, and it is a receipt in the strict sense — the user can verify it against their own memory in under a second, which is the two-second self-verification bar. (b) A trail-aware nomination, gated hard: the union of the last N visited notes' graph neighbourhoods as an additional nomination source into Stage 1b, alongside PPR. Not a score — nomination only, exactly the demotion PPR already received. The rationale is the measured one: 31% of held-out link pairs share zero common neighbours, so nomination coverage is the binding constraint, and the trail reaches candidates by a route neither content nor RA can take.

**Why it is not redundant.** The trail is behavioural, not textual and not structural: it is where attention went, which no shipped channel observes. Against RA the honest prior is moderate correlation — you often walk along links — but the walk also crosses the graph via search and the quick switcher, and those jumps are precisely the ones the link graph cannot see. As a receipt (use a) the question does not arise at all, since it changes no ordering.

**Kill experiment.** Split by use. (a) Receipt: nothing to measure beyond correctness — the label is either true of the session or not — plus a fire-rate report so it does not appear on every card. Ship it with the receipts work. (b) Nomination: the primary metric is nomination recall (does the true held-out target enter the candidate pool), measured before and after, with final recall@10 secondary, same discipline PPR was held to. This requires a protocol the lab does not have — held-out links do not come with the reading trail that preceded them — so it needs either an instrumented session log from a consenting user or a simulated trail (random walk from the source note) which is weak evidence and should be labelled as such. If a real trail log is not available, ship (a) and hold (b).

**Most likely reason it fails.** Use (a) is nearly risk-free; the worst case is a redundant label on a note the user obviously just left, which the fire-rate check catches. Use (b) has a genuine one: the trail is short, it resets, and on a fresh session it is empty, so the channel is intermittent by nature and a channel that fires on some ranks and not others is hard to reason about and harder to hold to a stable precision bar. Second: getLastOpenFiles has no timestamps, only order, so 'in this sitting' is an assumption — track opens in-memory with your own clock if the distinction matters.

**Needs first.** None for the receipt. Use (b) is blocked on a trail-aware evaluation protocol that does not currently exist.


## Knowledge-graph embeddings on a personal graph


<details><summary>12 techniques surveyed</summary>

- **Resource Allocation index** (established, 2009) — Local link-prediction score summing 1/deg(x) over shared neighbours x. Already the plugin's graph channel; every path-based proposal below is competing against this one-line baseline.  
  <sub>Zhou, Lu, Zhang, 'Predicting missing links via local information', Eur. Phys. J. B 71, 2009. Cited from my own knowledge, not re-verified online this session.</sub>
- **AnyBURL anytime bottom-up rule learning** (established, 2019) — Samples random paths from the graph, generalises them into cyclic/acyclic rules with constants, estimates confidence by grounding, aggregates candidate predictions by MAX confidence. Near-SOTA on standard KGC benchmarks in seconds of CPU, no GPU, no external libraries. The mined rule doubles as a human-readable receipt.  
  <sub>Meilicke, Chekol, Ruffinelli, Stuckenschmidt, 'Anytime Bottom-Up Rule Learning for Knowledge Graph Completion', IJCAI 2019. Cited from knowledge, not re-verified.</sub>
- **ComplEx-N3 (canonical tensor decomposition with nuclear-3 regularisation)** (established, 2018) — Complex-valued low-rank factorisation of the typed adjacency tensor with reciprocal relations and full-softmax loss; the strongest classical KGE family. Requires typed relations to be more than plain graph embedding.  
  <sub>Lacroix, Usunier, Obozinski, 'Canonical Tensor Decomposition for Knowledge Base Completion', ICML 2018. Cited from knowledge, not re-verified.</sub>
- **NodePiece anchor-based entity representation** (recent-but-replicated, 2022) — Represents each entity by its k nearest anchor nodes plus relational context instead of a private embedding row, giving inductive representations for unseen entities and a parameter budget independent of |E|.  
  <sub>Galkin, Denis, Wu, Hamilton, 'NodePiece: Compositional and Parameter-Efficient Representations of Large Knowledge Graphs', ICLR 2022. Cited from knowledge, not re-verified.</sub>
- **Poincare-ball hierarchy embedding** (established, 2017) — Embeds a partial order in hyperbolic space by Riemannian SGD so that norm encodes generality and distance encodes relatedness; a handful of dimensions capture deep hierarchies.  
  <sub>Nickel & Kiela, 'Poincare Embeddings for Learning Hierarchical Representations', NeurIPS 2017. Cited from knowledge, not re-verified.</sub>
- **Path Ranking Algorithm (PRA)** (established, 2010) — Enumerates relation-typed bounded-length paths, uses random-walk reachability along each path type as a feature, and fits a logistic regression over path types. Learns a weight per path type where RA hard-codes one.  
  <sub>Lao & Cohen, 'Relational retrieval using a combination of path-constrained random walks', Machine Learning 81(1), 2010. Cited from knowledge, not re-verified.</sub>
- **Simplicial closure / higher-order link prediction** (established, 2018) — Predicts whether an open triangle closes into a higher-order interaction; finds that simple means (harmonic/geometric) of pairwise tie strength are the strongest predictors and that local information dominates.  
  <sub>Benson, Abebe, Schaub, Jadbabaie, Kleinberg, 'Simplicial closure and higher-order link prediction', PNAS 115(48), 2018. Cited from knowledge, not re-verified.</sub>
- **Recurrency Baseline for temporal KG forecasting** (recent-but-replicated, 2024) — Parameter-free recency-weighted recurrency score over past co-occurrences; beats most neural temporal-KG models on several benchmarks. Two hyperparameters, no training.  
  <sub>Gastinger et al., 'History repeats itself: A Baseline for Temporal Knowledge Graph Forecasting' (arXiv 2404.16726, IJCAI 2024). Venue cited from knowledge with moderate confidence; verify before quoting.</sub>
- **ReFeX / RolX structural role extraction** (established, 2011) — Builds recursive neighbourhood-aggregated structural features per node, then factorises them into a small number of interpretable structural roles. The cheapest route to typing notes (person-note, MOC, leaf) without any NLP.  
  <sub>Henderson et al., ReFeX KDD 2011 and RolX KDD 2012. Cited from knowledge, not re-verified.</sub>
- **Degree bias in embedding-based KG completion** (recent-but-replicated, 2023) — Documents that low-degree entities receive systematically poor representations in KGE models. Directly relevant: median note degree in the lab vault is 5.  
  <sub>Shomer, Jin, Li, Tang, WWW 2023 (degree bias in KGC). Cited from knowledge; venue moderate confidence.</sub>
- **KGE training-protocol sensitivity** (established, 2020) — Shows that reported KGE differences are dominated by training hyperparameters rather than model class, so an untuned in-worker KGE is unlikely to reach published numbers.  
  <sub>Ruffinelli, Broscheit, Gemulla, 'You CAN Teach an Old Dog New Tricks! On Training Knowledge Graph Embeddings', ICLR 2020. Cited from knowledge, not re-verified.</sub>
- **Tie-corrected Spearman correlation (mid-rank assignment)** (established, n/a) — Standard statistics, listed because its absence is the single most consequential defect found. Rank correlation between two sparse channels must assign average ranks to tied values; assigning sequential ranks to a block of ties measures the sort's tie-break order, not the data.  
  <sub>Standard textbook statistics; verified empirically against the project's own bench/v4-channels.mjs this session.</sub>

</details>


### 1. FIRST, AND BLOCKING: repair the admission instrument (corrects proposal 12)

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. It changes which of everything else ships, and it invalidates two decisions already made.

**How.** I ran the project's own harness and found four independent defects, all reproduced on bench/v4-channels.mjs.
(a) TIE-BLIND SPEARMAN. spearman() assigns sequential ranks inside blocks of tied values. V8's sort is stable, so two mostly-zero channels get identical rank order inside their shared zero block and correlate spuriously. Two channels I generated to be provably independent and 2% dense score 0.916 under this estimator and 0.001 tie-corrected. 0.916 is exactly the number in the roadmap.
(b) THE REJECTED TAGS CHANNEL WAS A CONSTANT ZERO. bench tagsOf() calls n.body.match(/^---.../) on a body from which stripFront() has already removed the frontmatter, so it reads frontmatter tags from nothing. Measured: it finds tags on 1 of 495 notes (16 assignments); correct extraction finds 428 notes (1440). tagJaccard was identically 0 on all 4000 sampled pairs. Confirmation is already in the recorded results file: 'content + tags' 0.6642 equals 'content only' 0.6642 to four decimals, and 'content + graph + tags' equals 'content + graph' exactly. Correct extraction gives a true tie-corrected correlation with RA of 0.212, not 0.916.
(c) BIASED SHUFFLE. shuffled = [...edges].sort(() => rnd() - 0.5) is not a uniform permutation. Held-out edges have mean original index 609.8 against a uniform expectation of 663. Replacing it with Fisher-Yates moves content-only from 0.6752 and content+graph from 0.7561 to 0.7818 (15-seed means), i.e. the published 0.664/0.745 pair is one draw from a biased sampler at the low end of the true distribution.
(d) NO POWER. Single-seed unpaired recall@10 has sd 0.017 to 0.021 across seeds (range 0.749 to 0.809 for content+graph). Every kill criterion in this list is stated at 0.01 to 0.02, i.e. below the noise floor of the runs used to decide them. PAIRED deltas on shared splits have sd 0.003 to 0.011, so 3 to 5 paired seeds resolve a 0.02 effect; the fix is to report paired multi-seed deltas with a standard error, never a single-seed pair of numbers.
Deliverable: Fisher-Yates, mid-rank Spearman, paired multi-seed deltas with se and t, fixed tag extraction, plus the stratified reporting the original proposal 12 asked for (by source degree and by content-top-10 membership) and the forward split where timestamps allow.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is the instrument, and it has already produced at least one wrong verdict on the record. Nothing else on this list can be trusted until it is fixed.

**Kill experiment.** Self-validating and already partly done: re-run the current channel set under the repaired harness and restate the roadmap numbers. On the lab vault the corrected baselines are content-only 0.6752 +/- 0.0175 and content+RA 0.7818 +/- 0.0171 over 15 Fisher-Yates seeds. Ship the repair regardless of outcome.

**Most likely reason it fails.** None to the product. The cost is that several roadmap numbers must be publicly restated, and the forward-split half is still blocked by data: the lab vault has 3 distinct mtime days across all 528 notes and 24 date-named notes carrying 1.1% of links, so a git-backed donor vault is genuinely required for that half.

**Needs first.** None. Do this before anything else on the list.


### 2. SECOND: stop z-adding sparse channels — the combiner, not the channels, has been failing (new)

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing directly. It is the reason several rejected ideas were rejected, and it unblocks every future sparse signal.

**How.** index-store.ts:2628-2631 fuses by s.fused = zSem(content) + graphInfluence * zRa(ra). z-normalising a mostly-zero channel over a candidate pool hands a large positive z to the two or three candidates that fire and a uniform small NEGATIVE z to every other candidate, so a strong content match that happens to score zero on the new channel is pushed down. The penalty is larger than any effect these proposals are trying to detect. Measured over 15 paired splits, adding a channel by z-add versus by a fire-only bonus (add w * value/max only when the channel fires, never subtract): unlinked mentions -0.0357 (se 0.0023) by z-add versus +0.0005 fire-only; shared tags (idf) -0.0172 (se 0.0028) versus +0.0060 (se 0.0013, t=4.67) fire-only at w=1.0; block co-mention -0.0516 (se 0.0019) versus -0.0050 fire-only. The z-add penalty is 0.017 to 0.052 and is pure artefact. The product already has the right home for these: structuralSignals() adds W_SHARED_TAGS=0.6, W_BIBLIO=0.5, W_FRONTMATTER=0.3 into a monotone boost (raw * bMax), never a penalty. The bench evaluated tags in the fusion while the product boosts them in the structural term, so the bench's negative result never described the product. Fix: route every sparse channel through the monotone boost path, keep z-add only for dense channels (content, RA), and re-run the rejected candidates.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel at all. It is the arithmetic that decides whether any sparse channel can ever pass the admission rule, and right now the answer is no by construction.

**Kill experiment.** Already measured above on 15 paired Fisher-Yates splits. Next step is to confirm it on the real IndexStore rather than a reimplementation (per the project's own standing rule) and to re-run the previously rejected candidates through the corrected path. Shared tags at fire-only w=1.0 gives +0.0060 (t=4.67) over content+RA — small, but real and positive, where the recorded verdict was 'made things worse'.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The recovered effect is small in absolute terms (+0.006 recall@10 for tags). It is possible that after the fix no sparse channel clears a threshold worth shipping — but that would then be a real finding rather than an artefact. Second risk: a monotone-only combiner cannot express negative evidence, which is what an abstention gate needs, so this and the calibration work must be designed together.

**Needs first.** Proposal 1 (repaired harness) to measure it honestly.


### 3. Relation vocabulary manufactured from the vault itself (typed-edge layer) — CORRECTED, and it fails on the only vault we have

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing visible on its own.

**How.** Unchanged as designed: derive a relation label per link occurrence from frontmatter key, enclosing heading, syntactic form, and reciprocity. I measured every branch on the lab vault and three of the four are empty or contaminated. Frontmatter: 463 of 528 notes carry it, and the only keys present are tags (462), aliases (429) and 'related notes' (419); ZERO frontmatter lines contain a wikilink, so the frontmatter branch emits nothing. Dataview inline fields: 1 occurrence vault-wide, and it is a false positive. Transclusions: ZERO ![[ ]] embeds in the entire vault, so the form branch collapses to list-versus-prose. Headings: 58.2% of resolved link occurrences sit under NO heading at all, and only 34.1% sit under a top-32 heading — but the top link-bearing headings are 'orphan notes (63)' (126 links), 'suggested links (50)' (100), 'surprising connections (30)' (60), 'suggested tags (50)' (50), 'possibly duplicate or very similar (20)' (40), all of which are sections of the plugin's OWN generated report. Excluding those, the real link-bearing heading vocabulary is 'introduction' (202), 'einfuehrung' (42), 'notizen' (26), 'why it matters' (23): generic section names, not relation types. What survives is reciprocity, which is a genuine free bit (45.5% of directed link pairs are reciprocated), giving |R| of about 2 to 3, not 10 to 50.

**Why it is not redundant.** Still true as stated: graph-signals.ts:ensure() collapses to undirected adjacency and nothing in the plugin knows a link has a kind. The problem is not redundancy, it is that the vault does not contain the vocabulary.

**Kill experiment.** Corrected kill criterion. The original bar (KILL below 15% of links receiving a non-body type) passes at 34.1% only because plugin-generated report sections supply the mass. Restate it as: after excluding plugin-authored notes, KILL unless at least 15% of links receive a non-body type AND at least 3 distinct relations have support >= 30 links AND those relations are semantically distinguishable rather than generic section names. On the lab vault that bar fails today. Re-run on 2-3 donor vaults before writing any code; this is a one-afternoon scripting job with no plugin changes.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Measured, not hypothetical: on the only real vault available, |R| is effectively 2 (reciprocated / not). That single fact removes the headroom from proposals 3-typed, 7, 8, 10 and 14, which is most of the original list. Do not build the triple store on spec.

**Needs first.** None (metadataCache only), but donor vaults are required before this is worth any engineering time.


### 4. Phantom nodes and unlinked mentions: two panels, no ranking channel — CORRECTED and split

*cheap · high confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** Two small panels, both self-verifying in seconds. 'Six notes point at [[value function]], which does not exist' with the referrers listed and one click to create it pre-populated. And its mirror: 'you wrote "Erwartungswert" in this note without linking it' — the notes that mention an existing note by name or alias but never link it.

**How.** The panel half of the original proposal, kept; the RA half, cut. Corrected data: the original claimed 521 of 2503 wikilinks unresolved (21%) across 322 distinct targets. Applying Obsidian's actual basename/path-suffix resolution I measure 140 of 2503 unresolved (5.6%) across 88-89 distinct targets — the 21% figure came from not stripping path prefixes, and is inflated about fourfold. 13 phantoms have >= 3 distinct referrers, and they read as genuinely missing notes rather than typos: value function (8 referrers), javascript (6), reinforcement learning (5), transformer (4), language model (4), dijkstra algorithm (3), normal equation (3), quantenmechanik (3). The mirror panel needs no new machinery at all: title-index.ts already builds a surface-to-file map from basenames plus frontmatter aliases plus singular/plural variants, with explicit ambiguity handling, and already ships for the passive link glow. Scanning bodies with all [[...]] spans removed yields 1186 unambiguous surfaces, 1409 unlinked mention occurrences and 875 note pairs, 80% of which are not linked anywhere in the vault.

**Why it is not redundant.** resolvedLinks excludes phantoms by definition and unlinked mentions by definition, so the structural channel is blind to both. Neither is content similarity: both are exact surface-form co-reference, which is the one thing dense embeddings deliberately blur.

**Kill experiment.** Do NOT measure either on link recall — I already did, and both fail as ranking channels. Phantom-augmented RA over 15 paired splits: +0.0047 (se 0.0008, t=5.53) headline and +0.020 on the zero-shared-neighbour stratum from 3 links out of 150 — statistically real, four times below the proposal's own 0.02 bar, and rank correlation 0.993 with plain RA. Unlinked mentions as a channel: -0.0357 by z-add, +0.0005 fire-only, i.e. neutral at best. Measure the PANELS instead, by blind judged precision: sample 20 phantoms with >= 3 referrers and ask 'is this a note you would want to exist', KILL below 60%; sample 20 unlinked mentions and ask 'should this be a link', KILL below 70% (Obsidian's own unlinked-mentions pane is the precedent and the comparison).

**Most likely reason it fails.** Both panels are precision-critical surfaced claims under a silence-beats-wrong-answer policy. Phantoms need normalisation plus a >= 3 distinct-referrer floor or the panel becomes a list of the user's typos. Unlinked mentions need the existing ambiguity rule enforced strictly (a surface owned by two notes must be skipped) and a stopword/short-surface floor; at 4 characters minimum I still see plausible false positives.

**Needs first.** None. title-index.ts supplies the surface map for the mentions half.


### 5. AnyBURL-lite rule mining — CORRECTED to an untyped one-day spike, then probably dead

*moderate · speculative confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** A card that justifies itself in the user's own vocabulary: 'notes tagged #stochastik that link [[Erwartungswert]] also link [[Varianz]], 27 of 32 times. This one does not.'

**How.** Unchanged in outline (anytime path sampling, C/AC1/AC2 generalisation, Laplace-smoothed confidence, MAX aggregation) but resequenced. The typed version is blocked: proposal 3 above measures |R| at about 2 on the only real vault, so typed rules degenerate to untyped, and untyped cyclic length-2 rules ARE Resource Allocation with a learned weight — which the original proposal concedes. So build the untyped ablation FIRST, as a one-day spike, and treat it as the go/no-go. Training data is thinner than stated: after the 20% holdout the lab vault leaves 1062 training edges, not 1982.

**Why it is not redundant.** The independence, if any, lives in AC rules that pin specific constants (a specific MOC, a specific tag) and in length-3 paths. RA cannot express either. Cyclic length-2 rules will correlate with RA and that is expected, not a finding.

**Kill experiment.** Repaired harness only: paired multi-seed delta against content+RA with standard error, plus tie-corrected rank correlation against RA, plus the content-top-10-miss stratum. KILL if the paired delta is under +0.02 with the 15-seed se, or if tie-corrected correlation with RA exceeds 0.6 with no separate stratum gain. Note that the original proposal's correlation criterion would have been computed with the tie-blind estimator, which returns near-1 for any two sparse path-based channels; that criterion was unusable as written.

**Most likely reason it fails.** 1062 training edges with median note degree 5. Confidences estimated on <= 5 groundings are noise even with Laplace smoothing. The AnyBURL paper's own observation that longer learning slightly hurts on redundancy-heavy graphs is a direct warning for a vault whose largest node is a generated report. Most likely outcome: the untyped spike reproduces RA and the branch closes.

**Needs first.** Proposal 1 (harness). Typed variant additionally needs proposal 3, which currently fails.


### 6. Block-scoped co-mention and simplicial closure — DEAD as a ranking channel, kept only as a card

*moderate · speculative confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** Only the card survives: 'you have written about [[A]] with [[B]], and about [[B]] with [[C]], in the same breath, repeatedly — but never A with C.'

**How.** The substrate is real and I confirmed the researcher's counts almost exactly: 501 paragraphs contain >= 2 resolved links, yielding 11,270 distinct co-mention pairs against 1,537 linked pairs, 930 pairs seen >= 2x, and healthy block sizes (243 blocks with exactly two links). The channel is nonetheless dead. Measured over 15 paired Fisher-Yates splits with held-out pairs excluded from the co-mention counts so it cannot read the answer: block co-mention added to content+RA scores -0.0516 (se 0.0019, t=-27.6) by z-add and -0.0050 (se 0.0008, t=-6.16) under the corrected fire-only combiner. It is the one candidate I tested that is significantly negative even after the combiner is fixed, and it also loses 0.06 on the zero-shared-neighbour stratum it was supposed to win. Tie-corrected correlation with RA is 0.418 overall and 0.568 where both fire. Juxtaposition in a paragraph genuinely is a different act from relatedness — it just is not a link predictor.
