Instead, key the graph node on path+anchor when an anchor is present, using cachedMetadata.headings to validate it. A degree-200 uninformative node becomes roughly twenty degree-10 informative ones, and RA's existing 1/deg(x) term then does the work the hard cut is currently standing in for — no new constant, no percentile rule, and it degrades gracefully as the vault grows because section granularity scales with the MOC. The same treatment extends to block references ([[note^blockid]]), which are even more specific.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a new channel and it needs no independence claim — it is a refinement of the node set the existing RA runs over, so it composes with everything and competes with nothing. It is a strictly better-targeted version of what HUB_DEGREE approximates, and unlike proposal 4's percentile rule it removes the need for a size-dependent constant rather than replacing one constant with another.

**Kill experiment.** Held-out recall@10 with the anchor-aware node set against the current collapsed one, over >= 8 Fisher-Yates seeds, bar one seed SD. The specific subgroup that should move: pairs whose only shared neighbour is a note above the 95th degree percentile — i.e. the pairs the hub cut currently discards. Report how many nodes actually subdivide and the before/after degree distribution; if p99 degree barely drops, the vault does not use anchors and this is inert. Separately, judged-precision on the receipt: does 'via the Economy section of [[X]]' beat 'via [[X]]'.

**Most likely reason it fails.** MEASURED BLOCKER, and it is the same one that kills two other proposals: the lab vault contains ZERO heading-anchored wikilinks (grep for anchored link syntax returns nothing across 2503 total wikilinks). So this cannot be measured on the current corpus either, and its value depends entirely on whether real users write anchored links — a usage question nobody here has data on. Check anchor prevalence on a real vault before writing any code; if under a few percent of links are anchored, drop it. Second risk: subdividing inflates the node count and could fragment evidence, making two notes that link to different sections of the same MOC score zero where they previously scored something small — that is the intended behaviour, but it should be confirmed to help rather than hurt.

**Needs first.** Proposal 1, and a corpus with anchored links. Depends on nothing else.


### 16. NEW: Per-note calibrated silence — replace the single global minSimilarity with a null model

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Fewer confident-looking wrong cards on notes that have no real relatives, and fewer missing cards on notes in sparse corners of the vault. The panel goes quiet when it should, without the user touching a threshold slider.

**How.** Grounded in the shipped code. Fusion at index-store.ts:2628-2632 z-normalises content and RA across the candidate pool, so only ORDER is fused — a deliberate and good decision, because admission is then made on the ABSOLUTE cosine at line 2646 (`s.display < minSim`), which keeps the % pill meaningful. The gap is that minSimilarity is one global constant for an entire vault. The absolute cosine that means 'genuinely related' is not the same in a dense technical cluster as in a sparse personal corner, and it varies with note length and language — this is exactly the size-invariance objection proposal 4 raised about HUB_DEGREE, except here it is in the channel that decides what the user actually sees. Mechanism: at index time, for each note, sample 128 random other notes and store the mean and SD of its centred cosine against them — two floats per note, 800 KB at 100k notes, one 128xN dot-product pass that fits trivially in the existing idle indexing. Admission becomes 'top-1 is at least k SDs above THIS note's own null distribution' rather than 'top-1 > 0.35'. The existing minSimilarity slider maps onto k, so the setting survives with the same meaning. This is standard score calibration for link prediction, and it is the one place where the project's stated first principle — silence beats a wrong answer — is currently implemented by a single hand-set number.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel; it is the admission rule, so the correlation bar does not apply. It is also the natural consumer of the connected-component test that survived proposal 8: 'top-1 is only 0.4 SD above this note's null AND crosses a component boundary' is a much sharper silence condition than either signal alone, and neither is a ranking change.

**Kill experiment.** Link recall is the WRONG metric — a rule that withholds cards can only lower recall by construction, so measuring it that way guarantees a false negative. Use the blind judged-precision protocol, and report three things: precision@5 with and without calibration; the card-count delta (how many cards removed, how many admitted that the global floor excluded); and the diagnostic that decides whether it is real — does it remove more cards in dense regions and admit more in sparse ones, or does it merely shift the global threshold uniformly? If the per-note thresholds are nearly constant across the vault, it is a rename of minSimilarity and must be cut. Report the cross-vault spread of fitted per-note thresholds as the headline number.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The most likely failure is exactly that null distributions turn out near-uniform across a vault, making this an expensive way to rename a constant — the spread diagnostic above is cheap and should be run first, before any UI work. Second risk: 128 samples gives a noisy per-note SD for short notes, which could make the threshold jitter between indexing runs and produce cards that appear and disappear; that is worse than a slightly wrong constant, so the estimate needs smoothing toward the vault-wide mean for low-confidence notes. Third: it interacts with the structural rescue path at index-store.ts:2640-2646 (STRUCTURAL_FLOOR_RATIO), which also relaxes the floor, and the two relaxations must not compound.

**Needs first.** Proposal 1 for the error bar. Independent of every channel proposal — it can be built in parallel.


### 17. NEW: Rebuild the measurement corpus — it is the blocker behind four proposals and all of priority A

*moderate · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing directly. It is the precondition for evaluating people, places and time at all.

**How.** While checking the proposals above I accumulated a list of things the lab vault (494 indexed notes, 528 files) simply does not contain, each of which independently blocks something on the roadmap. ZERO external URLs across all 528 files — blocks the shared-external-reference channel entirely. ZERO heading-anchored wikilinks out of 2503 total — blocks hub subdivision. Every file mtime on one of two days (2026-07-01, 2026-07-02), no `created:` or `date:` frontmatter anywhere, and six notes in Daily/ — blocks the co-editing channel's ctime fallback and every other temporal signal. Max degree 37 against HUB_DEGREE=40 — means the hub logic has never executed in any published measurement. Only 38 of 530 held-out directed instances have a source of degree <= 1 — makes the orphan-note subgroup statistically empty. Taken together: the owner's stated priority A, people/places/time as first-class structure, has NO measurement instrument. Two routes, and they are not exclusive. (a) Consented real vaults: the highest-value asset the project could acquire, since the existing corpus manifest already separates real/ from generated notes and the protocol is designed to run per-vault. Three vaults spanning ~500, ~5,000 and ~50,000 notes would also unblock proposal 4's size-invariance question. (b) A corpus extension that adds the missing structure to the existing vault: external links and embeds, anchored wikilinks, and plausible per-note timestamps with realistic session clustering. Route (b) is faster but has the obvious hazard that generated temporal structure will confirm whatever hypothesis generated it, so it can falsify a channel (zero coverage kills it) but should never be allowed to admit one.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel. It is the same category as proposals 1 and 2 — instrument work — and it gates more of the roadmap than any single channel does: the external-reference channel, the co-editing channel, hub subdivision, the hub constant, and the orphan-note distillation idea all terminate on 'cannot be measured here'.

**Kill experiment.** Self-validating, and cheap to specify: for each candidate corpus, publish the coverage table before running any channel — external-reference density, anchored-link fraction, timestamp spread, degree distribution with max and p99, component-size distribution, and the size of each of the three pre-registered subgroups from proposal 2. A corpus that reports zero on a dimension is disqualified from evaluating channels that depend on it, and that must be stated in the result rather than discovered later. Make this table a mandatory header on every bench run.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Consented real vaults are a privacy and logistics problem, not an engineering one, and may simply not be obtainable — the plugin's whole promise is that nothing leaves the machine, which makes 'send me your vault' an awkward ask. The mitigation is to ship the harness and have users run it locally and report only aggregate numbers, which fits the architecture but slows iteration a lot. The generated-corpus route risks measuring the generator instead of the vault, which is precisely how a shared-tags-style false positive gets admitted, so it must be constrained to falsification only.

**Needs first.** Proposal 1, so that whatever corpus arrives is evaluated with a correct split and a published error bar from the start.


## Higher-order structure: hypergraphs, motifs, topology


<details><summary>12 techniques surveyed</summary>

- **Resource Allocation index (the shipped baseline)** (established, 2009) — RA(s,t) = sum over shared neighbours x of 1/deg(x). The 1/deg term is what makes it beat raw shared-neighbour counting. This is what src/graph-signals.ts already implements and what every proposal here is measured against.  
  <sub>Zhou, Lu, Zhang, 'Predicting missing links via local information', European Physical Journal B 71:623 (2009). Verified from memory, high confidence.</sub>
- **Simplicial closure / ScHoLP higher-order link prediction** (established, 2018) — Predicts which open triples of nodes (all three pairs have co-occurred, but never all three in one hyperedge) will later appear together in a single hyperedge. The winning score was a harmonic mean of the three pairwise tie strengths, and local features beat long-range ones. This is the only technique in this area whose defining object is provably not expressible on the clique-expanded pairwise graph.  
  <sub>Benson, Abebe, Schaub, Jadbabaie, Kleinberg, 'Simplicial closure and higher-order link prediction', PNAS 115(48) (2018), arXiv:1802.06916. Verified from memory, high confidence.</sub>
- **Edge-dependent vertex weights in hypergraph random walks** (established, 2019) — Proves that a hypergraph random walk with edge-INdependent vertex weights is exactly equivalent to a walk on a weighted clique expansion, so only the edge-DEPENDENT variant can express anything new. Useful here mainly as a negative result: it tells you not to bother building the edge-independent version.  
  <sub>Chitra & Raphael, 'Random Walks on Hypergraphs with Edge-Dependent Vertex Weights', ICML 2019, arXiv:1905.08287. Verified from memory, high confidence.</sub>
- **Hypergraph modularity (all-or-nothing / generative)** (established, 2021) — Louvain-style community detection with a degree-corrected null model generalized over hyperedge sizes, so a hyperedge only counts as 'within a community' if all its members are. Combinatorial, no embeddings.  
  <sub>Chodrow, Veldt, Benson, 'Generative hypergraph clustering: from blockmodels to modularity', Science Advances 7 (2021), arXiv:2101.09611. Verified from memory, high confidence.</sub>
- **Citation Proximity Analysis (CPA)** (established, 2009) — Weights co-citation by how physically close the two citations sit in the citing document (same sentence > same paragraph > same section > same paper). Beat plain co-citation on relatedness. This is the source of the proximity ladder in proposal 2.  
  <sub>Gipp & Beel, 'Citation Proximity Analysis (CPA) — a new approach for identifying related work based on co-citation analysis', ISSI 2009. Verified from memory, high confidence.</sub>
- **Hypergraph motifs and MoCHy sampling** (recent-but-replicated, 2020) — Enumerates/samples the isomorphism classes of triples of connected hyperedges (h-motifs) to give a structural fingerprint per node or per hypergraph. MoCHy-A is the sampling approximation that makes it tractable.  
  <sub>Lee, Ko, Shin, 'Hypergraph motifs: concepts, algorithms, and discoveries', VLDB 2020, arXiv:2003.01853. Fairly confident from memory; verify the arXiv id before citing in a release note.</sub>
- **Burt's structural holes: constraint and effective size** (established, 1992) — Measures how much a node's contacts are themselves connected. Low constraint = the node brokers otherwise-separate groups. Closed form, one sparse product against the row-normalised adjacency.  
  <sub>Burt, 'Structural Holes: The Social Structure of Competition', Harvard University Press (1992). Verified from memory, high confidence. NOTE: I measured its degree correlation on this vault and it is disqualifying — see proposal 11.</sub>
- **Forman-Ricci curvature on networks** (established, 2016) — A closed-form combinatorial curvature per edge from degrees and triangle counts. Strongly negative curvature marks bridge edges. Much cheaper than Ollivier-Ricci, which needs an optimal-transport solve per edge.  
  <sub>Sreejith, Mohanraj, Jost, Saucan, Samal, 'Forman curvature for complex networks', J. Stat. Mech. (2016). Verified from memory, high confidence.</sub>
- **MinHash / banded LSH for set similarity** (established, 1997) — Sublinear retrieval of sets with high Jaccard overlap via permuted-hash signatures and banding. The substrate for the 'copying' proposal.  
  <sub>Broder, 'On the resemblance and containment of documents', SEQUENCES 1997. Verified from memory, high confidence.</sub>
- **Overlapness / hyperedge overlap statistics** (recent-but-replicated, 2021 or 2024, unconfirmed) — Structural statistics describing how much hyperedges in a hypergraph share members, proposed as discriminative descriptors of real hypergraphs.  
  <sub>COULD NOT VERIFY. The first researcher cited a '2024 hypergraph-mining survey'; my recollection points instead to Lee, Choe, Shin, 'How do hyperedges overlap?' (WWW 2021 / VLDBJ). My web-search budget was exhausted before I could confirm either. Treat the citation as unverified — the proposal that depends on it (4) I am correcting on measured grounds anyway.</sub>
- **Hyperedge-prediction 'irreducibility' finding** (frontier-unproven, 2020, unconfirmed) — Cited by the first researcher (WWW 2020) as showing that whether higher-order structure helps prediction is a property of the dataset, not a universal. This is the intellectual basis for proposal 13.  
  <sub>COULD NOT VERIFY — web-search budget exhausted. I could not match this to a specific WWW 2020 paper from memory. The claim it supports (dataset-dependence) is independently plausible and is measurable on our own vaults, so proposal 13 does not actually need this citation to stand.</sub>
- **Persistent homology H1 on concept networks for gap-finding** (frontier-unproven, 2026, unconfirmed) — Builds a filtration on a concept graph and reads 1-dimensional persistent homology classes (rings with no filled interior) as missing-concept indicators.  
  <sub>COULD NOT VERIFY. The first researcher cited 'the CHI 2026 concept-link work'. CHI 2026 falls at the very edge of my knowledge and my search budget was exhausted; I could not confirm this paper exists. Combined with the substrate error I measured (see proposal 10), an unverifiable citation is enough for me to refuse to build on it.</sub>

</details>


### 1. Hyperedge incidence index — keep, but demote it from prerequisite to optional

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing directly. It is plumbing. The important correction is that it is NOT the gateway it was presented as.

**How.** The build described is correct: read app.metadataCache.getFileCache(f).links (positions per link) plus .sections/.headings/.listItems, emit a hyperedge per nesting level with >=2 resolved links, keep adj/degree derived so nothing regresses. Three corrections. (1) MEMORY ARITHMETIC IS WRONG BUT IN OUR FAVOUR: the proposal assumes ~15 links/note. Measured on the 465-note protocol vault, mean distinct out-degree is 4.05, and the four levels together produce 6,306 hyperedge member-slots for 1,883 link edges (3.3x). At 100k notes that is ~1.2M slots, ~10 MB as two Int32Arrays — comfortable. (2) STORING ONLY THE TIGHTEST LEVEL PER (src,pair) SAVES ALMOST NOTHING: measured 5,939 vs 6,306 slots, a 6% saving. Do not bother with the optimisation; store all four levels flat. (3) THE UNBUDGETED PREREQUISITE THE PROPOSAL MISSED: bench/obsidian-stub.mjs builds its cache at line ~132 as `c = { headings, tags, frontmatter, links: [], embeds: [] }` — links is an EMPTY ARRAY, and there is no .sections and no .listItems. Every bench harness runs against this stub. So none of proposals 2, 4, 7, 8 or 12 can be measured at all until the stub is taught to emit positioned links and block structure. That is the real first task in this whole area, and it was invisible in the proposal.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel, makes no independence claim. But the dependency graph as drawn is wrong, and that matters more than the index itself: the two magic features (9, and new proposal N4) need only note-level co-occurrence, which app.metadataCache.resolvedLinks already gives for free. Proposal 1 is a prerequisite for 2, 4, 7, 8 and 12 — it is NOT a prerequisite for 5, 6, 9 or N4. Build order should be inverted so the magic ships without waiting on plumbing.

**Kill experiment.** Gate (1) cost is right as written: full build under 3x current graph-rebuild wall time, retained heap <40 MB at 100k synthetic notes. Gate (2) as written asks to prove the current per-link mask is biased against higher-order channels — I would not accept this as stated, because the direction is asserted, not derived. Masking s->t removes t from s's own hyperedges but does not touch the shared neighbour x whose hyperedges RA and closure actually read, so the bias may be near zero. Reformulate the gate as a two-sided question: run content+RA under both masks and REPORT the delta, with no prior on its sign. If the delta is under 0.005, drop the second mask entirely and stop paying for two protocols forever.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The stub work (above) is the real cost and it is bench-only, so it buys no user value on its own. Secondary: Obsidian's listItems/sections cache is less uniform than resolvedLinks on Dataview-rendered content; fall back to note-level and count the fallback rate, as the proposal already says.

**Needs first.** Extend bench/obsidian-stub.mjs to emit positioned links, sections, headings and listItems. Nothing else.


### 2. Co-mention proximity weighting of RA — right idea, the formula deletes a third of the evidence

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Results improve when a connection came from two links written side by side, and weaken when it came from two links that merely share a 200-item index note. Receipts get sharper: 'via the Reading log section of <note>'.

**How.** MEASURED, AND THE PROPOSED FORMULA FAILS ITS OWN GATE. I implemented RA_h exactly as specified — max over hyperedges e of x containing both s and t, of prox(level)/(|e|-1), divided by log(1+deg(x)) — over all 6,199 pairs with a non-hub shared neighbour on the protocol vault. Spearman(RA, RA_h) = 0.305. The proposal predicted 0.7-0.9 and said explicitly that a low value means 'the ladder is doing something other than refining RA'. It is: 2,259 of 6,199 pairs (36%) get RA_h = 0 while RA > 0. The cause is structural, not a tuning issue. The proximity ladder is only DEFINABLE on the co-citation orbit, where the shared neighbour x itself wrote both links and so there is a block containing both. For the coupling orbit (s->x, t->x) and the mixed orbits, no block anywhere contains both s and t, so prox is 0 by construction and a third of RA's evidence is silently deleted. CORRECTED FORMULA, MEASURED: apply the proximity ladder as a MULTIPLIER on the co-citation term only, and leave the other orbits on plain 1/deg — RA_h(s,t) = sum over shared x of [ 1/deg(x) * (isCocite(x,s,t) ? prox(x,s,t)/prox_note : 1) ]. Spearman with RA = 0.957, zero pairs dropped. That is a refinement, which is what the proposal wanted. Keep the ladder constants (1.0 / 0.6 / 0.3 / 0.12) fit once on the lab vault, not exposed as settings.

**Why it is not redundant.** Correctly disclaimed by the original as not-a-channel, and the disclaimer stands: it must be measured as a REPLACEMENT for RA under the existing fusion weight (src/index-store.ts computes s.fused = zSem(s.content) + graphInfluence * zRa(s.ra); this swaps what feeds zRa). The proximity signal does exist on this vault: of 3,873 note-level co-occurring pairs, only 21.6% also co-occur at line level, 32.1% at paragraph, 47.8% at section. The ladder has real contrast to work with.

**Kill experiment.** Held-out links, RA -> corrected RA_h with all else fixed, require recall@10 >= 0.745 + 0.010. Correlation gate now has a measured target: expect ~0.95, and treat anything under 0.85 as a sign the implementation drifted back toward the broken variant. DELETE THE THIRD GATE AS WRITTEN — 'report recall on pairs whose only shared neighbour has >60 links' has an EMPTY subpopulation: the maximum node degree on the 465-note protocol vault is 37. Replace with a split at the 95th percentile of the observed degree distribution, which on this vault is around degree 12.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The vault is prose-dominated: 863 of 1,214 link-bearing lines are prose, only 351 are list items. So the list-item rung of the ladder is thin and most of the discrimination will come from the paragraph and section rungs. If the win is real it will be small, and a 0.010 effect may not resolve on 465 notes — be prepared for an honest null.

**Needs first.** Proposal 1, and therefore the bench stub work.


### 3. Split RA into co-citation and bibliographic-coupling terms — measured, and it is the strongest item on the list

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Two different reasons a note can be related get weighted and labelled differently: 'both of these are cited by <note>' versus 'both of these cite <note>'.

**How.** Unchanged from the original — the adjacency is symmetrized in graph-signals.ts ensure() (link(from,to); link(to,from)), which merges two distinct orbits into one number. Compute RA_cocite(s,t) over x with x->s and x->t, RA_couple(s,t) over x with s->x and t->x, plus the mixed orbit, and fuse as separately z-normalised channels with independently fit weights. I am promoting this from the original's 'medium' to high confidence because I measured it and the result is emphatic.

**Why it is not redundant.** MEASURED: Spearman(RA_cocite, RA_couple) = -0.013 across 6,199 pairs on the protocol vault. The proposal's own kill gate was 'drop it if correlation exceeds 0.8'. It is essentially zero — these two terms rank pairs independently. Populations are all healthy: 3,940 pairs with nonzero co-citation, 3,244 with nonzero coupling, 3,193 mixed. The stated risk (that Obsidian links are reciprocal-in-spirit) is measurable and I measured it: 50.8% of directed edges are reciprocal — and it still does not collapse the distinction, because reciprocity at the edge level does not imply agreement at the shared-neighbour level. Spearman(RA, RA_cocite) = 0.322, so even the current single RA is not a proxy for either half.

**Kill experiment.** Held-out links, fit the two weights on a 50% split of masked links, evaluate on the other 50%. Keep the original's gates — kill if the fitted weight ratio lands within 20% of 1:1, or if recall@10 does not improve by >= 0.008. The correlation gate is already passed pre-emptively at -0.013, so record that number as the pre-registered baseline and do not re-litigate it. Report per-orbit fire rates alongside recall.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Splitting halves the evidence per term, which will hurt on sparse vaults — 18 of 465 notes here have zero resolved links and would be unaffected either way, but a vault of loose daily notes could see both terms go quiet. The z-normaliser already maps a flat channel to 0, so the failure mode is graceful. Genuine remaining risk: two more fitted weights is two more things overfit to one vault, which is exactly what proposal 13 exists to worry about.

**Needs first.** NONE. This needs only the directed resolvedLinks that graph-signals.ts already reads and then throws away. It does not need proposal 1. Build it first.


### 4. Hub definition from higher-order structure — the idea survives, the proposed statistic does not

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Index notes and MOCs stop poisoning results, while genuinely well-connected topic notes stop being wrongly silenced.

**How.** MEASURED, AND maxHyperedgeSize FAILS. On the protocol vault the five MOCs have max block-level hyperedge size 6-8, while ordinary Concept notes have 4-6. That is not separation, and the proposal's own instruction was 'If degree already separates them, drop this' — degree separates them at 28-37 versus 9-13. BUT a statistic the proposal did not consider separates them cleanly and in the opposite direction from the one assumed: BLOCK-LEVEL HYPEREDGE PARTICIPATION COUNT, i.e. how many line/paragraph/section hyperedges a note is a member of. Machine Learning MOC: node degree 37, block-hyperedge degree 2. Mathematik Uebersicht: 37 / 2. Programmierung MOC: 34 / 2. Theoretische Informatik MOC: 30 / 2. Statistik MOC: 28 / 2. Against topic notes: Neural Networks 22 / 31, Turing Machine 21 / 37, Gradient Descent 17 / 26. An index note has a high degree concentrated in one or two giant blocks it authored; a topic note has a lower degree spread across dozens of paragraphs elsewhere in the vault that mention it. The ratio degree/blockHyperedgeDegree is the discriminator, not max hyperedge size. Suppress a shared neighbour smoothly on that ratio, and keep the hard degree cutoff only as the PPR frontier performance guard.

**Why it is not redundant.** Still a correction to an existing heuristic, not a channel. The specific claim is now the measured one above and is directly falsifiable on another vault.

**Kill experiment.** Replace the AUC experiment's feature: hand-label 40 notes as index/topic/atomic and compare AUC of (a) node degree, (b) maxHyperedgeSize as originally proposed, (c) degree/blockHyperedgeDegree. On the evidence above I expect (c) near 1.0, (a) high but confounded, (b) near chance — and the honest outcome may be that (a) is good enough, in which case ship nothing. Then ablate on held-out links requiring recall@10 non-regression plus a reduction in top-1 concentration skew (the metric that moved 2.3 -> 3.6 when PPR entered the score). CRITICAL CAVEAT ON ALL OF THIS: see new proposal N2 — hub suppression currently never fires on this vault at all, so this ablation measures nothing until the threshold is fixed.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The separation above is measured on 8 notes at the top of the degree distribution, which is suggestive, not conclusive. It could also be an artifact of how the lab corpus was grown: only 124 of the 465 notes are the user's authentic export, the rest is user-directed generated growth, and a generator's habits about where it places links inside a note are exactly what a block-level statistic reads. This risk applies to EVERY proposal in this area and nobody flagged it — block-level link placement is the one signal most likely to be synthetic in a partly-synthetic corpus. Validate on the authentic 124-note subset separately before believing any block-level result.

**Needs first.** Proposal 1; new proposal N2 must land first or the ablation is vacuous.


### 5. Simplicial closure channel — real signal, but the population depends entirely on a definition the proposal left unspecified

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** Better ranking from a signal no existing channel can see: you connected A to B, and B to C, and A to C, but never put all three in the same place.

**How.** The ScHoLP recipe is right — score each open-triangle support x by the harmonic mean of the three proximity-weighted tie strengths, divided by log(1+deg(x)), summed over x, fused as a fourth z-normalised channel, strictly one hop with no Katz/PPR term. THE UNSPECIFIED CHOICE THAT DECIDES EVERYTHING: 'no single hyperedge contains all of s, t, x' means completely different things depending on which levels count as hyperedges. Measured on the protocol vault, open triangles by closure definition: line-level only 228; line+paragraph 331; line+paragraph+section 1,014; all four levels including note 1,181 — against 29,261 CLOSED triples. So 96% of triples that could co-occur already do, and the surviving open set is dominated by section- and note-level closure. Pin the definition to note-level closure and say so in the code, because that is the only level with a workable population — and note-level closure means 'these three were never in one note', which is a weaker claim than the mechanism's prose implies.

**Why it is not redundant.** The irreducibility argument is sound and is the best one in the whole set: clique expansion maps 'A, B, C in one bullet' and 'A-B, B-C, A-C written in three different notes months apart' to an identical pairwise graph, and closure is defined by exactly that distinction. I did not measure the closure/RA correlation directly (it needs the corrected tie-strength weighting from proposal 2, which needs the stub work), so the predicted 0.25-0.45 band stands as an unverified prediction — treat it as the live question, not a settled one.

**Kill experiment.** Keep both mask variants only if proposal 1's reformulated gate (2) shows the masks actually differ; otherwise one protocol. Gates: rank correlation with RA < 0.5, and correlation with the content channel near zero (a nonzero value means the tie-strength weighting is leaking topicality). REPLACE THE RECALL GATE: 'Delta recall@10 >= +0.015' on a mean metric is the wrong instrument for a channel that is silent on most queries, and the proposal half-admits this in its own risk field. Pre-register instead: fire rate (fraction of queries with >=1 open-triangle support), and conditional recall@10 on firing queries, requiring >= +0.04 conditional with no regression on the non-firing set. That is the precision-first framing the project actually uses.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The population is thinner than the proposal assumed at every level except note. If the corrected proposal 2 tie-strengths turn out to be mostly note-level (prox 0.12 for everything), the harmonic mean degenerates toward a constant and this becomes a re-weighted triangle count. Second: my 29,261-vs-1,181 measurement says this vault is already 96% simplicially closed, which is a low ceiling for a closure-based channel.

**Needs first.** Proposal 2's CORRECTED formula (the broken one would zero the tie strengths for 36% of pairs and silently gut this channel too). Proposal 1 only if closure is defined below note level.


### 6. Hyperedge copying / set completion — downgrade to speculative, it is a folder detector on this vault

*moderate · speculative confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** While you write a note linking to four things, the panel notices another note links to those same four plus two more, and offers the two you left out.

**How.** MEASURED AND IT LARGELY REDISCOVERS THE FILE EXPLORER. Implementing the stated filter (|L(s) intersect L(x)| >= 3 and Jaccard >= 0.35, over the 255 notes with >= 4 outlinks) yields 330 qualifying pairs on the protocol vault. Of those, 323 (98%) are notes in the SAME FOLDER. The proposal's anticipated failure mode was templates and daily notes; measured, that is not it — zero of the 330 touch the Daily folder, so the existing template de-crowding has nothing to exclude. The actual failure mode is that on a folder-organised vault, link-set overlap is a proxy for folder membership, and the user can already see folder membership in the sidebar. The stated tail value (pairs with ZERO shared neighbours) is where the idea would have to live, and I could not find evidence that tail is populated. Separately, the memory arithmetic in the proposal is internally inconsistent: it computes 128-permutation signatures at ~1 KB/note = 100 MB at 100k notes, then substitutes '~8 KB per 1000 notes' for band-hashing without deriving it. Those differ by ~1000x and the second number is unexplained; a builder cannot budget from this.

**Why it is not redundant.** The honest self-assessment in the original (global correlation 0.4-0.6 with RA, value in the tail not the mean) is probably right, and my folder measurement makes it worse: the tail is likely to be same-folder too.

**Kill experiment.** Before any MinHash code, run the 20-line kill test I ran: enumerate qualifying pairs and report what fraction are same-folder, and how many have zero shared neighbours after masking. On this vault that is 98% and (unmeasured) probably near zero. Ship only if the zero-shared-neighbour subpopulation is non-trivial AND beats content+RA on it by >= 0.05 with no overall regression, AND clears >= 0.7 blind judged precision at top-3. Three conjunctive gates on a signal whose cheapest precursor already looks like a folder listing is not a good bet.

**Most likely reason it fails.** DOWNGRADED TO SPECULATIVE ON MEASURED GROUNDS: 323 of 330 qualifying pairs are same-folder, so the feature's most likely shipped behaviour is to recommend siblings the user can already see, with confident-looking set-completion framing that makes the redundancy worse rather than better. Precision-first policy says silence beats a right-but-useless answer.

**Needs first.** None to run the kill test — it needs only resolvedLinks. Do not build past the kill test.


### 7. DEAD: edge-dependent hypergraph random walk

*expensive · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Would have made the structural ranker care why a note was linked, not just that it was.

**How.** The theory is correct (Chitra & Raphael: edge-independent walks are provably clique-expansion walks, so only edge-dependent is worth building) and the conclusion drawn from it is correct. The implementation claim is not. Three blockers, two of them fatal. (1) 'No new embedding work — the chunk containing a link block is already embedded' is FALSE. The NoteChunk interface in src/index-store.ts is { text, isTitle, heading?, embedText?, ideaId?, isMean? } — there are NO character offsets anywhere on a chunk. There is no way to map a link's position.start.offset to a chunk index. Adding offsets means changing the chunker, bumping INDEX_VERSION (currently 8), and forcing a full re-embed for every existing user, which on a large vault is hours. That cost is invisible in the proposal and dwarfs everything else in this area. (2) The latency budget is unreachable. DEQUANT_CACHE_FLOOR is 256 entries; the walk keeps a 400-node frontier over 3 hops. That is a guaranteed near-100% miss rate, and each miss dequantizes chunkCount x dims int8, mean-centers against the corpus centroid, subtracts the per-note template direction and renormalizes. 30 ms per query at 100k notes is not in reach without a separate coarse per-block vector cache, which is a second unbudgeted subsystem. (3) The correlation risk the proposal names is real — gamma is built from embeddings, so it pulls the structural channel toward the content channel and threatens the 0.075 correlation that justified the graph channel existing at all. THE SALVAGE, which is worth keeping: chunks already carry a `heading` breadcrumb, and Obsidian's cached headings carry positions. So a link can be mapped to its enclosing SECTION and matched to same-heading chunks with no offsets, no schema bump and no re-embed. Section granularity is also where the measured proximity contrast actually lives (47.8% of note-level pairs co-occur at section level, versus 21.6% at line level). If anyone wants to test edge-dependent weights, that is the version to prototype — but it should be scoped as an experiment against proposal 2, not as a replacement for PPR nomination.

**Why it is not redundant.** The theorem-backed non-redundancy claim is the best in the set and is why I am recording the salvage rather than deleting the entry. It does not rescue the proposal as specified.

**Kill experiment.** Not applicable as specified. If the section-granularity salvage is prototyped: nomination-only first, does candidate recall@200 beat plain PPR at equal cost, with a hard per-query latency budget measured against the existing 256-entry cache and no new caching subsystem permitted. Do not evaluate it as a score until nomination is proven.

**Most likely reason it fails.** DEAD AS SPECIFIED: it requires index schema migration and a forced full re-embed that the proposal does not acknowledge, and its query-time budget is contradicted by the shipped 256-entry dequant LRU. Even setting those aside, its own correlation gate is the likeliest outcome — gamma is embedding-derived and would collapse the content/structure independence that is the graph channel's entire justification.

**Needs first.** Would need: chunk character offsets (schema bump, full re-embed), a per-block vector cache, chunk-vector access from the ranking thread. The salvage needs none of these.


### 8. Recurring-hyperedge de-crowding — keep, but run the population check as a standalone kill test first

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Notes that always appear together in the same list stop being recommended to each other purely for that reason.

**How.** Unchanged in principle: find hyperedges whose member set recurs (same set, or Jaccard >= 0.8, across >= k distinct notes), estimate the top principal direction of the members' note vectors with the power iteration the vault map already has, subtract its projection scaled by how mechanical the recurrence is. The generalization over template groups is genuinely well-aimed — a hand-maintained 'Core reading' list recurring across 30 notes is not a template and is invisible to the current de-crowding, which keys on template structure. The mechanical-recurrence scaling from mtime is the right guard in principle but SEE THE MEASUREMENT NOTE: it cannot be calibrated on the lab vault.

**Why it is not redundant.** Correctly framed as a correction to the content channel driven by higher-order structure, not a channel. That framing is right and I have no quarrel with it.

**Kill experiment.** RUN THE SECOND GATE FIRST AND ALONE — the proposal buries it, and it is a two-hour script that can kill the whole thing: count how many recurring-hyperedge families exist that are NOT already template groups, and if the answer is under 3, stop. My prior after measuring this vault is that it fires: there are only 403 note-level hyperedges with mean size 4.59, and the 330 high-overlap note pairs are 98% same-folder, i.e. topical rather than mechanical, which is precisely the over-subtraction case rather than the population this feature needs. Only if >= 3 non-template families exist, proceed to the crowding metric (the one that moved 9.9 -> 0.7) requiring a further fall with held-out recall@10 regressing by no more than 0.005. SEPARATE BLOCKER: the mechanical-recurrence scaling divides occurrences by distinct authoring days from mtime, and mtimes on the lab vault span 26 days of corpus-generation time with 360 distinct values over 528 files, and zero notes carry frontmatter dates. That term cannot be calibrated here at all — it needs the owner's real vault or it must be dropped from the formula.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Over-subtraction, as the original says, and my folder measurement sharpens it: on this vault co-listing and topical relatedness are heavily confounded, so the guard term is doing all the work — and the guard term is the one part that cannot be calibrated on the available corpus. That combination is why I would not build past the population check.

**Needs first.** Proposal 1; existing de-crowding and power-iteration code; a vault with real mtimes for the guard term.


### 9. MAGIC: the triangle you never closed — keep, and build it FIRST because it needs no plumbing

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** A card: you have written about A and B together, and B and C together, and A and C together — but never all three at once. Three receipts, one per pair, each pointing at the note where that pair met. Verifiable in about two seconds because the user wrote all three notes.

**How.** Enumeration and scoring as specified — open triples where each pair co-occurs in some hyperedge but no hyperedge holds all three, ranked by harmonic mean of the three proximity-weighted tie strengths, gated by content diversity (not near-duplicates) and by the existing Surprising Connections surprise rule (at least one of the three outside the other two's content top-k), computed on idle, cached, at most 3 cards a week. THE CORRECTION THAT CHANGES THE PROJECT PLAN: this does not need proposal 1. Measured, the candidate pool only exists at note-level closure — 1,181 open triangles all-levels versus 1,014 at line+para+section, 331 at line+para, 228 at line only. Note-level co-occurrence is exactly app.metadataCache.resolvedLinks, which graph-signals.ts already reads. So the flagship magic feature can ship on existing data while the incidence index is still unwritten. ADD ONE GATE THE PROPOSAL MISSED: exclude triples where any of the three pairs is DIRECTLY linked — a direct link means the user already fused that pair and the card is not a discovery. Measured effect: 1,181 open triangles drop to 330 with no direct link among the three, and 131 of those span more than one folder. 131 pre-gate candidates on 465 notes, before diversity and surprise, is enough for 3 cards a week for a year but leaves no room for sloppy gating.

**Why it is not redundant.** The object ranked is a TRIPLE and the defining property is a negative fact about co-occurrence, so content similarity cannot produce this output at all and the pairwise link channel would score all three pairs as already-connected non-predictions. Against the shipped Surprising Connections feature the distinction is real and clean: that finds pairs never connected, this finds triples connected pairwise and never fused.

**Kill experiment.** Keep both protocols. (1) Structural: remove 20% of hyperedges of size >= 3, measure triple-recall@10 of the removed triples against a control ranking open triangles by the product of pairwise content similarity; ship at >= +0.05 over that control. (2) Blind judged on the real vault: 20 top-ranked triples against 20 random open triangles, judged on 'would writing a note about all three be worth doing?', target >= 0.5 yes-rate at top-5 with >= 3x separation from random, kill under 2x. Both are well-specified and I would run them as written. ADD: report the surviving count after each gate in sequence (1,181 -> 330 no-direct-link -> N after diversity -> N after surprise), because the fire rate is the number most likely to kill this and it is cheap to watch.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Fire rate after gating, and the two gates are doing all the work as the original says. My numbers make that concrete: the pool is 330 before content gating on a 465-note vault, so an aggressive diversity threshold could leave single digits. Second risk, unchanged and real: it competes for the same UI slot as Surprising Connections and may cannibalize rather than add — decide up front whether they share a slot or the panel gets a second one. Third, my own caveat: 29,261 of 30,442 candidate triples on this vault are already closed, so the phenomenon this feature reports is genuinely rare, which is good for precision and bad for frequency.

**Needs first.** NONE beyond what ships today — note-level co-occurrence from resolvedLinks, the existing content vectors for the diversity gate, and the existing Surprising Connections gating code. Proposal 1 is NOT required.


### 10. DEAD: persistent H1 over the cluster complex

*expensive · speculative confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** Would have said 'five of your topics form a ring with nothing in the centre' and named where a missing note would go.

**How.** The substrate the proposal builds on does not exist. It states 'run it on the 30-80 clusters the vault map already produces'. The vault map produces at most 16: src/vault-map.ts, suggestK(n) returns Math.max(3, Math.min(16, Math.round(Math.sqrt(n / 2)))). On the 465-note vault that is 15-16, and the clamp means it is still 16 on a 100,000-note vault. A filtration over 16 vertices is a handful of simplices, and 'five of your topics form a ring' where the vault has only 16 topics total is a statement about a quarter of everything the user has ever written — too coarse to point at a missing note. Raising k to reach the assumed 30-80 means retuning a shipped, measured component (purity 0.65 against folders, versus a 0.29 baseline) for the benefit of an unproven downstream feature, or standing up a second independent clustering, and neither cost appears in the proposal. On top of that I could not verify the CHI 2026 paper the construction is patterned on, and the proposal's own honest risk section concedes the TDA-on-text literature's successes are corpus characterization rather than actionable per-item output.

**Why it is not redundant.** The output object is a cycle and no pairwise score encodes it — that part is true, and it is the only reason this entry is not simply deleted. It does not survive the substrate error.

**Kill experiment.** If the owner wants closure on it, run ONLY the cheap gate the proposal itself puts second, as a throwaway script and before any other work: recompute H1 classes over 10 bootstrap resamples of the cluster assignment and check whether the top class is stable in >= 7 of 10. My expectation is that it fails, because k-means at k=16 with 3 restarts on 465 notes is not that stable and the proposal's own risk names cluster granularity as the dependency. Two days, and a clean negative. Do not run the plant-and-recover experiment before the stability gate passes.

**Most likely reason it fails.** DEAD ON SUBSTRATE: it assumes 30-80 clusters and the shipped vault map is hard-clamped to 16 at every vault size, so either the feature is far too coarse to be actionable or it forces a retune of a component already measured and shipped. Compounding: the anchor citation is unverifiable, H1 classes are hard to explain even when correct, and the proposal's own prior on TDA-for-text is bad. This is the one item where I would decline to build before an experiment, not after.

**Needs first.** Would need a second clustering at k=30-80 independent of the shipped vault map, plus a boundary-matrix reduction. Recommend neither.


### 11. DEAD: bridge notes and structural holes panel — it rediscovers node degree

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** Would have said 'these 8 notes are the only thing holding two halves of your vault together'.

**How.** MEASURED, AND ITS OWN KILL TEST FIRES. I computed Burt's constraint in closed form over the protocol vault's link graph for all 439 notes with degree >= 2. Spearman(constraint, degree) = -0.836. Applying the proposal's own degree >= 4 floor barely helps: n = 357, Spearman = -0.755. The ten lowest-constraint notes are led by Mathematik Uebersicht (c=0.050, degree 37), Machine Learning MOC (0.061, degree 37), Programmierung MOC (0.077, degree 34), Theoretische Informatik MOC (0.090, degree 30) and Statistik MOC (0.101, degree 28) — that is five of the vault's six highest-degree notes, and they are its MOCs. The panel as specified would output 'here are your MOCs', which the user's own file names already tell them. The proposal names this exact failure mode in its risk field and prescribes a degree-matched control as the kill test; I ran the cheaper precursor and it fires before the judged protocol is worth anyone's time. Forman-Ricci curvature per edge is a separate and defensible computation, but it rides on the same 'find the brokers' framing and would surface the same notes.

**Why it is not redundant.** It asks a question about the vault rather than about a pair, so redundancy with the ranking channels was never the relevant test — correct framing, and the relevant test (redundancy with node degree) is the one it fails.

**Kill experiment.** The measurement design was right and that is why this is a clean kill: run the degree-matched blind judged protocol only if a residualized metric first shows independent signal. THE SALVAGEABLE VERSION, which is a different proposal and unmeasured: regress constraint on log(degree) and rank notes by the RESIDUAL, so the panel surfaces notes that broker more than their degree predicts. That is a real quantity, it is cheap, and nobody has measured it. I am not proposing it as live because I have not tested it and it may simply be noise once the dominant term is removed.

**Most likely reason it fails.** DEAD AS SPECIFIED ON MEASURED GROUNDS: Spearman(constraint, degree) = -0.836 on the protocol vault, -0.755 with the proposal's own degree floor applied, and the low-constraint list is the MOC list. The feature would ship a restatement of node degree with topological vocabulary attached, which is worse than shipping nothing because the framing implies a discovery.

**Needs first.** None. The kill test costs 30 lines and I have already run it.


### 12. Hypergraph modularity — downgrade to plain Louvain, keep the disagreement output

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** The vault map gets a second, structural clustering, and the interesting output is where the two disagree: 'these notes read alike but you never group them', and 'you always group these but they are about different things'.

**How.** The disagreement output is the good half and I would keep it. The hypergraph machinery underneath is not earning its place on this data. Measured hyperedge sizes on the protocol vault: line-level mean 2.78 (max 7), paragraph 2.94 (max 8), section 4.07 (max 9), note 4.59 (max 36). Chodrow-Veldt-Benson all-or-nothing modularity with a size-generalized null term, applied to hyperedges of mean size 3 that are overwhelmingly of size 2 and 3, is arithmetically very close to degree-corrected graph Louvain — the proposal's own stated risk ('on vaults with few multi-link notes the hypergraph collapses to a near-graph') is confirmed by the size distribution rather than merely feared. CORRECTED PLAN: run ordinary degree-corrected Louvain on the existing adjacency, compute the confusion against the shipped spherical k-means partition, and surface the two extreme cells. If and only if the disagreement cells judge well does it become worth testing whether the hypergraph null model moves them.

**Why it is not redundant.** The shipped output is explicitly the DISAGREEMENT between two partitions, which by construction neither alone can produce. That argument holds regardless of which clustering algorithm produces the structural side, which is exactly why the expensive version should not be built first.

**Kill experiment.** Unchanged and well-specified: the structural partition need not beat content purity (0.65 vs 0.29 precedent), it must be non-degenerate (no component over 40% of notes, no more than 30% singletons) and land in an adjusted-Rand band of roughly 0.2-0.6 against the content partition — below 0.2 the cells are noise, above 0.6 it is redundant. Then judge the cells blind: 20 'unrealized grouping' pairs against 20 random content-similar pairs, target >= 0.6 versus <= 0.3. ADD ONE STEP: run all of this with plain Louvain first and record the ARI, then re-run with hypergraph modularity and report the ARI BETWEEN THE TWO STRUCTURAL PARTITIONS. If that exceeds ~0.85 the hypergraph version is cosmetic on this data and should be dropped permanently, which is a cheap and publishable negative.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Modularity's resolution limit will merge small genuine clusters and the resolution parameter becomes another constant fit on one vault — the original is right to want it fixed rather than exposed. Additional risk I would flag: the content partition it is compared against is k <= 16, so the confusion matrix is coarse on the content side no matter how fine the structural side gets, which caps how specific the disagreement cells can be.

**Needs first.** None for the Louvain version. Proposal 1 only for the hypergraph variant, which should not be built until the Louvain version judges well.


### 13. Adaptive graph-channel weight — keep the goal, cut it to one statistic

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** The plugin works about as well on a 600-note vault of loose daily notes as on a 40,000-note densely-linked Zettelkasten, instead of being tuned for one of them.

**How.** The plumbing is trivial and real: src/index-store.ts fuses with s.fused = zSem(s.content) + graphInfluence * zRa(s.ra), and graphInfluence is a single documented scalar ('Measured best around 1.0'). Making it a function of the vault is a small change at index-build time, recomputed never per query, shipped as fitted constants and not a setting — all correct. CUT THE PARAMETERISATION FROM THREE STATISTICS TO ONE. Three statistics fitted over 4-6 vaults is over-parameterized to the point of guaranteed overfit, and one of the three is nearly constant on real data anyway: higher-order transitivity on the protocol vault is 29,261 closed against 1,181 open, i.e. 96% closed, so it carries almost no variance to fit against. Use the single statistic with the most obvious mechanical link to how much the graph channel can possibly contribute — the fraction of notes with zero resolved links (18 of 465 here, 3.9%) — or equivalently mean out-degree (4.05 here), and interpolate between two fitted endpoints. One parameter, two anchor points, leave-one-vault-out.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel; it is the calibration layer, and the reason a channel worth +0.081 on one vault might be worth +0.02 or -0.01 on another. That reasoning stands on its own and does not need the WWW 2020 citation I was unable to verify.

**Kill experiment.** Unchanged in design and it is the right design: assemble 4-6 vaults spanning size and link density, leave-one-vault-out, fit on all but one, evaluate held-out recall@10 on the remainder. Ship only if the adaptive weight beats the fixed weight on >= 4 of 6 folds AND never loses more than 0.01 on any fold. With one parameter instead of three this is a fair test rather than a formality. If it cannot clear the bar, 'one fixed weight is fine' is a genuine and cheap result worth writing down.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The binding constraint is data, not code: this needs real vaults with different linking styles, and the lab corpus cannot supply them — 465 of its 528 notes are one corpus, of which only 124 are the user's authentic export and the rest is user-directed generated growth sharing the generator's linking habits. Generated corpora will not span the density range this proposal exists to handle, so leave-one-out over near-identical folds would produce a falsely confident fit. Acquire at least two genuinely foreign real vaults before running it, or do not run it.

**Needs first.** More than one genuinely independent evaluation vault. No code prerequisites.


### 14. NEW — Hyperedge-degree Resource Allocation: change the denominator, not the numerator

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** A shared note counts as evidence based on how many distinct places it gets mentioned, rather than how many links it has. A 40-link index note stops being treated as the same kind of evidence as a 40-link concept note.

**How.** Everything in this area proposed changing RA's NUMERATOR (proposal 2 adds proximity) and nobody proposed changing the denominator. RA is sum over shared x of 1/deg(x), where deg is node degree. The hypergraph-native quantity is 1/hyperedgeDegree(x) — the number of distinct BLOCK-level hyperedges (line/paragraph/section, excluding note-level) that x is a member of. It is a one-line swap against the same incidence index proposal 1 builds, with no new data structure. Mechanically it asks a different question: node degree asks how many links touch x, hyperedge degree asks in how many separate authoring moments x was invoked. An index note has enormous degree concentrated in one or two blocks it authored; a concept note has moderate degree spread across dozens of paragraphs elsewhere.

**Why it is not redundant.** MEASURED, and this is the striking number in my whole review. Spearman(RA, RA_hyperedgeDegree) = 0.037 over the same 6,199 pairs. That is LOWER than the 0.075 correlation between the link-graph channel and content — the number that justified the graph channel's existence under the admission rule. The two rank pairs almost independently. And it is interpretable rather than accidental: Machine Learning MOC has node degree 37 and block-hyperedge degree 2, while Turing Machine has node degree 21 and block-hyperedge degree 37 — the statistic inverts the ordering of exactly the notes where RA's hub penalty is supposed to act and currently does not (see N2).

**Kill experiment.** Held-out links, both as a REPLACEMENT for RA and as an ADDITIONAL z-normalised channel alongside it, since at 0.037 correlation the additive form is the one the admission rule actually favours. Gates: as a replacement, recall@10 >= 0.745 + 0.010; as an addition, Delta recall@10 >= +0.015 over content+RA with rank correlation against RA already pre-registered at 0.037 and against content required below 0.15. Also report top-1 concentration skew (the 2.3 -> 3.6 metric), because the mechanism story predicts this should IMPROVE skew, and if it worsens it the story is wrong. Sanity check first: confirm the 0.037 is not degeneracy by reporting the nonzero count and the value distribution — a near-constant channel would also look uncorrelated.

**Most likely reason it fails.** 0.037 is low enough to be suspicious. The honest alternative explanation is that block-hyperedge degree is dominated by how many OTHER notes happen to mention x in prose, which is close to in-degree, and a channel that is uncorrelated because it is noisy will fail the recall gate immediately — which is fine, it costs a day. Second and more serious: this is a block-level statistic measured on a corpus that is 73% user-directed generated growth, so where links sit inside a note may be a generator artifact. Validate on the authentic 124-note subset before believing it.

**Needs first.** Proposal 1 (block-level incidence), and the bench stub work.


### 15. NEW — The hub threshold is dead code: HUB_DEGREE=40 never fires on any vault we measure on

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing directly, but every claim the plugin makes about not letting index notes poison results is currently untested.

**How.** src/graph-signals.ts sets HUB_DEGREE = 40 and uses it in two places: evidence() skips a shared neighbour when deg > HUB_DEGREE, and pprFrom() refuses to expand the frontier through a node with more than HUB_DEGREE neighbours. Measured on the 465-note protocol vault, the maximum node degree is 37 and the count of notes with degree > 40 is ZERO. Both guards are unreachable. Every measurement in this area that involves hub suppression — including proposal 4's entire ablation and proposal 2's third gate, which asks for pairs whose only shared neighbour has more than 60 links and would select an empty set — is currently vacuous. The fix is to make the threshold relative: take the 98th percentile of the observed degree distribution at graph-build time with a floor, so the guard scales with the vault instead of sitting above it. On this vault that lands around degree 22-28 and would suppress precisely the five MOCs.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel. It is a correctness finding that gates the measurability of proposals 2, 4 and N1, and it changes shipped ranking behaviour on real vaults the moment it lands.

**Kill experiment.** Two parts. (1) Regression: switching from constant 40 to a percentile must not reduce held-out recall@10 by more than 0.005, and should improve top-1 concentration skew. Report the threshold each vault picks. (2) Coverage, which is the real point: report what fraction of notes the guard actually suppresses on 3+ vaults under the old and new rules. If the answer under the old rule is 0% everywhere, that number belongs in the architecture doc, because the comment block in graph-signals.ts currently reads as though hub suppression is a working counterweight to hubness and on this evidence it has never once run. Sequencing: this must land BEFORE proposal 4 or 4's ablation measures nothing.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Making the guard reachable will change results on every vault, and some of that change will be for the worse — a percentile threshold on a small or sparsely linked vault could suppress ordinary notes and starve the RA channel. The floor is the guard against that and it needs fitting. There is also a real chance the honest outcome is that 40 was empirically fine for large vaults and the finding is simply 'our test corpus is too small to exercise it', which is still worth knowing and cheap to establish.

**Needs first.** None. Twenty lines in src/graph-signals.ts.


### 16. NEW — Orbit-typed receipts: spend the co-citation/coupling split on the explanation, not the score

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Instead of 'via <note>', the card says why: 'both cited by Machine Learning MOC', or 'both link to Gradient Descent', or 'Backprop links this, and this links Backprop'. Same results, a receipt the user can check without opening anything.

**How.** src/index-store.ts currently sets the graph receipt as { kind: 'graph', detail: basenameOf(s.shared[0]) }, which renders as a bare note name. Proposal 3 computes the directed orbit of every shared neighbour anyway; keep that classification and attach it to the receipt whether or not the score split ships. Three labels cover the connected 3-node cases that matter: co-cited (x links both), coupled (both link x), and mixed/chain. No ranking change, no fusion change, no new fitted weight, no risk to recall.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel and not competing with one. Its value is that it converts a measured structural distinction into user-checkable evidence, which is the project's precision-first policy applied to explanation rather than to ranking. Its claim to independence is inherited: I measured Spearman(cocite, coupling) = -0.013, so the label is genuinely telling the user something the single RA number cannot.

**Kill experiment.** Not the link protocol — this cannot move recall by construction. Blind judged receipt-quality: show 30 cards with the bare receipt and 30 with the orbit-typed receipt, and measure the rate at which the judge correctly predicts, before opening anything, whether the suggestion is worth following. Ship at a >= 0.15 absolute improvement in that prediction rate. Second, cheaper gate: coverage — what fraction of graph-reasoned cards get a label that is not 'mixed'. Measured populations say this should be high (3,940 pairs with nonzero co-citation, 3,244 coupling), but confirm on the shipped path, and fall back silently to the current bare receipt when the orbit is ambiguous.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The distinction may not mean anything to the user even though it means something to the ranker — 'both cited by X' and 'both link to X' could read as the same sentence to a non-technical reader, in which case the extra words are noise and the current receipt is better. That is exactly what the judged protocol is for, and it is a day of work to find out. Secondary: with 50.8% of directed edges reciprocal, some pairs will qualify as both co-cited and coupled and need a deterministic tie-break, or the label will look arbitrary across sessions.

**Needs first.** Proposal 3's orbit computation. No incidence index needed.


### 17. NEW — Temporal simplicial closure: rank the open triangles by how far apart you wrote them

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** Of all the triples you connected pairwise but never fused, the card surfaces the ones where the three pairs were written months apart — 'you connected these in March, these in July, and these last week, and never all three'. A triple assembled across a year is a real gap in your thinking; three notes written in one afternoon are just one afternoon.

**How.** The owner's priority A is people, places and TIME as first-class structure, and this is the natural place where time meets higher-order structure — nobody in the original list used time at all. For each open triangle from proposal 9, take the three defining hyperedges (the note where each pair met) and their authoring times, then score temporal dispersion as the spread of those three timestamps, and multiply it into the closure propensity. Timestamps come free from what Obsidian already exposes: file ctime/mtime, plus frontmatter dates when present, plus daily-note filename dates. This also fixes a real weakness in proposal 9 as written — its own stated risk is that the best triples are often 'three notes in one project', and temporal dispersion is precisely the term that demotes those, because a single project's notes were written together. It is a multiplier on an existing ranking, so it adds no new enumeration cost.

**Why it is not redundant.** Content similarity has no access to time at all. The link-graph channel has no access to time — graph-signals.ts reads resolvedLinks and nothing else. And the existing Surprising Connections rule is content-based. The quantity here (dispersion of the authoring moments of three separate co-occurrences) is not computable from any shipped channel, and it is specifically an antidote to proposal 9's most likely failure mode rather than an independent bid for the same slot.

**Kill experiment.** Blind judged, as an ABLATION on proposal 9 rather than a standalone feature, which keeps it cheap: take proposal 9's top 20 triples ranked with the temporal multiplier and its top 20 without, judge both blind on 'would writing a note about all three be worth doing?', and require the temporal arm to beat the non-temporal arm by >= 0.15 absolute. Also report what fraction of the non-temporal top 20 are same-project or same-folder triples versus the temporal arm — that is the mechanism story and it should show up directly. If proposal 9 fails its own gates this proposal dies with it and costs nothing.

**Most likely reason it fails.** HONEST MEASUREMENT BLOCKER, and it is the main reason this is not high confidence: it cannot be evaluated on the lab vault at all. File mtimes there span 26 days of corpus-generation time (1782941116 to 1785237322) with 360 distinct values across 528 files, and ZERO notes carry frontmatter dates. There is no authoring history to measure against. This needs the owner's real iCloud vault, which per the project's own notes is TCC-blocked from shell access, so the evaluation has to run inside the plugin rather than in bench/. Second risk, independent of measurement: mtime is a lie on any vault that has been synced, restored, or bulk-edited, so the score needs to degrade gracefully to the non-temporal ranking when timestamps look degenerate — detect that by checking whether the vault's mtime spread is implausibly narrow, exactly as the lab vault's is.

**Needs first.** Proposal 9. A vault with genuine authoring history.


## Graph diffusion, kernels and spectral methods


<details><summary>16 techniques surveyed</summary>

- **Resource Allocation index** (established, 2009) — Scores a pair by shared neighbours weighted 1/deg(x). Already shipped. My re-measurement says it is the strongest graph channel available on this vault and nothing in the kernel family beats it.  
  <sub>Zhou, Lu, Zhang, 'Predicting missing links via local information', Eur. Phys. J. B 71:623 (2009), arXiv:0901.0553</sub>
- **Adamic-Adar index** (established, 2003) — Same as RA with 1/log(deg) instead of 1/deg. Measured indistinguishable from RA here (0.796 vs 0.796, Spearman 1.000). Confirms the degree-damping shape, not the exact function, is what matters.  
  <sub>Adamic & Adar, 'Friends and neighbors on the Web', Social Networks 25(3):211-230 (2003)</sub>
- **Katz / von Neumann kernel** (established, 1953) — sum_k beta^k A^k, a damped path count. Measured 0.787 recall@10, Spearman 0.655 with RA — different enough to be a distinct ranking, not good enough to keep.  
  <sub>Katz, 'A new status index derived from sociometric analysis', Psychometrika 18:39-43 (1953)</sub>
- **Regularized commute-time kernel** (established, 2012) — K = (D - alpha*A)^-1. Fouss et al. found this the strongest kernel on link-prediction-shaped tasks. Measured here: 0.792 recall@10 (RA 0.796) and 0.269 on the discriminating subset (RA 0.379). The first researcher's 'honest candidate' prediction is falsified.  
  <sub>Fouss, Francoisse, Yen, Pirotte, Saerens, 'An experimental investigation of kernels on graphs for collaborative recommendation and semisupervised classification', Neural Networks 31:53-72 (2012); earlier RWR/commute-time treatment in Fouss et al., IEEE TKDE 19(3) (2007)</sub>
- **Commute-time degeneracy theorem** (established, 2014) — Proves C_uv -> 1/d_u + 1/d_v as the graph grows, i.e. commute time asymptotically IS a degree prior. Measured on the lab vault's largest component (378 notes, 70,067 unlinked pairs): Pearson r=0.611, R^2=0.374 — the asymptotic regime has NOT been reached at vault scale, so commute-time-like kernels are not dead a priori.  
  <sub>von Luxburg, Radl, Hein, 'Hitting and commute times in large random neighborhood graphs', JMLR 15:1751-1798 (2014)</sub>
- **SimRank** (established, 2002) — Recursive structural similarity: two nodes are similar if their neighbours are similar. Measured 0.784 recall@10, Spearman 0.629 with RA. Also the most expensive thing I ran (O(N^2 d^2) per iteration) for the worst return.  
  <sub>Jeh & Widom, 'SimRank: a measure of structural-context similarity', KDD 2002</sub>
- **Manifold ranking / diffusion on a k-reciprocal kNN graph** (established, 2003/2017) — Build a reciprocal kNN graph over embeddings with cos^3 weights, diffuse from the query. Standard in image retrieval. Measured here it makes ranking WORSE than plain cosine (0.660-0.668 vs 0.673) and gets 0.000-0.022 on the subset it claims to own.  
  <sub>Zhou, Weston, Gretton, Bousquet, Scholkopf, 'Ranking on data manifolds', NIPS 2003; Iscen, Tolias, Avrithis, Furon, Chum, 'Efficient diffusion on region manifolds', CVPR 2017</sub>
- **Fast Spectral Ranking** (established, 2018) — Precompute a rank-r eigenbasis so any polynomial diffusion becomes an r-dim dot product. Sound, and genuinely the way to scale diffusion — but there is nothing here worth scaling, since the winning channel is a two-nested-loop set intersection.  
  <sub>Iscen, Avrithis, Tolias, Furon, Chum, 'Fast Spectral Ranking for Similarity Search', CVPR 2018</sub>
- **hk-relax (local heat-kernel push)** (established, 2014) — Approximates exp(-t(I-P))e_s by a residual push queue with a degree-weighted 1-norm error bound, work bounded independent of |V|. The right algorithm if the current truncations ever bind. Measured: on the lab vault they never bind.  
  <sub>Kloster & Gleich, 'Heat kernel based community detection', KDD 2014</sub>
- **Graph Diffusion Convolution (GDC)** (established, 2019) — Two practical rules for any diffusion operator: sparsify each row (top-k or epsilon), then RE-NORMALIZE. The renormalize step is the part people skip and it is what stops over-smoothing.  
  <sub>Gasteiger (Klicpera), Weissenberger, Gunnemann, 'Diffusion Improves Graph Learning', NeurIPS 2019, arXiv:1911.05485</sub>
- **Balanced Forman curvature** (established, 2022) — Per-edge bottleneck score from degrees, triangles and 4-cycles only. Measured on the lab vault: 45.7% of edges negatively curved, but Pearson(BFC, 1/du+1/dv)=0.171 and Pearson(BFC, #triangles)=0.296 — genuinely independent of both degree and RA. The only construct in this document that measured as independent.  
  <sub>Topping, Di Giovanni, Chamberlain, Dong, Bronstein, 'Understanding over-squashing and bottlenecks on graphs via curvature', ICLR 2022, arXiv:2111.14522</sub>
- **GraphWave structural embeddings** (established, 2018) — Heat wavelets treated as distributions, summarised by their empirical characteristic function, give a role signature invariant to position in the graph. Anti-proximity by design.  
  <sub>Donnat, Zitnik, Hallac, Leskovec, 'Learning Structural Node Embeddings via Diffusion Wavelets', KDD 2018, arXiv:1710.10321</sub>
- **Non-backtracking (Hashimoto) operator** (established, 2013) — A walk on directed edges that forbids immediate reversal. On sparse graphs its spectrum separates communities where the adjacency spectrum fails, because it removes the degree echoes that dominate short walks. It is the one operator in this area that is NOT a polynomial in A, which is why it is worth one shot after everything polynomial failed.  
  <sub>Krzakala, Moore, Mossel, Neeman, Sly, Zdeborova, Zhang, 'Spectral redemption in clustering sparse networks', PNAS 110(52):20935-20940 (2013), arXiv:1306.5550</sub>
- **BSPM blur-then-sharpen** (recent-but-replicated, 2023) — Adds a high-pass correction after low-pass diffusion in collaborative filtering. Its regime is dense user-item matrices with hundreds of interactions per row.  
  <sub>Choi, Hong, Park, Cho, 'Blurring-Sharpening Process Models for Collaborative Filtering', SIGIR 2023, arXiv:2211.09324</sub>
- **Turbo-CF style training-free polynomial graph filtering** (frontier-unproven, 2024) — Hand-specified polynomial filter coefficients, no training and no eigendecomposition, competitive on sparse interaction graphs at sub-second cost. Cited by the first researcher as the existence proof for the filter primitive; I did not independently verify the venue and did not need to, since I measured the filter family directly.  
  <sub>Park et al., 'Turbo-CF: Matrix Decomposition-Free Graph Filtering for Fast Recommendation', SIGIR 2024 (venue not independently verified)</sub>
- **Autocovariance link prediction (as cited by the first researcher)** (frontier-unproven, 2024) — Score(x) = p_t[x] - d_x/vol, a t-step co-visit probability minus its stationary null. The claim that unsupervised AC beats recent GNN link predictors under unbiased evaluation is the load-bearing citation and I could NOT verify it. My own implementation is reported under proposal 1: on this vault the null subtraction changes almost nothing (AC 0.808 vs raw p_t 0.802) and AC ties RA within seed noise.  
  <sub>Cited without a specific reference by the first researcher; related unbiased-evaluation critique is Menand & Seshadhri, 'Link prediction using low-dimensional node embeddings: the measurement problem', PNAS 121(8) (2024)</sub>

</details>


### 1. P0 (ADDED, do this first). Fix the held-out shuffle, then re-baseline everything

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. It is the reason any number below can be trusted.

**How.** bench/v4-channels.mjs:66 and bench/graph-fusion-eval2.mjs:44 build the held-out split with `[...edges].sort(() => rnd() - 0.5)`. That is not a permutation: the comparator is non-transitive, so the result is whatever V8's TimSort does with it, which is to leave elements close to where they started. Replace with a seeded Fisher-Yates: `for (let i = sh.length-1; i>0; i--) { const j = Math.floor(rnd()*(i+1)); [sh[i],sh[j]]=[sh[j],sh[i]]; }`. Ten lines, three files. Then re-run every published comparison and rewrite ARCHITECTURE.md section 4b and the README table.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel. It is the precondition for the admission rule meaning anything: right now the rule is being applied to a non-uniform sample of the user's links.

**Kill experiment.** Already run. Bias: the held-out 20% draws 82/64/60/46/13 edges across the five quintiles of the edge list against a uniform expectation of 53 each. Effect on the headline, same seed, my faithful reimplementation of the harness: biased split gives content 0.664 -> content+RA 0.745 (reproducing the published figures to three decimals, which is how I know the reimplementation is faithful); Fisher-Yates gives content 0.674 -> content+RA 0.800. Across three Fisher-Yates seeds: content 0.674/0.687/0.658, content+RA 0.800/0.808/0.781. The correct baseline to beat is ~0.80, not 0.745, and the graph channel's real contribution is +0.126, not +0.081.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The direction of past comparisons is probably preserved, since every configuration shared the split — so this does not invalidate the RA-beats-PPR or CSLS-is-bad conclusions. What it does invalidate is every ABSOLUTE number, every kill threshold expressed against 0.745, and any subset analysis (the 'outside content top-50' splits especially), because those draw from a biased sample of links. Second risk: someone re-runs and the graph channel looks even better, and that gets read as the fix being 'wrong'.

**Needs first.** None. Blocks P12 and every measurement in this document.


### 2. P1. Autocovariance instead of the sqrt(deg) fudge — DEAD, measured

*cheap · speculative confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing would have changed. That is the finding.

**How.** I implemented it: symmetrized train graph, p_t = (D^-1 A)^t e_s by t sparse mat-vecs, Score(x) = p_t[x] - d_x/vol, fused by z-normalised rank against content, t in {2..6}. About 40 lines as predicted.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not. Measured Spearman(RA, AC_t) over the graph-reachable pool: 0.83 (t=2), 0.39 (t=3), 0.80 (t=4), 0.48 (t=5), 0.75 (t=6). At the t values that score best (t=4, t=6) the correlation is 0.75-0.80, above the proposal's own 0.7 kill line. The odd/even parity is the tell: at even t the walk lands on the same 2-hop set RA counts.

**Kill experiment.** Already run, three Fisher-Yates seeds. content+RA 0.800/0.808/0.781. Best content+AC_t 0.811/0.821/0.792 — a +0.011 mean gain that is smaller than the 0.020-0.027 spread between seeds, and that is the MAXIMUM over five correlated estimates, which is exactly how you manufacture a fake gain. Decisive: content+PT_t, the raw walk with NO null subtraction at all, scores 0.809/0.813/0.785. The entire mechanism of the proposal — the additive stationary correction — is worth 0.006 recall.
