**Why it is not redundant.** Infrastructure, no channel. It is the other half of the first-index budget: proposal 12 makes each forward pass faster, this one removes forward passes entirely.

**Kill experiment.** Two numbers first, both computable in an afternoon with no code shipped. (1) On the lab vault and the user's real vault, the fraction of chunk embed-inputs that are exact duplicates ACROSS notes — this is the template saving on first index. (2) On a real vault's git or file history, the fraction of a note's chunks unchanged between consecutive mtimes — this is the incremental saving. Then, if the numbers justify it: end-to-end incremental re-embed wall clock for a one-paragraph edit at 5k and 20k notes, and first-index wall clock on a template-heavy vault. KILL IT if the cross-note duplicate rate is under 5% AND the unchanged-chunk rate does not cut single-note re-embed time by at least 3x — one or the other has to carry it.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Hash collision returns the wrong vector for a chunk; at 128 bits over a million chunks this is not a real probability, and the blast radius is one mis-scored chunk rather than corruption, but it must be a wide hash and not a cheap 32-bit one. The real risk is that the second measurement disappoints: chunk boundaries are computed from the whole note (windowSentences uses one-sentence overlap, and assignIdeas places boundaries at lexical-cohesion valleys computed over the whole body), so editing one paragraph can SHIFT every downstream chunk's text by a sentence and invalidate all of them. If that is what the measurement shows, the fix is to make chunk boundaries locally stable — content-defined chunking with a rolling hash, the same trick deduplicating backup systems use — which is a bigger change and should be decided by that number, not assumed.

**Needs first.** None. Interacts with proposal 15's blob layout, so decide them together.


### 18. NEW — make the bench able to tell a real win from noise, before spending it on any of the above

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. This is what decides which of everything else is allowed to ship.

**How.** Almost every kill threshold on this list is inside the measurement error, and so are two decisions the project has already shipped. The lab vault has 236 held-out links (lab/results/graph-fusion-eval2.json: 488 notes, 1182 edges, 236 held out). The binomial standard error on recall@10 at 0.745 is about 0.028. Against that: 'improve by at least 0.02', 'delta <= 0', 'less than 0.01 recall' are all sub-noise; and PPR-as-a-score 'costs jina 0.026' and CSLS 'cost jina 0.030' — the two measurements that shaped the current architecture — are each about one standard error on a single fixed seed. Build: (a) paired bootstrap over held-out links (resample the 236 with replacement, recompute the DELTA between two rankers on the same resample, 10k iterations, report the 95% CI on the paired difference — the pairing is what makes this far tighter than comparing two independent intervals); (b) at least 10 holdout seeds, reporting seed-to-seed spread next to the mean; (c) per-slice deltas as first-class output, especially the non-obvious slice (30.7% of links, so about 72 held out, where the SE is nearer 0.05 and a slice claim needs a much larger effect to be real); (d) a checked-in channel register recording every candidate channel's rho against content and against link-RA, so the admission rule is read off a table rather than re-derived per proposal.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not a channel, it is the instrument. Its independence claim is that without it the admission rule is unenforceable: a rule that says 'ship if delta recall > 0' cannot be applied by a harness whose noise floor is 0.028.

**Kill experiment.** The meta-measurement, and it is the first deliverable: run the paired bootstrap on the comparisons ALREADY DECIDED and report which survive. Content vs content+RA is +0.081, roughly 3 SE, and must come out significant — if it does not, the harness is too small and the correct next task is enlarging the held-out corpus rather than building channels. Then +RA vs +RA+PPR (-0.026) and CSLS on/off (-0.030): my expectation is that neither is distinguishable from zero, which would mean two shipped design decisions currently rest on coin flips. Report that honestly whichever way it lands. GATE for adopting the harness: it must reproduce the existing headline numbers exactly on the fixed seed before anyone trusts its CIs.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The likely and unwelcome result is that the harness declares most of this roadmap unresolvable at 465 notes, and the real work becomes assembling a larger held-out corpus — unglamorous, slow, and exactly why it keeps not happening. There is also a specific way it could mislead: held-out wikilinks are not a random sample of 'good suggestions', they are the pairs the user happened to notice, so tightening the statistics on a biased target buys precision about the wrong quantity. The mitigation is to keep reporting the blind-judged protocols alongside, and to treat link recall as a screening metric rather than the arbiter.

**Needs first.** None. Lives entirely in bench/; the splitters already exist.


## Signals only a local tool can have


<details><summary>15 techniques surveyed</summary>

- **Kleinberg burst detection (two-state automaton)** (established, 2002) — Segments a timestamp stream into 'bursts' by fitting a two-state HMM over inter-arrival gaps, giving principled episode boundaries instead of a hand-picked gap threshold. Proposed for cutting the creation/edit stream into episodes. NOTE: on this project's only measurement corpus there is no usable timestamp stream to run it over (see proposals).  
  <sub>J. Kleinberg, 'Bursty and Hierarchical Structure in Streams', KDD 2002 / Data Mining and Knowledge Discovery 7(4), 2003</sub>
- **Item-to-item collaborative filtering (co-occurrence cosine)** (established, 2003) — Normalises a raw co-occurrence count into a similarity: co(a,b)/sqrt(co(a,a)*co(b,b)). The right normaliser for a co-episode channel; without it, frequently-touched notes co-occur with everything.  
  <sub>G. Linden, B. Smith, J. York, 'Amazon.com Recommendations: Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering', IEEE Internet Computing 7(1), 2003</sub>
- **Mylyn degree-of-interest (DOI)** (established, 2005) — Weights each artifact by a decayed sum of interaction events (select/edit), producing a task context that filters an IDE's views. The closest prior art to 'the panel should track what I am working on, not just the open file', and the closest to a shipped, evaluated version of it.  
  <sub>M. Kersten, G. Murphy, 'Mylar: a degree-of-interest model for IDEs', AOSD 2005; and 'Using task context to improve programmer productivity', FSE 2006</sub>
- **ACT-R base-level activation** (established, 1991) — B(n) = ln(sum_j (t_now - t_j)^-d), d≈0.5. Models need-probability as a power law of recency and frequency. Used here in inverted form: low activation + high relevance = a forgotten neighbour worth resurfacing.  
  <sub>J. Anderson, L. Schooler, 'Reflections of the Environment in Memory', Psychological Science 2(6), 1991</sub>
- **Team-draft interleaving** (established, 2008) — Interleaves two rankers into one shown list with per-round coin flips, so clicks attribute cleanly to a team. Reported at roughly 10-100x the data efficiency of A/B, which is what makes single-user evaluation conceivable at all.  
  <sub>F. Radlinski, M. Kurup, T. Joachims, 'How Does Clickthrough Data Reflect Retrieval Quality?', CIKM 2008; validated at scale in O. Chapelle et al., 'Large-Scale Validation and Analysis of Interleaved Search Evaluation', ACM TOIS 30(1), 2012</sub>
- **Replay / unbiased offline evaluation for bandits** (established, 2011) — Walk a log of randomised slates, keep only events where the new policy's pick matches the logged pick, average the reward. Unbiased if and only if the logging policy was randomised — which is why it is strictly downstream of interleaving.  
  <sub>L. Li, W. Chu, J. Langford, X. Wang, 'Unbiased Offline Evaluation of Contextual-Bandit-Based News Article Recommendation Algorithms', WSDM 2011</sub>
- **Unbiased learning-to-rank with IPS** (established, 2017) — Reweights click-derived training pairs by 1/p(examination at rank) to remove position bias. With a population of one, use a FIXED examination prior rather than an estimated propensity — an estimated propensity from a few hundred slates is noisier than the bias it corrects.  
  <sub>T. Joachims, A. Swaminathan, T. Schnabel, 'Unbiased Learning-to-Rank with Biased Feedback', WSDM 2017</sub>
- **Skip-above pairwise preferences from implicit feedback** (established, 2005) — Constructs training pairs from (clicked item, item ranked above it that was skipped). This is the exact construction proposal 7 needs, and the right citation for it — the original researcher named the construction without naming its source.  
  <sub>F. Radlinski, T. Joachims, 'Query Chains: Learning to Rank from Implicit Feedback', KDD 2005</sub>
- **Session-based kNN (V-SKNN) and STAN** (established, 2017) — Retrieve the k most similar past sessions to the current one, vote for their next items; STAN adds session-recency and within-session position decays. Repeatedly beats RNN/GRU4Rec baselines on session data — but was validated on millions of sessions, which is the reason it is parked here.  
  <sub>D. Jannach, M. Ludewig, 'When Recurrent Neural Networks Meet the Neighborhood for Session-Based Recommendation', RecSys 2017; D. Garg et al., 'Sequence and Time Aware Neighborhood for Session-based Recommendations: STAN', SIGIR 2019</sub>
- **EdgeBank — memorisation baseline for dynamic link prediction** (recent-but-replicated, 2022) — A parameter-free baseline that predicts edges seen recently. Competitive with temporal GNNs on standard benchmarks; also introduces harder 'historical' and 'inductive' negative sampling, showing that uniform random negatives are why memorisation looks strong. Both halves are directly applicable to this project's held-out-links harness and cost an afternoon each.  
  <sub>F. Poursafaei, S. Huang, K. Pelrine, R. Rabbany, 'Towards Better Evaluation for Dynamic Link Prediction', NeurIPS 2022 Datasets & Benchmarks (arXiv 2207.10128 — id from memory, verify before citing in a release note)</sub>
- **Evolutionary coupling / co-change mining (ROSE)** (established, 2004) — Mines version history for files that change together, surfacing coupling static analysis cannot see. The intellectual basis for a co-episode channel. Its input is a commit stream; this project has none (see the ctime/git finding).  
  <sub>T. Zimmermann, P. Weissgerber, S. Diehl, A. Zeller, 'Mining Version Histories to Guide Software Changes', ICSE 2004 / IEEE TSE 31(6), 2005</sub>
- **Remembrance Agent / just-in-time information retrieval** (established, 1996) — Continuously retrieves documents relevant to the text window around the caret, displayed in a non-interrupting margin. The reported finding was not efficiency but that users retrieved things they would never have thought to search for — which is the exact 'magic' criterion this project is chasing.  
  <sub>B. Rhodes, T. Starner, 'Remembrance Agent: A continuously running automated information retrieval system', PAAM 1996; B. Rhodes, P. Maes, 'Just-in-time information retrieval agents', IBM Systems Journal 39(3-4), 2000</sub>
- **Dwell time as a satisfaction signal / valid-read gating** (established, 2014) — Short dwells are noise; a length-normalised dwell threshold separates a real read from a bounce. Supplies the t*(c) = base + per-word form and the idea of logging fast bounce-backs as explicit negatives rather than absences.  
  <sub>X. Yi et al., 'Beyond Clicks: Dwell Time for Personalization', RecSys 2014; Y. Kim et al., 'Modeling Dwell Time to Predict Click-level Satisfaction', WSDM 2014</sub>
- **NIL clustering (entity linking to non-existent entities)** (established, 2011) — Groups mentions that refer to the same entity absent from the knowledge base. The formal analogue of Obsidian's unresolved wikilinks: several notes referring to a note that does not exist are a cluster with a name the user already chose. Grounds the ghost-link proposal.  
  <sub>TAC KBP Entity Linking task definitions (NIST TAC, 2011-2014); see H. Ji, R. Grishman et al., TAC KBP Entity Linking track overviews</sub>
- **Resource Allocation index** (established, 2009) — Already shipped in this plugin. Listed because the two additions below (ghost links) reuse it verbatim on a second edge type, which is what makes them a ~10-line change rather than a new subsystem.  
  <sub>T. Zhou, L. Lü, Y.-C. Zhang, 'Predicting missing links via local information', European Physical Journal B 71(4), 2009</sub>

</details>


### 1. MEASURED THIS SESSION — the finding that reorders everything below

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. It is why six of the thirteen proposals cannot be evaluated at all right now.

**How.** I ran four checks against /Users/justus/obsidian_atomized_intermediary/lab/vault, the 528-note corpus every number in this project is measured on. (1) File birth times: 364 files created 2026-07-02, 160 created 2026-07-01. TWO distinct dates, both the corpus-assembly import. (2) No .git — `ls lab/vault/.git` -> No such file or directory. (3) Date-named notes: 24 daily notes, holding 26 of 2552 wikilinks = 1.0% of link mass, at most 2 links each. (4) External URLs across the whole corpus: ZERO. Consequences, in order: proposal 1's ctime-burst bootstrap yields exactly 2 episodes of 364 and 160 notes, i.e. zero after its own import guard fires; proposal 1's git bootstrap has no input; proposal 1's daily-note bootstrap yields ~24 episodes of size <=2, about 24 note-pairs total; proposal 12's chronological split has no timeline to split on and no honest approximation; proposals 2, 3 and 9 inherit all of it. The researcher wrote 'vaults without git should skip the chronological split rather than approximate it' — that sentence applies to the only vault this project measures on, and they did not check.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a proposal. It is the load-bearing fact the proposal set was written without.

**Kill experiment.** Already run. Reproduce with: find lab/vault -name '*.md' -exec stat -f '%SB %Sm %N' -t '%Y-%m-%d' {} \; | awk '{print $1,$2}' | sort | uniq -c

**Most likely reason it fails.** None. The risk is ignoring it and spending a quarter building an episode channel whose evaluation harness cannot run.

**Needs first.** None.


### 2. CORRECTED (was 11) — Cursor-window continuous retrieval. Best ROI here, and it is 80% already shipped.

*cheap · high confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** While you write, the panel tracks the paragraph you are in rather than the file you are in. Type a sentence about an idea and the note that already covers it appears beside you.

**How.** The researcher costed this as 'moderate: build a debounced caret-window embed and rank path, then measure typing latency'. That path already exists and ships today. In src/link-suggester.ts: CONTEXT_RADIUS = 600 chars around the caret (line 54), CONTEXT_DEBOUNCE = 200 ms (line 52), a debounced `store.embedQuery(trimmed)` (line 161), and `store.rankForContext(vec, pool, activePath)` — which at src/index-store.ts:1114 ALREADY does content + Resource Allocation z-fusion over a bounded pool, with a written-down reason for bounding it (unbounded z-normalisation of a mostly-zero RA gives a handful of notes z ~ sqrt(n/k), about +10 on a 5k vault, against a content z spanning +/-3). The latency question the researcher wants measured has been answered in production for several releases: it runs on every `[[` keystroke. Remaining work is three things: (i) fire that same path on a debounce when the user is NOT typing `[[`, (ii) a display gate — top score above a floor AND margin over second-best above a calibrated delta, else show nothing, (iii) its own panel slot so it never displaces the file-level results. Suppress inside code fences and while the link-autocomplete popup is open, as proposed. ONE REAL CORRECTION: the proposal says 'rank against the existing int8 chunk index with one-sided max-sim'. rankForContext today ranks against note MEAN vectors only (`cosineSimilarity(vec, entry.meanVector)`), not chunks. Going to chunks means driving the int8->fp32 LRU expansion per candidate, and THAT is where the 100k-note latency risk actually lives. Ship the mean-vector version first for free; move to chunks only if precision at the gate misses 0.7.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not independent and would fail a correlation test outright — same content channel, different query. Kept for the reason the researcher gives, which is correct: file-level retrieval answers a question the user already asked by opening the file; caret-level retrieval answers one they have not finished asking.

**Kill experiment.** bench/resurf-eval.mjs implements exactly this protocol and I verified its parameters: MINW = 25 content words (line 25, enforced line 163), three query variants typed/context/before. Extend it with the display gate; report precision at threshold together with fire rate, and sweep the threshold for a precision-vs-fire-rate curve. Ship condition: precision >= 0.7 at fire rate >= 0.15, on the 'before' variant only — that is the realistic case. Because the mean-vector path is free, this measurement can start this week.

**Most likely reason it fails.** With a loose gate it is a distraction machine inside a writing tool, which is worse than being absent. That is a UI-calibration risk, not an engineering one. Secondary: the mean-vector version may simply be too coarse to hit 0.7 precision, at which point the cost reverts to moderate because the chunk path has to be built.

**Needs first.** None. The existing chunk index and the existing suggester context path are enough.


### 3. NEW — Ghost links: Resource Allocation over metadataCache.unresolvedLinks, and 'the note you keep asking for'

*cheap · high confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** A card that reads: 'Four notes point at [[value function]]. It does not exist. Create it?' And, on ordinary cards, a receipt the panel cannot currently produce: 'both of these reference [[value function]] — a note you have not written.'

**How.** src/graph-signals.ts builds its adjacency from `app.metadataCache.resolvedLinks` and nothing else (GraphSignals.ensure(), the line `const resolved = this.app.metadataCache.resolvedLinks ?? {}`). Obsidian also exposes `metadataCache.unresolvedLinks` — obsidian.d.ts:4444, identical shape Record<string, Record<string, number>> — and the plugin never touches it. Every entry is a wikilink the user typed to a note that does not exist: unmediated intent, in the user's own vocabulary, needing zero logging and zero waiting. Simplest implementation: fold ghost targets into the SAME adjacency as case-folded pseudo-nodes. `evidence()`, `pprFrom()`, the 1/deg weighting and the HUB_DEGREE = 40 cutoff then all apply unchanged (a ghost 50 notes reference is a topic, not evidence), and the receipt string in rank()'s `{ kind: 'graph', detail: basenameOf(s.shared[0]) }` already renders it. That is roughly a 10-line change. The magic surface is separate and stronger: rank ghost targets by (number of DISTINCT referring notes) x (mean pairwise content dissimilarity of the referrers, so a ghost referenced from three unrelated corners beats one referenced three times inside one project), and surface the top one. MEASURED THIS SESSION on the lab vault: 150 of 2503 wikilink targets (6.0%) are unresolved, across 96 distinct ghost targets, of which 22 are referenced by >=2 distinct notes — top hits 'value function' (8 notes), 'javascript' (6), 'reinforcement learning' (5), 'goa storyline ideas' (4).

**Why it is not redundant.** A ghost target is BY CONSTRUCTION absent from the content index — the note does not exist, so it has no text and no vector — and absent from resolvedLinks, so it is in neither existing channel's input. Two notes that both say [[Aetherfall magic system]] about a note nobody wrote share nothing an embedding or the resolved graph can see. It is also the only signal in this whole document that is fully available on install day, with no log, no waiting and no consent question.

**Kill experiment.** Split the two halves, because they measure differently. (a) The CHANNEL: run bench/v4-channels.mjs — which already computes held-out link recall@10 AND inter-channel rank correlation, so no new harness is needed — with ghost-RA added. Same admission bar that passed RA at 0.075 correlation and killed shared-tags at 0.916. My prior is that this FAILS on effect size: 22 multi-referrer ghosts give at most ~107 note-pairs out of ~139,000 possible, so recall@10 will barely move. Set the kill threshold at +0.01 and expect to hit it. (b) The CARD: blind-judged protocol, same shape as the Surprising Connections evaluation. Generate the top 10 ghost cards, vault owner rates 'is this a note I actually want to write', negative control = 10 ghosts sampled at random from the 96. Target >=0.7 on the top 10, near-zero on the control. On the lab vault there are 22 candidates, enough to run this today.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The channel half is probably too sparse to earn admission — 6% of links, 22 usable ghosts on a 528-note vault. Build the card first and treat the channel as a free by-product of the same adjacency change. Second risk: typos and case/alias variants inflate the ghost list ('quantenmechanik' may be a real note under a different name), so the card needs a fuzzy-match guard against existing titles and aliases before it claims a note is missing — src/title-index.ts already has the machinery. RELATED FINDING, reported so nobody proposes it: the obvious sibling idea, co-citation over shared external URLs, is unmeasurable here — the lab corpus contains ZERO external URLs, which likely reflects its part-generated provenance rather than the real vault.

**Needs first.** None.


### 4. NEW — Move the behavioural substrate from panel clicks to `[[`-suggester accept/skip slates

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. It decides whether proposals 5, 6 and 7 are buildable at all.

**How.** The entire evaluation programme (interleaving, replay, learned weights) is built on sidebar card clicks. That is the wrong event, and the plugin already contains a much better one. src/link-suggester.ts is an EditorSuggest that gives both halves in one file: `getSuggestions(context)` (line 172) produces a fully ordered slate of known composition, with per-candidate components already computed — `normFuzzy`, `r.sem`, and the fixed blend `FUZZY_WEIGHT * normFuzzy + SEMANTIC_WEIGHT * r.sem` — and it already routes through `store.rankForContext()`, so the graph channel's contribution is in there too. `selectSuggestion(item, evt)` (line 358) fires on accept. Log per popup: query LENGTH (not the string — privacy), slate size, per-candidate (path, rank, normFuzzy, sem, whether graph fusion moved it), and either the accepted rank or 'abandoned'. Everything ranked above the accepted item is a skip-above negative (Radlinski & Joachims, KDD 2005). An abandoned popup is a slate-level negative. THE ARGUMENT: the reward here is the product's actual goal, not a proxy for it. This plugin exists to cause [[links]]; a suggester accept IS a link insertion. A sidebar card click is a guess that a link might follow. And the event rate is plausibly 10-50x higher, because typing `[[` is a deliberate act that happens many times a day whereas opening a sidebar card is incidental.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel — the missing currency. It also dissolves the researcher's own sharpest objection to interleaving ('interleaving optimises for clicks, but this product's value is precision and silence, so a ranker that shows more plausible cards can win while getting worse'). On the suggester, the click IS the terminal action, so that failure mode does not exist and no post-click dwell gate is needed to patch it.

**Kill experiment.** Before building anything downstream, instrument ONLY a counter for one week and report accepted-slates/day and total-slates/day. Ship gate for the whole behavioural programme: >=20 accepted slates/day makes interleaving and replay viable within weeks. Below ~5/day, kill proposals 5, 6 and 7 outright. That is a one-week, near-zero-cost experiment that decides the fate of three moderate-cost proposals — run it before anything else in this document except the ghost-link card.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The suggester slate is dominated by fuzzy text matching once the user has typed two or three characters (FUZZY_WEIGHT carries 60% of the blend), so accepts may mostly reflect typing, not ranking, and carry little information about the semantic or graph channels. Mitigation: the empty-query case (`query.length === 0`, pure semantic ordering — line 181) is the clean one, so log it separately and treat it as the primary signal; it is a smaller subset but it is uncontaminated.

**Needs first.** None. Downstream of it: proposals 5, 6, 7.


### 5. CORRECTED (was 4) — The interaction log, with idle-corrected valid-read gating

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing visible except an opt-in switch, a plain-text log you can open, and a one-click wipe.

**How.** Keep the design: append-only JSONL, byte-capped ring buffer, events {t, type, noteId, extra}, valid-read gate t*(c) = 3s + 0.25s per 100 words capped at 60s, weight w = min(2, ln(1 + dwell/t*)), sub-3-second bounce-backs logged as explicit negatives. The mandatory idle truncation at 5 minutes is the best line in the whole document — keep it verbatim, it is what stops the laptop-lid problem producing multi-hour 'reads'. THREE CORRECTIONS. (1) Storage location is a real problem the proposal misses: `.obsidian/plugins/<id>/` is synced by Obsidian Sync and, on this user's actual setup, sits in iCloud. A behaviour log written there silently leaves the machine, which contradicts hard constraint 2 and contradicts the release note the proposal itself says to write. Either put it somewhere the user picks, or state plainly in the UI that it syncs. Decide before writing a line. (2) Dwell reconstruction: main.ts:390 already registers `workspace.on('active-leaf-change')`, which is reliable, but Obsidian gives no dependable blur/close event. The honest implementation is open-to-next-open gaps plus `document.visibilitychange` (available in Electron's renderer), not a close event. Say so in the design rather than discovering it. (3) Writes must ride the existing idle discipline — the plugin already debounces re-embedding at 20s (main.ts `debouncedUpdate`) — so logging never contends with indexing, and the cap must be in BYTES not events.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel — the substrate, and the precise answer to 'what does a local tool know that a cloud service cannot': dwell, edit bursts, and dismissals for one person's private notes.

**Kill experiment.** Validated, not benchmarked, as proposed. Instrument a week of real use, sample 100 opens, hand-label 'actually read / not', report precision and recall of the valid-read gate, tune thresholds on that curve. Keep the second check verbatim, it is a genuinely good diagnostic: the dwell histogram should be bimodal AFTER idle truncation and a smeared long tail before it. If truncation does not visibly change the shape, the idle detection is broken.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Privacy perception, as stated — a behaviour log inside a note-taking app is the most alarming thing this plugin could add. But the sharper risk is opportunity cost: this is a subsystem whose payoff is months away, and the three proposals it unlocks may all die at the volume check. Build the suggester-slate counter FIRST (one week, near-zero cost); build the full log only if that counter clears its bar.

**Needs first.** None.


### 6. CORRECTED (was 12) — Split it: ship the two baselines now, create the chronology for later, drop the chronological split

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. It is the referee, and it will make several current numbers go down.

**How.** Three changes were proposed; they have very different fates. (1) CHRONOLOGICAL SPLIT — dead as specified, see the measurement finding above: two file birth dates, no git, 1.0% of links in dated notes. There is no timeline and no honest approximation. What replaces it is cheap and worth doing purely so this becomes possible in a year: main.ts:474 already registers `metadataCache.on('changed')` and already calls `store.invalidateGraph()` there. Diff resolvedLinks in that handler and append (source, target, first_seen_ts) to a small link-birth table. That creates the chronology this project does not have, for a few lines, starting now. (2) EDGEBANK MEMORISATION BASELINE — available today, no chronology needed for the co-touch variant's simpler form ('the notes most recently linked-to from anywhere'). Add to bench/. An afternoon. (3) HARD/HISTORICAL NEGATIVES — sample negatives from pairs linked elsewhere in the vault rather than uniformly at random. Available today. An afternoon. Items 2 and 3 are the parts that actually change conclusions, and neither depends on item 1.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a player. Several other proposals here are measurable-but-inflated without items 2 and 3, and unmeasurable full stop without item 1's replacement.

**Kill experiment.** For items 2 and 3: re-run the shipped 3.0 configuration under the new baselines and publish both numbers side by side. Validation condition unchanged and correct — the ORDERING of known results must survive (content < content+RA; RA-as-nomination > PPR-as-score; shared-tags still redundant) while absolute numbers drop. If the ordering flips, the harness is measuring something else and must be debugged before it is trusted. bench/v4-channels.mjs is the right file to extend: it already computes both held-out recall@10 and inter-channel rank correlation.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The chronological third of this proposal is not merely hard, it is impossible on the available corpus, and approximating it from ctime would produce a fake timeline — worse than none, because every temporal proposal would then be 'validated' against an artefact of the July 2026 import. Stating that plainly is the whole value of this entry. Secondary risk, correctly identified by the researcher: publishing a lower number for a shipped feature takes nerve.

**Needs first.** None. Items 2 and 3 should land before proposals 1, 2 and 3 are evaluated.


### 7. NEW — Per-note calibrated display gate, from a score distribution the code already computes and throws away

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** The panel stops showing four mediocre cards on a hub note, and starts showing the one good card on a short atomic note that currently shows nothing. Same slider, but it now means 'is this an outlier for THIS note' rather than 'does it clear a vault-wide number'.

**How.** `minSimilarity` is a single global constant applied to every note in every vault with every model. But score distributions differ per vault (model, language mix), and differ per NOTE: a 3,000-word reference note's BiMax distribution against the vault is nothing like a 40-word atomic note's. A local tool holds the full null distribution and can calibrate against it — a cloud service ranking one query cannot. And rank() already materialises exactly that distribution and discards it: the Stage-1 loop builds `shortlist` as every entry above COARSE_FLOOR with its `coarse` score, then keeps only the top `width` and drops the rest. Compute mu and sigma (or the 95th/99th percentile) over that full array before slicing, and gate the final display on `max(minSimilarity, mu + k*sigma)` — i.e. require a card to be an outlier for this note. Zero new data, zero logging, zero waiting, no new model work, no index format change, and it makes the existing slider mean something stable across vaults and models.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not a channel and cannot fail the correlation test; it changes the SILENCE policy rather than the order. Its value is precision, which is the project's stated first principle and which recall@10 does not measure at all. It is also the cheapest thing in this document that serves 'silence beats a wrong answer' directly rather than by proxy.

**Kill experiment.** recall@10 is the wrong metric — this reduces recall by construction. Measure a precision/coverage curve, the same shape as proposal 11's gate: sweep k and report (i) fire rate = fraction of notes showing >=1 card, (ii) held-out link recall@10 AMONG notes that still fire, (iii) mean cards per firing note. The decisive diagnostic, one afternoon and worth running before building: dump the per-note distribution of Stage-1 coarse scores across the lab vault and check whether mu and sigma actually vary by note. If every note's distribution has essentially the same shape after mean-centering, the global constant was already right and this dies for the cost of one script.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Mean-centering (the corpus centroid subtraction already shipped) may have already flattened the per-note variation this depends on — that is exactly what the diagnostic tests, and it is a live possibility. Second risk: a per-note adaptive floor makes the panel's behaviour harder to explain to the user, and 'why did this note show nothing' becomes a support question. Mitigate by keeping the user's slider as a hard floor and letting calibration only ever raise it, never lower it.

**Needs first.** None.


### 8. NEW — Zero-logging session bootstrap: open-tab co-presence plus getLastOpenFiles()

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing directly. It is why the session-shaped features do not have to be silent for their first quarter.

**How.** Three free sources, one already wired in this codebase. (i) `app.workspace.getLastOpenFiles()` — obsidian.d.ts:8035, and ALREADY used at src/view.ts:367 to render the recent-notes fallback — returns Obsidian's own persisted recent-file list, surviving app restarts. Sampling it at each `active-leaf-change` (handler already registered, main.ts:390) and diffing successive snapshots reconstructs an open SEQUENCE without writing a single event to disk. It is short (~10 entries) and lossy, but it exists on install day. (ii) The set of notes currently open across tabs and splits (`workspace.getLeavesOfType('markdown')`) is a co-presence episode with a timestamp — free, and arguably not 'logging' at all, since it is ambient state rather than history. (iii) Obsidian persists the tab layout in .obsidian/workspace.json across sessions, giving the co-presence set a little history for free too. What this feeds: proposal 3's DOI restart vector on day one instead of 'after the log accumulates'; proposal 14's prefetch; and a thin but non-empty version of proposal 1's co-episode weights — which matters enormously given that proposal 1's three stated bootstrap sources are all empirically empty.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel. It is the answer to the objection that sinks half this document: every behaviour proposal here is silent for months and therefore undiscoverable and unmeasurable. This converts three of them from 'ships in a year' to 'testable this week', at the price of a much weaker signal — which is the right trade when the alternative is not knowing the effect size at all.

**Kill experiment.** It has no metric of its own; the test is whether anything downstream pays. Concretely: seed proposal 3's DOI restart vector from this instead of from a real log, replay ~200 real note-opens, and measure paired recall@10 of each opened note's held-out links, delta-restart versus DOI-restart, with a Wilcoxon signed-rank test over the pairs (paired design, so n=200 has real power for a genuine effect). If it does not move, that is also strong evidence about the FULL-log version's likely effect size — obtained a year early, for a few days of work.

**Most likely reason it fails.** getLastOpenFiles() is capped at roughly ten entries and has no timestamps, so 'sessions' reconstructed from it have order but not gaps — no way to tell a 30-second switch from a three-day one, which is exactly what the episode-cutting logic needs. It is therefore a genuinely weaker substrate, and a null result from it does not fully clear the full-log version. Say so when reporting.

**Needs first.** None.


### 9. CORRECTED (was 10) — Forgotten neighbour: promote the mtime version from fallback to primary

*cheap · high confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** At most one card per day, in its own slot: 'You have not touched this since March. It is the closest thing in your vault to what you are writing now.'

**How.** Keep the scoring: resurfacing score = rel(active,c) * (1 - Ahat(c))^gamma with gamma ~ 1, hard gates at >=60 days untouched, rel above the existing minSimilarity floor, at most one card per panel and per day, in its own labelled slot so it never displaces a normal result. THE CORRECTION IS TO INVERT THE PRIORITY. The researcher treats file mtime as a grudging fallback to the ACT-R activation term A(c) = ln(sum_j (t_now - t_j)^-0.5), and notes that mtime 'conflates not-read with not-written'. But the access-history version needs months of log before Ahat is even defined, and getLastOpenFiles() gives only ~10 entries, so the ACT-R version is not shippable for a quarter minimum. Meanwhile `file.stat.mtime` is on every note today and the panel ALREADY renders `relativeTime(file.stat.mtime)` on cards (src/view.ts:390). 'You have not TOUCHED this since March' is a true, checkable, receipt-bearing statement on install day, and it is a defensibly different claim — not a degraded one. Ship it, gate it on the judged protocol, and add the ACT-R refinement when the log has data. SECOND CORRECTION, verified: the researcher's note that this 'deliberately opposes the current structural boost, which nudges recent notes up' is right — recency is a component of `structuralSignals`. Separate slots is the correct answer, but the resurfacing slot must also be EXCLUDED from the recency component of the boost, or the feature competes against itself inside its own score.

**Why it is not redundant.** The one signal definitionally absent from both existing channels. Content similarity and Resource Allocation are symmetric functions of the vault's text and links and are entirely blind to when — or whether — the user last touched the note. No amount of embedding or graph work produces it.

**Kill experiment.** Two parts, and part (b) is the one that matters. (a) Interleave the panel with and without the resurfacing slot. (b) Direct judged protocol: for 30 fired cards, ask 'did you already know this note was relevant here?' and count the noes. Targets: >=40% 'no' at >=70% rated useful. THE KILL METRIC IS ITS OWN DISMISSAL RATE — above ~40% means the feature is mining a graveyard of consciously abandoned notes rather than forgotten ones. Because the mtime version ships on day one, (b) can run immediately; it does not need the interleave harness and does not need the log.

**Most likely reason it fails.** mtime is touched by things that are not authoring — bulk reformatting, a linter plugin, sync metadata rewrites, a find-and-replace across the vault. Any of those resets the clock on hundreds of notes at once and silently empties the candidate pool. Needs the same import guard proposal 1 needs: ignore mtime transitions where more than N notes changed within M seconds. Second risk, unchanged: it may mostly resurface things the user deliberately walked away from.

**Needs first.** Proposal 8's dismissal escape hatch should land alongside it. The ACT-R refinement needs the interaction log.


### 10. CORRECTED (was 8) — Dismissal memory: ship pair-level, add impression fatigue, cut the cluster generalisation

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** An x on each card. A dismissed suggestion does not come back for this note. And a card you have been shown fifteen times from the same note and never opened quietly stops appearing.

**How.** Keep the pair level exactly as proposed: store (source, target, t), hard-suppress 30 days, then reinstate with a penalty decaying exp(-dt/60d), cap the total penalty so no note becomes permanently unreachable, and expose a 'things you hid' list with one-click undo. TWO CORRECTIONS. (1) CUT the k-means-cluster half of the generalisation. Template groups are defined by shared headings, which is fine — but the vault-map k-means clusters are defined by CONTENT SIMILARITY, so penalising a whole k-means cluster is literally 'suppress by content similarity', which will suppress the correct answers first and hardest. That is the shared-tags mistake in new clothes (correlation 0.916 with an existing channel) and it should not be built. Keep the template-group half only behind the regret metric, since template groups genuinely encode a different thing. (2) ADD the high-volume negative the proposal misses: IMPRESSION FATIGUE. A card shown N times from the same source note and never opened is a negative that requires no button and no user effort, at maybe 100x the volume of explicit dismissals, and it is self-limiting once decayed and capped. That is the version worth building first. The x button then becomes the escape hatch AND the ground truth for validating the fatigue signal — check that high-fatigue cards are the ones users dismiss when given the chance — rather than the primary input.

**Why it is not redundant.** Negative feedback is information no similarity statistic and no link statistic contains: the user saying 'correct by the model, useless to me'. Its value is precision — removing wrong cards — which is what this project says it optimises for and which recall@10 does not measure at all.

**Kill experiment.** Not recall@10. Keep the self-controlled alternating-week design over four weeks of real use: dismissals per impression with the feature on and off. Keep the REGRET metric verbatim — how often a note the user later wikilinked by hand had been actively suppressed at the moment they needed it — it is the best measurement idea in the original set. Ship condition: dismissal rate down, regret under 2% of manually created links. Kill on regret. For the fatigue half specifically, one extra check: the correlation between accumulated impression count and eventual explicit dismissal. If it is near zero, impressions are not measuring disinterest and only the explicit half should ship.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Over-generalisation, and the daily-note case is the obvious trap — but the k-means half was the dangerous version and it is now cut. Residual risk on the fatigue half: impression counts confound 'uninteresting' with 'the user never looks at the sidebar', and those are indistinguishable from inside the plugin. The cap and the regret metric are what make the failure recoverable.

**Needs first.** Impression counting needs a minimal log (a counter, not proposal 4's full subsystem). Explicit dismissal needs only local storage.


### 11. CORRECTED (was 14) — Predictive index warming, from signals that need no log at all

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** The panel is already populated when you switch notes instead of thinking for a moment first.

**How.** The idea survives; its stated prerequisites do not. It is listed as depending on proposals 1 and 4 — episodes and an interaction log — neither of which exists. But the two best predictors of 'which note opens next' need no log whatsoever: (a) the RESOLVED OUT-LINKS of the currently open note — the user is very often about to click one, and resolvedLinks is already loaded and already walked by GraphSignals.ensure(); (b) the notes ALREADY VISIBLE IN THE PANEL, which the plugin itself just ranked and rendered, and which the user is being invited to click. Pre-warm the int8->fp32 LRU expansion for those two sets on `active-leaf-change` (handler already registered, main.ts:390). That is a strictly better null hypothesis than the DOI/co-episode version, needs zero new data, and can ship this week. Also keep the one genuinely good log-free half of the original: a note inside an active edit burst goes to the front of the re-embed queue, so the panel for the note you are writing is never the stale one — `vault.on('modify')` already populates `this.dirty` and `debouncedUpdate` at main.ts:419, so this is a priority change to an existing queue, not a new one.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a ranking signal. It converts cheap structural predictions into felt latency, which is what users actually perceive, and it is the only way to get value out of this whole area before any ranking change survives its gate.

**Kill experiment.** Keep it as proposed: instrument p50/p95 time-to-first-card at panel render and LRU hit rate on the chunk cache, replayed over a recorded trace so the comparison runs on identical traffic. Target p95 below the current p50. KEEP THE NULL HYPOTHESIS THE RESEARCHER STATED, it is the right one: the existing LRU may already capture most of this. Kill if the hit-rate improvement is under 10 percentage points.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Prefetching spends memory against the 4 GiB WASM cap, and on a 100k-note vault an over-broad prefetch is a regression rather than an improvement — a hub note with 200 out-links would warm 200 notes' chunk vectors on every switch. Needs a hard breadth cap tied to current heap headroom, and it must never preempt the foreground rank. Note that rank()'s Stage 1 already scans every entry linearly (`for (const entry of this.entries.values())` with a centeredMean and cosine each), so at 100k notes the foreground cost is tens of ms before any prefetch competes with it — measure the two together, not separately.

**Needs first.** None, in the corrected form.


### 12. CORRECTED (was 3) — Session-rooted PageRank with a degree-of-interest restart vector

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** The panel answers 'related to what I am working on', not just 'related to this one file'. Open three notes on a topic and the third result shifts to match the thread.

**How.** Keep the design: replace the delta restart with a distribution over the session weighted by a Mylyn-style degree of interest (k_open=1, k_edit=3, k_link_inserted=5, decayed), normalised via softmax over the last ~20 touched notes, with a hard floor of 0.5 of the mass on the active note so the panel never drifts off the file on screen. TWO CORRECTIONS. (1) 'Nothing else in src/graph-signals.ts changes — this is one input vector' is wrong, and the difference matters for scoping. `pprFrom(from: string)` takes a single string and seeds `front = new Map([[from, 1]])`; multi-seed changes that signature. More importantly `acc.delete(from)` at the end, and `candidates(from, limit)`'s `const direct = this.adj.get(from)` exclusion, and its per-candidate `this.evidence(from, path)` call all assume a single root and all need a decision about which seed they refer to (answer: keep the ACTIVE note for all three — you want the walk to be session-shaped but the exclusions and the RA receipt to stay about the note on screen). That is roughly 20 lines across two methods. Still cheap, but it is not a one-line input swap. (2) The day-one seed source is NOT the interaction log. `getLastOpenFiles()` is already in this codebase at src/view.ts:367 and returns Obsidian's own persisted recent list. That makes this the only session-shaped proposal in the whole document that is buildable and testable this week rather than next quarter.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not a new channel and so cannot fail the correlation test; it re-aims an existing one. Its value is recall of two- and three-hop candidates close to the session but not to the active note — exactly the region graph-signals.ts's own comments say only the PPR walk can enumerate.

**Kill experiment.** The proposed fallback — 'with no log, simulate sessions from the ctime-burst episodes of proposal 1' — is impossible: the corpus has two ctimes and produces no episodes. Replace it with the real trace from getLastOpenFiles(): replay ~200 actual note-opens and, at each, measure recall@10 of that note's held-out links, delta-restart versus DOI-restart. The design is PAIRED, so a Wilcoxon signed-rank test over the 200 pairs has real power despite the small n. Kill if recall does not move, or if the mass floor has to be raised past 0.8 to keep results stable — that would mean the session mass is noise, and the researcher is right that this is the diagnostic.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Session drift: one unrelated detour poisons the restart vector. The 0.5 mass floor mitigates it and also caps the upside, so the honest expected effect size is small — and with the floor at 0.5 the walk is at most half session-shaped, which may not be enough to move anything measurable. Real chance this is a correct idea with an effect below the noise of a 465-note held-out set.

**Needs first.** None, using getLastOpenFiles(). Proposal 4's log would sharpen it later.


### 13. CORRECTED (was 5) — Team-draft interleaving, run on suggester slates rather than panel cards

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Hidden by default. Optionally a 'help tune ranking' toggle showing the user their own scoreboard.

**How.** The harness design is right and should be built as specified: two ranker configs A (production) and B (candidate); per impression, build the shown list by team draft with a fresh coin each round; record team membership per item; an acceptance credits that item's team; aggregate the per-impression sign of (accepts_A - accepts_B) and run a two-sided sign test with a binomial CI. THE CORRECTION IS WHICH IMPRESSION. Run it on the `[[` suggester slate (see the substrate proposal above), not on sidebar cards, or at minimum on both with the suggester as primary. Two reasons. (i) Volume: typing `[[` is deliberate and frequent; opening a sidebar card is incidental and rare, and the researcher's own risk section concedes 'a user may open one card a day', which makes even a 100x-efficient method take months. (ii) The reward is right: on the suggester, the accept IS a link insertion, which is the action the product exists to cause. That fully resolves the objection the researcher files as a secondary risk — 'interleaving optimises for clicks, but this product's value is precision and silence, so a ranker showing more plausible cards can win while getting worse' — which is actually the PRIMARY risk on card clicks and which a post-click dwell gate patches only partially.

**Why it is not redundant.** There is no second user to A/B against, and held-out links cannot score a feature about attention, dwell or dismissal. Without this, every behaviour proposal in this document is unfalsifiable and therefore, by the project's own admission rule, unshippable.

**Kill experiment.** Keep the validation-before-use design, it is excellent: B = production (must converge to a tie) and B = production with the top 3 moved to the bottom (must be called worse), and the sabotage must be detected within a few dozen impressions. On suggester slates that bar is plausibly reachable in days. Run the accepted-slates/day counter first (see the substrate proposal) — below ~5/day, do not build this at all.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Volume is still the risk, just a smaller one. Second, specific to the suggester: interleaving a `[[` autocomplete popup is USER-VISIBLE in a way the sidebar is not — the top row is where most accepts land, and shuffling it changes the accept rate itself and will feel like the tool got worse. Randomise conservatively (ranks 2-5, not rank 1) and accept that this limits you to evaluating mid-list changes.

**Needs first.** The suggester-slate substrate.


### 14. CORRECTED (was 6) — Logged-slate replay and IPS, plus a zero-randomisation shortcut

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. It makes future ranking changes testable in seconds against past use instead of weeks of waiting.

**How.** Keep Li et al.'s replay estimator and the fixed examination prior p(rank) = 1/log2(1+rank) — the argument for a fixed prior over an estimated propensity with n=1 user is correct and well made. TWO CORRECTIONS. (1) Same substrate swap: log suggester slates, whose impression rate makes the 1/|slate| retention survivable (thousands of usable events rather than ~100). (2) DROP 'randomise only the top 3' as the mitigation — in a `[[` popup that is the most user-visible thing you could do (see the interleaving entry). Exploit instead a property the code already has: `getSuggestions()` blends exactly two components with FIXED constants, `FUZZY_WEIGHT * normFuzzy + SEMANTIC_WEIGHT * r.sem` (src/link-suggester.ts). Log both components per candidate and ANY reweighting of that two-scalar family is evaluable by counterfactual re-sort with ZERO randomisation and zero exploration cost. That is not unbiased in general, but for a two-parameter family over a slate you fully observe, it is honest, and it is the version that can run on day one of logging. Reserve true randomised replay for changes the re-sort cannot reach.

**Why it is not redundant.** It turns an interaction log into a reusable benchmark, which is the difference between one shippable behaviour feature and a programme of them.

**Kill experiment.** Keep the sanity check verbatim, it is the right one: replay must reproduce the interleaving verdict in sign, and roughly in magnitude, for changes already measured online. If replay and interleaving disagree in sign on two of three known changes, discard replay and keep interleaving only. For the zero-randomisation re-sort variant, the check is narrower: the re-sort estimate of a weight change must agree in sign with an interleave of that same weight change.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Replay retains ~1/|slate| of events and a thin log becomes very thin. The zero-randomisation re-sort avoids that but only covers reweightings of already-logged components — it cannot evaluate a NEW channel, which is the case anyone actually cares about. Be explicit that the cheap variant answers a narrower question than the expensive one.

**Needs first.** The suggester-slate substrate, and the interleaving harness for validation.


### 15. CORRECTED (was 7) — Learned fusion weights: it is three existing sliders, not a new model

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** The balance between 'says the same thing' and 'sits in the same part of your graph' quietly tunes itself to how you actually use the panel.

**How.** The core argument is right and worth restating: the previously-rejected personal similarity metric learned a transform in a 384/768-dimensional space from a few hundred link examples — a hopeless capacity ratio — whereas this learns a handful of scalars from clicks. Two orders of magnitude less capacity is the entire reason to retry it. THE CORRECTION IS SCOPE, and it makes the proposal cheaper and safer than described. There are not '4 to 8 weights' to learn. The fused score in rank() is z(content) + graphInfluence * z(RA), plus a separately-added structural boost scaled by structureInfluence, plus ideaInfluence blending idea-level MaxSim into the content term. That is THREE free scalars — `options.graphInfluence`, `options.structureInfluence`, `options.ideaInfluence` — and all three are ALREADY user-facing settings with safe shipped defaults. So this is not 'learn a fusion model', it is 'auto-tune three sliders the user can already move and can move back'. Fit by pairwise logistic regression over skip-above pairs (Radlinski & Joachims, KDD 2005), each weighted by the fixed examination prior and the valid-read weight, L2-regularised toward the shipped defaults so that with no data the model IS the shipped ranker. Refit on idle, weekly at most, gated behind an interleave.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a new channel; a per-user calibration of existing ones, so it cannot fail the admission rule. It also has a defensible worst case that the original framing lacks: the failure mode is 'a slider moved', which the user can undo.

**Kill experiment.** Replay as the offline screen, interleave as the ship gate. And keep the researcher's best line here verbatim: report the FITTED WEIGHTS with confidence intervals, and if they land within noise of the shipped defaults, the honest conclusion is that the uniform prior was already right — publish that and cut the feature, rather than shipping a no-op with a settings toggle attached. In the three-scalar framing that null result is genuinely informative and cheap to obtain.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Overfits whatever the user has been obsessed with for the last month, and degrades the ranker invisibly without the interleave gate. Realistic outcome is no measurable gain — this remains the proposal most likely to be honestly killed, and with only three scalars and L2 pull toward the defaults, the honest expected effect is very small.

**Needs first.** The suggester-slate substrate, the interleaving harness, and replay.


### 16. CORRECTED, PARKED (was 1) — Co-episode channel: the idea is right, all three bootstrap sources are empirically empty

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** A card can say 'written in the same sitting as X, three weeks ago'. Notes that came out of one piece of work surface together even when they share no words and no links.

**How.** Keep the scoring machinery, which is well designed: co(a,b) accumulated over episodes with the 1/log(1+|E|) hub discount (the RA degree penalty lifted to episode level, so a 60-file bulk rename cannot mint 1770 strong edges) and exp(-dt/lambda) recency at lambda ~ 90 days; normalise item-to-item as co(a,b)/sqrt(co(a,a)*co(b,b)); fuse by z-normalisation over the candidate pool exactly as RA is fused; store sparse, top-32 per note. ALL THREE BOOTSTRAP SOURCES ARE DEAD ON THE MEASUREMENT CORPUS, measured this session: ctime bursts give 2 episodes of 364 and 160 notes, which is zero after the proposal's own import guard; there is no .git; daily-note episodes give ~24 episodes of at most 2 members, covering 1.0% of link mass — about 24 pairs. Replace all three with the sources that actually exist: (a) getLastOpenFiles() sampled at each active-leaf-change, already read at src/view.ts:367 and persisted by Obsidian across restarts; (b) open-tab co-presence, free ambient state, no logging; (c) mtime-transition episodes harvested from the `vault.on('modify')` handler the incremental indexer already runs (main.ts:419) — persist (path, mtime) transitions into the existing index and you have an edit-episode stream in about five lines rather than a new subsystem. Keep the import guard (reject episodes with more than N notes inside M seconds) — it is what makes (c) safe.

**Why it is not redundant.** Two notes written in one sitting need share no vocabulary and no linked neighbour. The correlation risk is NOT with content; it is with the existing direct-link boost, because people link what they have just written. The researcher is right that this is the first number to compute.

**Kill experiment.** The proposed measurement cannot run: no episodes means nothing to score, and the chronological split it depends on does not exist. The honest first measurement is DESCRIPTIVE. Run the collector for four weeks, then report episode count, median episode size, and — the actual admission test — Spearman rank correlation of s_epi against content and against RA over the panel's real candidate pool. bench/v4-channels.mjs already computes exactly this kind of inter-channel correlation, so no new harness. Only if |rho| < 0.5 with both existing channels AND the channel is non-degenerate does a recall experiment make sense, and it must use a held-out set built from links created AFTER collection started — the only chronologically honest split available.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The mechanism is cheap; the DATA is a quarter away, and until then there is nothing to measure and nothing to ship. The bulk-import problem is worse than the proposal allows: it is not an edge case on the corpus this project measures on, it IS the corpus. Also worth flagging: because people link what they just wrote, the most likely outcome is high correlation with the direct-link boost and rejection under the project's own admission rule — the same fate as shared tags.

**Needs first.** The zero-logging session bootstrap for day-one episodes; proposal 4's log to grow them.


### 17. CORRECTED, DEFERRED (was 9) — MAGIC: Thread reconstruction. Right instinct, two false reuse claims, and no data for a year

*expensive · speculative confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** A card that reads: 'Thread — the magic-system rework. 9 notes, last touched 6 weeks ago, you stopped mid-way through <note>.' Clicking reopens the whole set.

**How.** The concept is the strongest magic candidate in the set and the scoring formula is good — dormancy x unfinishedness x relevance, where unfinishedness = fraction of members with unresolved links, open checkboxes, or edits never linked from anywhere. Keep all of that. BUT BOTH REUSE CLAIMS ARE FALSE, which is where the cost estimate went wrong. (1) 'Run the spherical k-means / power-iteration machinery already in src/vault-map.ts over that [episode-episode] graph' — `kmeans(vectors: Float32Array[], k, seed)` and `pca2` operate on dense embedding vectors under cosine, not on a graph. Episodes-as-incidence-vectors would be |V|-dimensional (100,000 dims on a large vault), which is both wrong for spherical k-means and a heap problem against the 4 GiB cap. The honest replacement is single-link agglomerative merging or connected components over a thresholded weighted-Jaccard episode graph — about 50 lines of new code, no reuse. (2) 'Run the existing c-TF-IDF cluster labeller over the concatenated text of its member notes' — `labelClusters(titles, assign, k)` (src/vault-map.ts:187) takes TITLES, not bodies. Feeding concatenated bodies is a different function with different vocabulary and stop-word behaviour. Feed member TITLES instead: it works, it is free, and it matches what the labeller was tuned on. Bonus, the researcher's degenerate-thread worry is already half-solved there — vault-map.ts:198 has an explicit guard stripping dated titles so daily notes cannot label their whole cluster.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not a pairwise scorer at all. It is a set-level object assembled from time and attention, and neither content similarity nor Resource Allocation can produce the statement 'these nine notes were one piece of work, and it stopped'. Genuinely the thing a cloud service structurally cannot build.

**Kill experiment.** The blind-judged protocol is right, and the SHUFFLED-EPISODE NEGATIVE CONTROL is the single best measurement idea in the original document — keep it. Targets: >=0.8 on 'was this a real thread of work', >=0.3 on 'did not know it was unfinished', shuffled controls near zero on the first. But it cannot run: it needs episodes, and there are none (see the co-episode entry). Earliest honest evaluation is roughly one quarter after episode collection starts.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Three, and together they are why this drops to speculative. It needs months of episode history that does not exist and cannot be back-filled from ctime or git on this corpus. Its two claimed code reuses are both wrong, so the real cost is a new clustering plus a new labelling path, not a wiring job. And a feature that is necessarily silent for its first quarter after install is a feature nobody discovers — which for a magic feature is the same as not shipping. Build the co-episode collector first and revisit only if its descriptive report shows non-degenerate episodes.

**Needs first.** The co-episode collector plus a quarter of real data. Does NOT usefully reuse vault-map.ts's clustering.


### 18. PARKED, effectively dead for now (was 2) — Trail-conditioned section: session kNN over past episodes

*moderate · speculative confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** A small second section in the panel — 'where you're heading' — showing notes that usually come next given the last few notes you moved through.

**How.** V-SKNN over past episodes with STAN's session-recency and within-episode position decays, top-m=100 neighbour episodes, current session S = last k=8 touched notes weighted 1/(1+i) with edits above opens, at most 3 cards above a calibrated floor, each with its own receipt. The design is a faithful and competent reading of the literature. It is parked, not corrected, because its input does not exist and the arithmetic against it is bad even once it does.

**Why it is not redundant.** The claim is sound in principle — it predicts the next artifact in a workflow rather than a semantic neighbour, and its strongest predictions would be project index notes and checklists, which have low content similarity and exactly the high degree that RA's 1/deg weighting deliberately discounts. Both existing channels are structurally incapable of ranking those.

**Kill experiment.** As proposed — leave-one-out next-item prediction over episodes with HR@3 and MRR, against an EdgeBank-style memorisation baseline ('the 3 notes most recently co-opened with the current one'), then an online interleave. Note that the memorisation baseline is very likely to WIN on a personal vault: with a few hundred episodes and a strongly habitual user, 'what came next last time' is close to the Bayes rate. Poursafaei et al.'s result is that memorisation beats far more sophisticated models on much larger graphs.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Dead for now, for three compounding reasons. (1) No episodes exist — same finding that guts proposal 1. (2) Data starvation is not a caveat but the whole story: V-SKNN and STAN were validated on millions of sessions; a personal vault produces a few hundred episodes a year, so there will rarely be enough neighbour episodes to vote, and the m=100 neighbourhood is larger than the entire corpus of episodes for the first year or two. (3) It makes the panel STATEFUL across navigation, so the same note shows different results depending on how you arrived — which directly fights the project's precision-first, receipts-you-can-check principle, because the receipt now depends on invisible history. Revisit only if the co-episode descriptive report shows hundreds of non-degenerate episodes AND memorisation is beatable.

**Needs first.** A year of episodes, minimum.


### 19. DEAD (was 13) — Read-mass chunk weighting

*expensive · speculative confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** A 3,000-word reference note you only ever open to check one section stops dragging in matches for the other 2,900 words.

**How.** Proposed: map visible line ranges to chunk indices on scroll, accumulate idle-truncated dwell per chunk, keep a decayed int8 read-mass vector alongside the chunk vectors, and weight the source note's chunk contributions inside BiMax by (1 + gamma * rhat_i). Elegant on paper. It rests on a premise I checked and which is false.

**Why it is not redundant.** The independence argument was actually the strongest in the document — no text-derived or link-derived statistic can say which section a reader cares about, and the same file legitimately produces different neighbours for different readers. That is a genuinely new degree of freedom. It is a shame the plumbing does not exist.

**Kill experiment.** Only the diagnostic is worth running, and only if someone is curious: dump the read-mass distribution over chunk index across 200 notes and check whether it concentrates on the first screen. The researcher predicts it will, and is probably right, in which case the signal measures scroll position rather than interest.

**Most likely reason it fails.** DEAD, for a reason the proposal states as an assumption and gets wrong. 'The chunker already stores offsets' is false. `interface NoteChunk` (src/index-store.ts:353) is exactly { text, isTitle, heading?, embedText?, ideaId?, isMean? } — no offsets, no line numbers, no character positions. Worse, chunks are cut from STRIPPED markdown: stripMarkdown/stripMarkdownBlocks run first, then splitIntoSections, then sentence windowing, then splitToBudget — three successive text-rewriting transforms, so there is no surviving map from a rendered line range back to a chunk index and no reliable way to search one back. Adding offsets means threading positions through all three transforms and bumping INDEX_VERSION, and that file's own comment states a version bump 'forces ONE clean rebuild on upgrade' — i.e. a full re-embed of every user's entire vault, on a WASM model, to acquire a speculative signal whose own author expects position bias to swamp it. Two independent reasons to kill, either sufficient. Scroll instrumentation is also the most invasive logging proposed anywhere here, and it would be spent on the least likely payoff.

**Needs first.** None — do not build it.


## Completeness Critique Across


<details><summary>10 techniques surveyed</summary>

- **Query Performance Prediction (post-retrieval, unsupervised): NQC, Clarity, WIG, SMV, sigma-max** (established, 2002-2012, with an active dense-retrieval line since ~2022) — Predicts, from the shape of the retrieved score distribution alone and with no labels, whether a given query was answered well. NQC is the standard deviation of the top-k scores divided by the corpus-score norm; sigma-max is the sd up to the rank of the maximum; WIG is the mean gap between the top-k scores and the whole-collection score. All are a handful of lines over numbers the ranker already computes and throws away. Critically, the family also comes with an evaluation protocol — Kendall tau or sARE between the predictor and the actual per-query effectiveness — which is exactly what the six independent abstention proposals in this corpus lack.  
  <sub>Cronen-Townsend, Zhou & Croft, 'Predicting Query Performance', SIGIR 2002 (Clarity); Zhou & Croft, 'Query Performance Prediction in Web Search Environments', SIGIR 2007 (WIG); Shtok, Kurland, Carmel, Raiber & Markovits, 'Predicting Query Performance by Query-Drift Estimation', ACM TOIS 30(2), 2012 (NQC)</sub>
- **Reciprocal Rank Fusion and the score-normalisation family (CombSUM/CombMNZ, Condorcet)** (established, 1994-2009) — Fuses ranked lists by summing 1/(k+rank) with k~60, instead of summing z-scores. It is explicitly designed for the case this project keeps hitting: channels whose score distributions are not comparable, and sparse channels where most candidates score exactly zero so the z-score is a degenerate transform of a spike-at-zero distribution. The KG reviewer correctly diagnosed 'stop z-adding sparse channels' as the reason several ideas were rejected, but did not name the 5-line replacement that the IR community standardised on for precisely that failure.  
  <sub>Cormack, Clarke & Buettcher, 'Reciprocal Rank Fusion outperforms Condorcet and individual Rank Learning Methods', SIGIR 2009; Fox & Shaw, 'Combination of Multiple Searches', TREC-2, 1994</sub>
- **Set-level result selection: MMR and fast greedy MAP inference for Determinantal Point Processes** (established, 1998-2018) — Chooses the displayed SET rather than the top-k individually, trading relevance against redundancy. A DPP over a kernel L = diag(quality) * Similarity * diag(quality) has a greedy MAP algorithm that runs in O(k^2 n) with a Cholesky update — trivially affordable on a 100-candidate pool — and, unlike a fixed top-k, it naturally stops adding items when marginal gain goes negative, which is the 'silence beats a wrong answer' behaviour as a consequence of the objective rather than a threshold. Across all twelve reports, exactly one line ('Diversification — cut') addresses set-level selection; every other proposal optimises pointwise score.  
  <sub>Carbonell & Goldstein, 'The Use of MMR, Diversity-Based Reranking', SIGIR 1998; Chen, Zhang & Zhou, 'Fast Greedy MAP Inference for Determinantal Point Process to Improve Recommendation Diversity', NeurIPS 2018; Kulesza & Taskar, 'Determinantal Point Processes for Machine Learning', FnTML 2012</sub>
- **Serendipity and unexpectedness as measurable quantities, with a primitive-baseline protocol** (established, 2005-2018) — Defines unexpectedness as the set difference between what a system recommends and what a deliberately obvious 'primitive prediction' baseline recommends, then defines serendipity as unexpectedness intersected with usefulness. This gives the missing second admission rule: a candidate discovery is one the obvious baseline would NOT have produced but the user judges good. The field also supplies the offline machinery — serendipity ground-truth collections gathered by asking users directly, and the standard warning that accuracy metrics and serendipity metrics move in opposite directions, which is exactly the trap this project's held-out-link protocol is sitting in.  
  <sub>Ge, Delgado-Battenfeld & Jannach, 'Beyond Accuracy: Evaluating Recommender Systems by Coverage and Serendipity', RecSys 2010; Kotkov, Konstan, Zhao & Veijalainen, 'Investigating Serendipity in Recommender Systems Based on Real User Feedback' (SAC 2018) and the Serendipity-2018 dataset; Itti & Baldi, 'Bayesian Surprise Attracts Human Attention', NIPS 2005</sub>
- **Language-identity subspace in multilingual sentence encoders** (established, 2019-2022) — Multilingual encoders reserve a low-dimensional, near-linearly-separable subspace for language identity, so same-language pairs sit systematically closer than cross-language pairs regardless of meaning. The standard remedies are centring per language, projecting out the between-language mean difference, or explicit translation-ranking objectives (which is what LaBSE adds). I measured the axis on this project's own shipped geometry: AUC 0.981 from one direction, 6.1% of variance, +0.088 cosine bias. The literature also predicts what I then measured — that naive global removal costs monolingual quality, which is why LaBSE-style methods train the alignment rather than subtracting it post hoc.  
  <sub>Libovicky, Rosa & Fraser, 'How Language-Neutral is Multilingual BERT?', arXiv 1911.03310, 2019; Feng, Yang, Cer, Arivazhagan & Wang, 'Language-agnostic BERT Sentence Embedding' (LaBSE), ACL 2022</sub>
- **All-but-the-top and whitening — the generalisation of the shipped mean-centring** (established, 2018-2021) — The plugin subtracts the corpus centroid, which removes exactly one component. All-but-the-top says: subtract the mean AND project out the top d/100 principal directions, because embedding spaces are anisotropic in more than one direction and those top components encode frequency and register rather than meaning. Whitening goes further and rescales the covariance to isotropic. Both are ~20 lines over the existing centred matrix and both have a directly applicable failure mode here — my language axis is very likely one of those top components, which means all-but-the-top and language de-crowding are the same operation and should be measured jointly rather than separately.  
  <sub>Mu & Viswanath, 'All-but-the-Top: Simple and Effective Postprocessing for Word Representations', ICLR 2018; Su, Cao, Liu & Ou, 'Whitening Sentence Representations for Better Semantics and Faster Retrieval', arXiv 2103.15316, 2021</sub>
- **Half-life regression / FSRS — memory decay as a surfacing policy** (established, 2016-2024) — Models the probability that a person still recalls an item as an exponential decay whose half-life is fit from their own review history, and schedules a resurfacing when predicted recall crosses a threshold. This is the principled version of the 'forgotten neighbour', 'dormant rediscovery' and 'unfinished threads' proposals, all of which use raw mtime and all of which wave at the question of how often the card should fire. The decay model answers it: the firing rate is a consequence of the threshold, it is per-note rather than global, and it degrades gracefully when history is thin.  
  <sub>Settles & Meeder, 'A Trainable Spaced Repetition Model for Language Learning', ACL 2016 (half-life regression); the open-source FSRS scheduler (Free Spaced Repetition Scheduler), 2022-2024, a DSR model fit by gradient descent</sub>
- **TagMe-style entity linking: anchor-text keyphraseness plus collective coherence disambiguation** (established, 2008-2010) — Decides whether a plain-text string is a mention of an entity by how often that exact string appears as LINK ANCHOR TEXT elsewhere in the same corpus (keyphraseness), then disambiguates among candidates by choosing the assignment most coherent with the other entities in the same document. It needs no NER model, no gazetteer and no language assumption — only the link graph and display text, both of which Obsidian already exposes. The people report considered a GLiNER download (dead, expensive) and a hand-built German determiner heuristic; the places report considered a bundled gazetteer. Neither considered the family that is built from the user's own linking behaviour and therefore works for people, places, projects and concepts with one mechanism.  
  <sub>Ferragina & Scaiella, 'TAGME: On-the-fly Annotation of Short Text Fragments', CIKM 2010; Milne & Witten, 'Learning to Link with Wikipedia', CIKM 2008</sub>
- **Pairwise learning-to-rank on a handful of features (LambdaRank/LambdaMART), as distinct from metric learning** (established, 2010) — Learns a ranking function from pairwise preferences by weighting each pair's gradient by the NDCG change that swapping them would cause. The distinction the reports missed: the project measured and rejected 'a personal similarity metric learned from the user's own links', which is a Mahalanobis form over 384-768 dimensions and will overfit on 1300 edges — whereas a pairwise ranker over 5-8 CHANNEL features (content z, RA z, degree, folder distance, language match, recency) has single-digit capacity, trains in milliseconds, and is a completely different bias-variance regime. Four separate proposals want to fit fusion weights and none made this distinction, so the earlier negative result is being over-generalised.  
  <sub>Burges, 'From RankNet to LambdaRank to LambdaMART: An Overview', Microsoft Research Technical Report MSR-TR-2010-82, 2010</sub>
- **Matryoshka Representation Learning — the model-side alternative to a vault-fitted PCA cascade** (established, 2022) — Trains an embedding so that its first 64, 128, 256 ... dimensions are each independently a usable embedding, which gives a free coarse-to-fine cascade with no PCA fit, no per-vault state to maintain and no drift when the vault changes. The retrieval report proposes fitting PCA per vault to shrink the index; MRL gets the same cascade for free IF the chosen model was trained with it. This matters concretely because jina-v5-nano, the model the lab has standardised on, is from a family that supports dimension truncation — so the question 'do we need the PCA at all' is answerable by one truncation experiment before any cascade is written.  
  <sub>Kusupati, Bhatt, Rege, Wallingford et al., 'Matryoshka Representation Learning', NeurIPS 2022</sub>
