</details>


### 1. The Übersetzung slot: cross-language bridges as their own section, scored against their own null

*moderate · high confidence · magic: yes*

**What you get.** A small section under the related notes: 'Also in German — Kettenregel', linking the English note you are reading to the German note that says the same thing in your other language. It fires on maybe one note in eight and it finds the pairs you built your understanding out of but never linked.

**How.** Language is detected per note with a stopword-frequency ratio over the existing tokeniser (no model, no download, ~30 lines; I used a 24-word DE list and a 22-word EN list and it separated 439 of 457 notes, 18 left undetermined and excluded). At index time, fit one direction u = normalise(mean(vecs of language A) - mean(vecs of language B)) over the mean-centred whole-note vectors. At rank time, partition the candidate pool by language relative to the query note. The MAIN ranking is untouched — this is the load-bearing correction, because I measured that re-stratifying it loses 119 same-language hits to gain 7. Instead, the cross-language partition is z-normalised separately (its own mean and sd) and its top 2 candidates are offered in a separate slot, gated on: (a) the candidate's stratified z exceeds a fixed threshold, (b) the pair is not already linked, (c) both notes have >=8 language-marker tokens so the detection is confident. Optionally add the projection-out of u as a second scorer within the slot only, where I measured it closes the same/cross cosine gap from +0.0882 to -0.0044.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not the content channel: the content channel is what CREATES the deficit, because the encoder puts a +0.088 cosine wall between the languages and cross-language recall sits at 0.576 against a 0.805 global. It is not the graph channel: 64 of the vault's 1264 edges are cross-language, so RA has almost nothing to propagate through in this stratum and the pairs it finds are by definition the ones with no shared neighbourhood. Rank correlation with both existing channels is bounded above by the fraction of the pool that is even eligible (roughly 38% here, and near 0 in a monolingual vault, where the whole feature correctly switches itself off).

**Kill experiment.** Held-out-links protocol, Fisher-Yates, 8+ seeds, but reported on the PRE-REGISTERED cross-language subset rather than the global average (n=446 in this vault over 8 seeds — thin, so report the paired flip count as the primary statistic, not the rate). Ship criterion: on cross-language held-out links, miss->hit must exceed hit->miss by at least 4:1 in the paired comparison against the shipped ranker, AND the same-language paired flips must be exactly zero because the main ranking is not modified. I have already run the strong version of this: 8 miss->hit vs 1 hit->miss on cross-language. Second, a precision gate before shipping: the slot must reach judged precision@2 >= 0.8 on a blind panel, using the same protocol Surprising Connections v2 used. Kill if the vault is >90% one language and the slot never fires — that is correct behaviour, not failure, but it should be reported in settings.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The lab vault gives only ~446 cross-language query instances over 8 seeds and 34 authentic-authentic edges of any kind, so the effect is measurable but the precision gate is not — it needs a judged panel, and the panel needs bilingual notes the user actually wrote rather than generated ones. Second risk: stopword-ratio language ID will misfire on short notes and on maths-heavy notes that are mostly LaTeX; the >=8-marker gate handles this but costs coverage. Third: on a monolingual vault this ships nothing, so it cannot be the only thing built.

**Needs first.** None in code. Depends on nothing else in the roadmap. Needs a bilingual judged panel for the precision gate.


### 2. The deficit atlas: replace 'does it move the average' with 'which stratum does it move, and what did it cost'

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing. It is the instrument that decides which of the other ~180 proposals is allowed to ship, and it will move several numbers this project has already published.

**How.** One harness that runs the existing held-out-links protocol and reports recall@10 broken out by pre-registered strata rather than pooled: link provenance (authentic/generated, straight from corpus-manifest.json), language pair, folder pair, source and target length bucket, source degree bucket, and note age bucket where dates exist. Each cell reports rate, n, and a binomial standard error, and every proposal must declare its target stratum BEFORE measurement. Paired flip counts (miss->hit vs hit->miss on identical held-out sets) are the primary statistic rather than rate differences, because with 8 seeds the seed-to-seed sd is 0.018-0.021 and half the corpus's proposals claim less than that. I have this running; it is ~150 lines and it produced the numbers in the area summary.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel — it is the referee, and it is independent of every channel because it changes what 'a win' means. It also subsumes the GNN reviewer's 'pre-registered subgroup reporting' proposal, which stated the idea but never instantiated a stratum that shows a deficit.

**Kill experiment.** Self-measuring by construction. The validation is that it reproduces the project's published numbers when the strata are pooled, and that it surfaces at least one deficit larger than 2 sd that no existing report predicted — it already has: authentic-authentic 0.353 vs generated-generated 0.830, and source-degree-11+ 0.595, which contradicts the five reports that concluded from HUB_DEGREE=40 never firing that there is no hub pathology. Kill condition: none; if every stratum sits within 2 sd of the global average, that is a publishable negative and the corpus concern is dead.

**Most likely reason it fails.** It makes several current numbers go down and will invalidate accept/reject calls already made — a political cost, not a technical one. The real technical risk is stratum proliferation: with 6 strata families you get multiple-comparison problems, so the strata must be pre-registered and fixed, not fished for.

**Needs first.** None. Should be built before anything else, because it changes what everything else has to prove.


### 3. A second admission rule: the discovery protocol, so a magic feature can actually pass something

*moderate · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Nothing directly. It is the reason the next magic feature can be believed instead of argued about, and it is what stops the panel optimising itself into obviousness.

**How.** Held-out link recall rewards predicting links the user DID make. Codify the complementary rule the project already improvised once for Surprising Connections and then forgot. A pair (A,B) is a DISCOVERY CANDIDATE iff: not linked in either direction; B is outside A's content top-50 and A outside B's top-50 (the existing surprising-connections gate); and both notes are the user's own writing. Score two ways. (1) An automatic proxy, unexpectedness relative to a primitive baseline: run a deliberately obvious ranker (content top-10 alone) and measure the set difference — a feature with zero set difference from the primitive baseline is by definition producing nothing the user could not have gotten by scrolling. (2) The judged panel, which the project has already run at Fleiss kappa 0.77: blind, sandboxed, 3 judges, with the pre-registered question being not 'is this related' but 'was this obvious from the two titles alone', because that is the question the Continuity v2 experiment failed on (8 of 9 finds judged obvious). Ship criterion for anything claiming the magic slot: judged non-obviousness >= 0.5 AND judged precision >= 0.8, both at the fire rate the feature will actually run at.

**Why it is not redundant.** It measures the orthogonal complement of what the existing rule measures. A feature can score 0.000 on held-out link recall and still be the best thing in the product — that is definitionally true of any feature whose output is pairs the user never linked, which is every proposal in the corpus marked magic:yes.

**Kill experiment.** Validate the instrument against the two features whose magic status is already known: Surprising Connections v2 must pass (measured P@5 1.00, 36% surprising) and Continuity v2 must fail on non-obviousness (measured 8/9 obvious). If the protocol does not reproduce both of those verdicts, the protocol is wrong. Then pre-register it before the next magic candidate is built.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Judged panels are slow and cannot be run per-commit, so the automatic proxy carries most of the weight and it is weaker than the judgement. The bigger risk is corpus: judging non-obviousness requires notes the user actually wrote, and there are 124 of those. This proposal is therefore downstream of the corpus rebuild in a way the recall protocol is not.

**Needs first.** The corpus rebuild, for the judged half. The automatic proxy can run today.


### 4. Semantic search and select-to-search — the entire query surface, absent from ~180 proposals

*moderate · high confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** A search box that finds notes by what they mean, not by the words they contain: type 'the trick where you subtract the mean' and get the note about centering. And its twin — highlight any paragraph anywhere in your vault, hit a hotkey, and the panel becomes 'passages elsewhere that say this', with the matching sentences shown.

**How.** The whole apparatus already exists: chunk vectors, mean-centring, the coarse-to-fine funnel, BiMax, the graph channel, the receipt renderer. What is missing is a query that is not a file. Embed the query string with the model's Query: prefix rather than Document: (jina-v5-nano has asymmetric prefixes; the plugin currently only ever uses Document:), run it through the identical Stage-1/Stage-2 funnel with the graph channel disabled (a query string has no neighbours), and render the same cards. Select-to-search is the same path with the selection as the query, a ~10-line addition once the query path exists. The retrieval report's 'select a passage, get its relatives' is half of this; the other half — a free-text box — is proposed by nobody.

**Why it is not redundant.** It is not a ranking channel at all, so the admission rule does not apply. It is the missing half of the product: every existing proposal answers 'what relates to this file', and none answers 'where did I write about X'. It is also the cheapest possible answer to the compounding goal, because a search box gets strictly more useful as the vault grows while a related-notes panel does not.

**Kill experiment.** Not the held-out-links protocol — that protocol has no queries. Use the lab's existing aq-eval harness (question -> answer-note ground truth, /Users/justus/obsidian_atomized_intermediary/related-notes/bench/aq-eval.mjs and lab/ground-truth.json), which is exactly a query-to-note retrieval benchmark and is currently used for something else. Report MRR@10 and recall@10 against two baselines: Obsidian's built-in search, and BM25 over the same corpus. Ship criterion: beat BM25 on MRR@10 for queries that share no content word with the target (the paraphrase slice), which is the only slice where a semantic index can justify its cost. Kill if it loses to BM25 on the lexical slice by more than 0.1 MRR — then it needs hybrid fusion (RRF, see the techniques list), not shipping alone.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The asymmetric Query:/Document: prefix means the shipped index may need re-embedding or at least a verification pass that Document-prefixed note vectors are comparable to Query-prefixed query vectors — jina's text-matching variant is symmetric-ish, MiniLM has no prefixes at all, so the two supported models behave differently and this must be checked per model before UI work. Second risk: it competes with Obsidian's own search and with Omnisearch, so it must be clearly better on paraphrase queries or it is noise.

**Needs first.** None. The index and the funnel already exist.


### 5. Make the `[[` completion semantic — the highest-frequency touchpoint in the product, currently prefix-matched

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** You type `[[` and the suggestions are the notes that belong there, not the notes whose titles start with the letters you typed. Type `[[chain` in a German note and Kettenregel is offered.

**How.** src/link-suggester.ts already exists (468 lines) and already hooks the editor suggester. Replace or augment its candidate ordering with the same fused score the panel uses, seeded from two things Obsidian gives free: the partial string the user has typed (as a query, via the search path above) and the note being edited (as context, via the existing rank()). Fuse the lexical prefix match and the semantic score with RRF rather than z-addition, because a prefix match is a binary spike and z-normalising a spike distribution is the exact failure the KG reviewer diagnosed. Two-for-one: this is also the cleanest behavioural log in the product — every `[[` interaction is a SLATE (what was offered) plus an OUTCOME (what was chosen or nothing), which is precisely the counterfactual-evaluable data that the seven session-log proposals and the four learned-fusion proposals all need and none of them has.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel; a surface. But it independently solves the data problem behind nine other proposals: unlike a click log on the sidebar panel, a suggester log records what was NOT chosen, which is what makes inverse-propensity replay possible at all. The local-signals reviewer identified this exact point and it is the only report that did.

**Kill experiment.** Two stages. Offline first, no users needed: replay the vault's OWN existing links as ground truth — for each real wikilink, take the first 1/2/3 characters of the link text as a simulated prefix, rank candidates, and measure MRR of the true target against the current prefix-match baseline. That is a few thousand queries from the existing corpus with no new data. Ship criterion: MRR@5 improvement > 2 sd on the paired comparison, and no regression on the exact-prefix slice (where prefix matching should still win and RRF should preserve it). Stage two, after shipping: team-draft interleaving on real slates.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Latency. The suggester fires on every keystroke inside `[[`, and the panel's funnel is budgeted for a note change, not for 10 Hz. This needs the coarse Stage-0 to be genuinely cheap (binary/quantised) before it is safe, so it is downstream of the retrieval report's Stage-0 work. Second risk: users have deep muscle memory for prefix matching, so reordering must be additive (semantic candidates appended or interleaved below exact prefix hits) or it will feel broken.

**Needs first.** A cheap Stage-0 (retrieval report's binary shortlist). The semantic query path from the previous proposal.


### 6. One session logger, replacing seven proposals across five reports

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** An opt-in switch, a plain-text log you can open and read, and a one-click wipe. Later, and only later: 'you keep working on these two together'.

**How.** A single append-only JSONL file recording (timestamp, event, path) for file-open, file-modify and active-leaf-change, with a session boundary at 30 minutes idle. That is the complete substrate for all seven of: the GNN multiplex co-editing layer, KG recency-weighted co-activity, time-embedding session co-edit channel, time-graph session co-visit graph, local-signals co-episode channel, places writing-sessions-as-pseudo-places, and events reading trail. The point of this proposal is not the idea — seven teams had it — but the sequencing: NONE of the seven can be measured for at least a quarter after logging starts, so the logger's value is entirely in how early it ships, and it is currently blocked behind seven separate design debates about what to do with the data. Ship the logger with no consumer at all. Bootstrap the cold quarter from the two zero-logging sources the local-signals reviewer found: workspace.getLastOpenFiles() and open-tab co-presence at startup.

**Why it is not redundant.** Co-editing is measurably independent of both existing channels by construction — it is the only signal in the entire corpus that comes from the user's behaviour rather than from the text or the links, so it cannot be a re-derivation of either. Whether it is USEFUL is unknown and unknowable today; that is exactly why the logger must ship before the argument is settled.

**Kill experiment.** The logger itself is measured by coverage, not recall: after 90 days, what fraction of note pairs have >=1 co-session, and what is the degree distribution of the co-session graph? Pre-register the kill condition NOW: if fewer than 5% of note pairs that share a co-session are non-linked and non-similar, the channel has no population to serve and all seven proposals die together. Only after that gate does the held-out-links measurement make sense, and it must be run on the CHRONOLOGICAL protocol (train on links before date T, test after) because a co-session that postdates the link it is predicting is leakage.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Privacy optics — a keystroke-adjacent log in a local-first plugin is the single most reputationally dangerous thing in this roadmap, so opt-in, human-readable and wipeable are not nice-to-haves. Technical risk: 90 days of latency before anything can be learned, and the possibility that after 90 days the answer is 'this vault has no co-editing structure', which is a real outcome for someone who reads one note at a time.

**Needs first.** None. Its value is monotonically decreasing in how long it waits.


### 7. Anchor-text entity layer: one mechanism for people, places, projects and concepts, built from the user's own links

*moderate · medium confidence · magic: partly*

**What you get.** The plugin knows that 'Kettenregel', 'Anna M.' and 'Freiburg' are things in your vault and that a note mentioning one of them without linking it is still about it — and it works out which is which from how you have linked them before, in whatever language you wrote them in.

**How.** TagMe's keyphraseness, computed over the vault instead of Wikipedia. For every distinct anchor string that appears inside a wikilink anywhere in the vault (including the display half of [[target|display]]) plus every frontmatter alias, compute keyphraseness = (times the string appears as an anchor) / (times the string appears at all). Strings above threshold are ENTITIES. Disambiguation among candidate targets uses collective coherence: choose the assignment maximising agreement with the other entities already identified in the same note, where agreement is the existing RA over the link graph. Type the entity — person, place, project, concept — only where the vault volunteers it (a #person tag, a folder, a frontmatter key), and otherwise leave it untyped, because the untyped version is already useful and the typed version is what killed three people proposals and five place proposals. This directly replaces both forks the two reports considered: the GLiNER download (dead: expensive, English-biased) and the German determiner heuristic (language-specific).

**Why it is not redundant.** The mention channel fires on pairs that name the same entity WITHOUT either note linking it — those pairs have RA contribution of exactly zero through that entity by definition, so it is disjoint from the graph channel by construction rather than by measurement. Against content it is not obviously independent, and that is the thing to measure first: a shared rare proper noun and a high cosine will correlate, and the corpus already killed a lexical keyword channel at correlation 0.30.

**Kill experiment.** Stage 1, no ranking, one afternoon: run the keyphraseness extractor over the vault and report how many strings clear threshold, what fraction of the 106 unresolved link targets it recovers, and precision on a hand-checked sample of 50. Kill if fewer than 30 entities clear threshold — the corpus has nothing to link. Stage 2, admission rule proper: add shared-unlinked-entity as a channel, measure Spearman rank correlation against both the content channel and the RA channel on the same candidate pools, and measure recall@10 delta on the deficit atlas's CROSS-FOLDER stratum specifically (0.604 today, the population where entity co-mention should help most). Ship only if correlation with content < 0.25 (the lexical channel died at 0.30) AND the cross-folder stratum improves by > 2 sd on paired flips.

**Most likely reason it fails.** On this corpus stage 1 probably passes on concepts and definitely fails on people — the vault has one #person tag and it is an empty template. So the honest framing is that this ships as a concept/entity feature and the people story waits for a corpus, which is what the people reviewer concluded by a different route. Second risk: keyphraseness on a small vault is a ratio of small integers and will be noisy; it needs a minimum-occurrence floor.

**Needs first.** Reading metadataCache.unresolvedLinks and link display text — neither of which src/ currently touches.


### 8. Fix the hub constant with the number that actually exists, and fix the receipt-attachment rule while in there

*cheap · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** Fewer bad cards on the notes that link to everything, and a card that tells you WHY it is there whenever the graph is what put it there — not only when the graph was also what found it.

**How.** Two small corrections that five reports circled without landing. (1) HUB_DEGREE = 40 is not merely dead code; my stratified measurement shows source notes of degree 11+ score 0.595 against a 0.805 global, a -0.210 deficit, so the pathology is real and the constant is off by roughly 4x. Replace the absolute constant with a vault-relative one — the 90th percentile of the degree distribution, recomputed at index time — which fires on every vault instead of on none, and sweep it against the degree-stratified recall rather than the global average, which is why previous sweeps found nothing. (2) index-store.ts attaches the graph receipt only when `rescued = s.nominated && s.ra > 0 && s.shared.length > 0`, where `nominated` means the GRAPH put the note in the candidate pool. Since 91.2% of the pool arrives via the content shortlist, a card whose rank was materially moved by graphLift but which was reached by content renders as plain 'Similar text'. Change the condition from pipeline provenance to score contribution: attach the receipt when graphLift exceeds a threshold, whatever put the note in the pool. The graph-signals.ts evidence() function already sorts shared neighbours by 1/deg descending, so shared[0] is genuinely the top contributor — the ranking of the receipt is correct, only its coverage is wrong.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a new channel; a correction to two shipped ones. The independence question does not apply, but the measurement discipline does.

**Kill experiment.** For (1): sweep the hub percentile over {80, 85, 90, 95, 99} and report recall@10 on the degree-stratified atlas, not pooled — the ship criterion is improvement in the 11+ stratum with no more than 1 sd loss in the 3-10 stratum, on paired flips over 8 seeds. This measurement is only possible with the deficit atlas built first, which is why five reports concluded 'unmeasurable'. For (2): instrument the shipped ranker over the lab vault and report the fraction of top-10 cards with graphLift above the proposed threshold whose reason.kind is not 'graph' — that number is the receipt coverage gap, and it is a fact about the current build, not an experiment. Then re-run the judged-precision panel to confirm that showing more receipts does not show WRONG receipts.

**Most likely reason it fails.** The lab vault's max degree is 37, so a 90th-percentile rule is being fitted on a graph whose whole degree range sits below the current constant — the correction is right in direction but its calibration will not transfer to a 40,000-note vault with real MOCs, and the benches additionally strip MOC notes with /\bMOC\b/i, deleting exactly the population the constant exists for. That strip must be removed before this is measured, which will move other published numbers.

**Needs first.** The deficit atlas. Removal of the MOC strip from the bench harnesses.


### 9. The scaling protocol: evidence for 'compounds with vault size', which currently has none

*moderate · high confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** An honest answer to 'will this work on my 40,000-note vault' — and, where it will not, a setting that says so instead of a spinner.

**How.** Constraint 4 promises 500 to 100,000 notes; every number this project has ever published comes from one vault of 465. Build a size ladder — 500 / 2k / 10k / 50k / 100k — and at each rung measure four things: (a) held-out link recall@10, to test whether quality compounds or decays; (b) time to first card and time to full index; (c) peak WASM heap against the 4 GiB cap; (d) the fraction of the candidate pool reachable, which the diffusion reviewer measured at 97% on 465 notes and which is the number most likely to collapse with scale. The corpus for the ladder does not need to be a real vault: tile and perturb public multilingual corpora (Wikipedia section dumps in DE and EN give you both the size and the language mix, with the section link graph as ground-truth edges) — legitimate here because the question is scaling behaviour, not semantic realism. Report every project number with the vault size it was measured at, from now on.

**Why it is not redundant.** It measures a dimension nothing else measures. Several shipped mechanisms are anti-compounding by construction and nobody has checked: the corpus centroid carries less information as the vault diversifies, so mean-centring should weaken with scale; k-means k is fixed, so cluster labels should degrade; and c-TF-IDF over titles gets noisier as titles proliferate. All three are testable on the ladder and all three are currently assumed benign.

**Kill experiment.** Self-measuring. Pre-register the failure conditions: recall@10 falling by more than 0.05 between the 500 and 10k rungs kills the 'compounds' claim outright; peak heap exceeding 3 GiB at 100k kills the current index format; time-to-first-card exceeding 500 ms at 100k kills the current Stage-0. Each of those has a named owner in the retrieval report (chunk text in the JSON manifest, incremental centering, binary Stage 0) and this is the experiment that decides which of them is urgent rather than theoretical.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Synthetic corpora do not have the link topology of a personal vault — Wikipedia is far denser and far more hierarchical, so the recall numbers on the ladder are not comparable to the lab vault's and must be read as a within-ladder trend only. Also: embedding 100k notes to build the ladder is itself a multi-day job, which is a real cost and is also, usefully, the first honest datapoint about first-index time.

**Needs first.** None, but it should be read together with the deficit atlas — 'which stratum' and 'at what size' are the two axes every future number needs.


### 10. Writeback and interop: make the plugin's output queryable by the rest of Obsidian

*cheap · medium confidence · magic: no*

**What you get.** An optional command that writes the plugin's current answer into the note's own frontmatter — `Related Notes: [[A]], [[B]]` — so Dataview, Bases, the graph view and every other plugin can see it. Your own template already has that empty key waiting.

**How.** The user's authentic notes carry an empty `Related Notes:` frontmatter key — their own template anticipated exactly this and nothing fills it. Add an explicit, never-automatic command that writes the current top-k (with a configurable count) into that key via Obsidian's processFrontMatter API, plus a provenance marker so the plugin can tell its own writes from the user's. The provenance marker is not decoration: the KG reviewer's point that the plugin will otherwise poison the link graph it learns from is exactly right, and a written-back link must be excluded from resolvedLinks when building the RA channel, or the system trains on its own output within one index cycle. The same mechanism serves the MOC generator the hierarchy report proposes.

**Why it is not redundant.** Not a channel. It is the distribution strategy: in the Obsidian ecosystem the plugins that become load-bearing are the ones whose output other plugins can query, and nothing in ~180 proposals addresses that. It also converts every suggestion into a durable, user-editable artefact, which is the strongest possible version of the self-verifying goal — the receipt stops being a tooltip and becomes a link the user can follow six months later.

**Kill experiment.** Not a ranking change, so not the held-out protocol. The measurement that matters is the poisoning control, and it is mandatory before shipping: build the RA channel twice, once counting written-back links and once excluding them, and confirm the two agree to within noise on a vault where writeback has run. If they diverge, the exclusion is not working and the feature is actively degrading the product. Second, an acceptance measurement: of the links written back, what fraction survive 30 days without the user deleting them — the closest thing to a precision measurement this product can get from real use.

**Most likely reason it fails.** Writing to the user's files is the highest-consequence action in the plugin and a bug here loses data. It must be explicit-command-only, never on a timer, never on save. Second risk, and the subtle one: written-back links change the link graph, which changes RA, which changes the next suggestion — a feedback loop that will look like the system getting more confident when it is only getting more self-referential. The provenance exclusion is the whole safety argument and it must be tested, not assumed.

**Needs first.** Provenance-aware graph construction (the KG report's addition) is a hard prerequisite, not a nice-to-have.
